L35: ## I. Executive Summary
L36: ## 1. Overview
L37: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L38: Under the 505(b)(1) regulatory pathway, Vertex Pharmaceuticals submitted new drug application (NDA) 219209, suzetrigine (SUZ) for the treatment of moderate to severe acute pain in adults. SUZ is a selective inhibitor of voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (\text{Na}_{\text{v}}1.8). These channels are expressed in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, and are believed to play a role in pain signaling.
L39: The regulatory history is notable for Breakthrough Therapy designation and a Priority Review determination. This NDA was not presented at the Anesthetic and Analgesic Drug Products Advisory Committee because the benefit-risk assessment did not identify unexpected significant safety or efficacy issues, and no controversial issues arose that would benefit from advisory committee discussion.
L40: Substantial evidence of effectiveness for SUZ in adults with moderate-to-severe acute pain was established using data from two adequate and well-controlled trials. These studies show that SUZ administered as 100 mg for the first dose, then 50 mg every 12 hours has significant reduction in acute pain compared to placebo.
L41: The safety database for SUZ is sufficient for the evaluation of risk. The available safety data show that SUZ has a favorable safety profile and is safe for its intended use. Serious adverse events were generally consistent with known postoperative complications of surgery and their incidence was low and balanced between treatment arms. The most common adverse reactions (greater incidence in SUZ-treated subjects compared to placebo) were pruritus, muscle spasms, increased creatine phosphokinase, and rash. SUZ is associated with potentially significant drug interactions and subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had greater SUZ and M6-SUZ (active metabolite) exposure than those with normal hepatic function. Negative effects to fertility and pregnancy were observed in nonclinical studies; however, the clinical relevance of these findings is unclear.
L42: This NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval for adults with moderate-to-severe acute pain. The signatory authority concurs that identified risks and uncertainties can be mitigated through labeling and further evaluated through postmarketing studies and routine pharmacovigilance. The review team is requiring postmarketing studies to evaluate the use of SUZ in pediatric patients and the risks to pregnancy and lactation in females.
L43: The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below (Table 2). For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this Integrated Assessment document and the Product Quality Review.
L44: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L45: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations.
L46: The Applicant conducted two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies (Studies 104 and 105) using two different acute pain models to assess the efficacy of SUZ. These studies provided sufficient evidence for the efficacy of SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults.
L47: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L48: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L49: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L50: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Acute pain is a common and well-recognized medical condition that is nearly ubiquitous.
Moderate-to-severe acute pain is a serious clinical condition that has a significant impact on quality of life and physical function.
Symptoms can last hours, days, or weeks.
There are numerous causes of acute pain. These include surgery or other invasive procedures and nonsurgical causes, such as trauma.
Inadequate pain management negatively impacts patients' health and can increase the risk of developing chronic pain.
Acute pain is a serious and common condition that is associated with many different medical and surgical conditions. Undertreated acute pain can result in significant medical and economic consequences. There is an unmet need for novel analgesics.
Current treatment options	Multiple prescription and nonprescription medications are used for the treatment of acute pain. A multimodal approach involving the simultaneous use of different analgesics is a commonly used strategy for treating acute pain.
Pharmacologic options for the treatment of acute pain include acetaminophen, NSAIDs, muscle relaxants, local anesthetics, and opioids.
Opioids are often the treatment of choice for surgical and nonsurgical causes of moderate-to-severe acute pain.
There are several significant risks associated with the use of opioids, including respiratory depression, addiction, overdose, and death.
The use of opioids can be limited by other side effects, such as constipation, nausea, and vomiting.
There are multiple medications currently used for the treatment of acute pain. These can be used as individual therapies or in conjunction with other analgesics. Acetaminophen and NSAIDs are frequently used as first-line therapy for mild-to-moderate pain, but their effectiveness can be limited for more severe pain. Opioids are frequently used for moderate-to-severe acute pain but are associated with multiple significant risks and side effects.
L51: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Non-opioid analgesics can also be associated with significant risks, such as NSAIDs and their potential effects on the cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, and renal systems.
The Applicant conducted two randomized, multicenter, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies to assess the efficacy of SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. Both studies evaluated a 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours for a 48-hour treatment period.
The primary efficacy endpoint in both studies was the time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours (SPID48) compared to placebo. The SPID48 is a cumulative assessment of pain reduction from baseline.
The key secondary efficacy endpoints in both studies were SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo.
Study 104 evaluated the efficacy of SUZ in a bunionectomy pain model. In this study, SUZ demonstrated a statistically greater reduction in pain than did placebo as measured by the SPID48 with a least-squares (LS) mean difference of 29.3.
Study 105 evaluated the efficacy of SUZ in an abdominoplasty pain model. In this study, SUZ demonstrated a statistically greater reduction in pain than did placebo as measured by the SPID48 with a LS mean difference of 48.4.
SUZ did not demonstrate greater pain reduction than did HB/APAP as measured by the SPID48 in either Study 104 or 105.
Two placebo- and active-comparator (HB/APAP), adequate and well-controlled phase 3 clinical studies were conducted using two different acute pain models representing bony and soft tissue pain. Compared to placebo, there is sufficient evidence of efficacy of SUZ 100 mg followed by 50 mg every 12 hours.
These studies did not demonstrate superior efficacy of SUZ compared to HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. Considering that this is a lower dosage regimen for HB/APAP, the use of SUZ as a sole agent may be insufficient for the treatment of more severe acute pain.
L52: ## NDA 219209
## Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	A total of 1883 unique subjects were exposed to at least 1 dose of SUZ (1266 with acute pain at the to-be-marketed dose regimen in the phase 2 and 3 studies, 169 in the acute pain studies who received a lower dose of SUZ, 393 in the phase 1 studies in healthy subjects, and 55 in the phase 1 studies in special populations). The safety of SUZ was evaluated in 874 subjects with acute postoperative pain in controlled phase 3 studies 104 and 105 and 256 subjects in uncontrolled phase 3 study 107.
There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
In the controlled acute pain studies, the incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences between the treatment arms.
In the controlled acute pain studies, the most common AEs (incidence >2%) were nausea, constipation, headache, dizziness, and pruritis.
There is no evidence of abuse potential with SUZ.
Small changes in laboratory assessments of renal function were noted, which were considered unlikely to be clinically significant.
There were negative effects on fertility and pregnancy observed in nonclinical studies. However, there are insufficient clinical data regarding the use of SUZ in pregnancy and in patients of reproductive potential.
SUZ represents a new pharmacologic class of non-opioid therapy for acute pain. The size of the safety database was adequate to characterize the safety profile of SUZ. The safety profile was favorable in subjects with moderate-to-severe acute pain and the known risks can be adequately described in product labeling. There was no evidence of abuse potential.
Uncertainties surrounding the effects of SUZ on fertility, pregnancy, and lactation will be addressed through labeling and postmarketing studies.
L54: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen; LS, least-squares; NPRS, numeric pain rating scale; NSAID, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug; SAE, serious adverse event; SPID48, time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine
L55: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L56: Moderate-to-severe acute pain is a serious and nearly ubiquitous condition that can result from a wide variety of surgical and nonsurgical causes. Inadequately managed acute pain is significantly detrimental to patients' quality of life and is a risk factor for the development of chronic pain. There are multiple pharmacologic classes available for the treatment of acute pain, including acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), and opioids. However, significant limitations exist with currently available therapies, such as the abuse potential and side effect profile of opioids and the limited effectiveness of non-opioids for severe pain. Therefore, the treatment of acute pain continues to have unmet needs.
L57: SUZ is a selective inhibitor of voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (Na_v1.8). This is predominantly expressed in peripheral sensory neurons including DRG neurons. The Applicant purported that this selectivity would lead to the avoidance of side effects that are associated with less selective sodium channel modulators and central nervous system (CNS)-active agents.
L58: The Applicant provided substantial evidence of efficacy of SUZ from two adequate and well-controlled studies using the bunionectomy and abdominoplasty acute pain models. The benefit of SUZ is based on the time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the numeric pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours (SPID48), which demonstrated statistically significant pain relief compared to placebo in both studies. The first key secondary endpoint, a comparison of SUZ versus an opioid active control (hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen [HB/APAP] 5/325 mg every 6 hours) on SPID48, was not met in either study. In the study of acute pain post-bunionectomy, subjects in the SUZ group had less pain reduction compared to those in the HB/APAP group.
L59: The safety evaluation of SUZ was adequate, and the demonstrated safety profile is acceptable for the indicated dose and population. There were no concerning major safety findings and the controlled phase 3 acute pain studies demonstrated that subjects treated with SUZ had a lower incidence of some of the adverse events (AEs) commonly associated with opioids, such as nausea, vomiting, and dizziness, than did those treated with HB/APAP. Additionally, the incidence of any AE was lower in the SUZ group than those in either the HB/APAP or placebo groups. SUZ does not appear to have a potential for abuse. SUZ is associated with potentially significant drug interactions including some hormonal contraceptives; dosage modifications are recommended for moderate CYP3A inhibitors and SUZ is contraindicated for use with strong CYP3A inhibitors. Subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had greater SUZ and M6-SUZ (active metabolite) exposure than those with normal hepatic function, and the recommended dosage is lower in patients with moderate hepatic impairment than those with normal hepatic function. Uncertainties regarding safety include use of SUZ in pediatric patients, patients with severe hepatic impairment or renal impairment of eGFR &lt; 15 mL/min, and the risks to fertility, pregnancy, and lactation. These uncertainties will be addressed in the labeling and assessed through postmarketing studies.
L60: There is a clear unmet medical need for new therapies for the treatment of pain. SUZ is the first member of a novel pharmacologic class of analgesics and has demonstrated efficacy for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in two adequate and well-controlled clinical studies. Considering the totality of the data, the benefits of SUZ clearly outweigh the risks. However, it is important to note that the performance of SUZ in clinical studies compared to a HB/APAP
L61: NDA 219209
L62: Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
L63: comparator administered at a relatively low dose may indicate limited efficacy as a sole agent for the treatment of more severe acute pain. Nonetheless, its use as part of multimodal analgesic therapy may reduce reliance on currently used analgesics and subsequently mitigate the associated risks, such as abuse potential with opioid use. In summary, SUZ and the new pharmacologic class it represents have the potential to be valuable additions to the current armamentarium of analgesics.
L64: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L65: ## 3. Introduction
L66: Vertex Pharmaceuticals Inc. has developed suzetrigine (SUZ; VX-548), an inhibitor of voltage-gated sodium channel 1.8 (Na_v1.8). There are nine Na_v isoforms expressed in humans, and SUZ is selective for Na_v1.8, which plays a role in pain signaling. SUZ is the first potential member of this pharmacologic class of non-opioid analgesics. The Applicant asserts that because Na_v1.8 is selectively expressed in peripheral pain-sensing neurons in humans, Na_v1.8 inhibitors are not expected to have the abuse liability associated with opioids or the adverse effect profile observed with central nervous system (CNS)-active agents.
L67: The proposed indication for SUZ is for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. SUZ is an orally bioavailable small molecule, and the Applicant has developed a 50-mg tablet as the to-be-marketed formulation. The proposed dosing regimen is 100 mg for the first dose (loading), followed by 50 mg every 12 hours (b) (4)
L68: Pain is a nearly ubiquitous experience that impacts multiple facets of patients' lives, including physical functioning and mental health. There is a significant economic burden associated with the management of pain. Acute pain is usually time-limited and can be associated with a variety of surgical and nonsurgical conditions. Inadequately treated acute pain can increase the risk of developing chronic pain. Moderate-to-severe acute pain is considered a serious clinical condition for which there is an urgent need for safe and effective treatments.
L69: There are multiple classes of FDA-approved medications for the management of acute pain in adults. These include acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), opioids, and local anesthetics. Muscle relaxants are indicated as an adjunct for the treatment of discomfort and muscle spasm associated with acute, painful musculoskeletal conditions. However, these medications are all associated with various adverse effects across multiple different organ systems. Opioids, in particular, have gained significant attention given their abuse liability and the growing public health concerns regarding addiction and overdose.
L70: Two phase 3 clinical studies (Studies 104 and 105) were conducted to assess the safety and establish the efficacy of SUZ in moderate-to-severe acute pain. These studies were both randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled in design. Study 104 used bunionectomy and Study 105 used abdominoplasty as the postsurgical acute pain models. The phase 3 program also included Study 107, a single-arm, uncontrolled study that evaluated SUZ in various acute surgical and nonsurgical painful conditions for up to 14 days of treatment. In the two controlled phase 3 studies, the primary endpoint was the time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the numeric pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours (SPID48) for SUZ compared to placebo.
L71: SUZ was granted Breakthrough Therapy designation after review of data from its phase 2 clinical studies suggesting favorable efficacy compared to placebo, as well as a favorable clinical safety profile and a mechanistic rationale for less abuse potential compared to opioids. This NDA was granted Priority Review designation considering the potential for SUZ to be a significant improvement in the safety of the treatment of acute pain.
L72: ## 3.1. Review Issue List
L73: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L74: ## 3.1.1.1. Differences in Efficacy Between Pain Models
L75: ## 3.1.1.2. Handling of Intercurrent Events
L76: ## 3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues

L78: ## 3.1.2.2. Duration of Use
L79: ## 3.1.2.3. Risk of Reproductive and Developmental Effects
L80: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L81: The primary evidence for the efficacy and safety of SUZ for the proposed indication of the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults was provided by two adequate and well-controlled studies conducted in the United States (Studies 104 and 105). These were both randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies. The active comparator in both studies was hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen, an opioid-containing combination medication. In addition, a single-arm phase 3 study (Study 107) provided supportive safety data. All of the phase 3 studies were conducted in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP) as described in Integrated Addendum to International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guideline *E6(R2) Integrated Addendum to ICH E6(R1): Guideline for Good Clinical Practice* (November 2016).
L82: Additional supportive safety information from the phase 2 acute pain studies (Studies 101 and 102) was reviewed. These were both randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, and active-controlled studies conducted in the United States using the same acute pain models (bunionectomy and abdominoplasty) as the controlled phase 3 studies. These studies evaluated different dose regimens of SUZ in addition to the to-be-marketed regimen. This included 60 mg for the first dose, then 30 mg q12h (Studies 101 and 102) and 20 mg for the first dose, then 10 mg q12h (Study 101).
L83: ## 3.3. Approach To Establishing Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

L98: ## 4. Patient Experience Data
L99: The Applicant submitted patient experience data to assess the efficacy of SUZ. The NPRS is a patient-reported pain intensity measure that was collected at scheduled time points throughout the controlled phase 3 studies. These measurements were used to produce the SPID48, which was the primary efficacy endpoint for these studies. The verbal rating scale (VRS) is another patient-reported pain intensity measure that was used as an inclusion criterion to ensure that all subjects had moderate or severe pain at baseline in the controlled and uncontrolled phase 3 studies. The Patient Global Assessment (PGA) of study drug is a patient-reported measure of overall pain control with the study drug that was also used in these studies. Safety monitoring in Study 107 included the Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale, which is an assessment of suicidal thoughts and behaviors.

L102: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L103: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L104: SUZ selectively inhibits voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (\text{Na}_v1.8), which is expressed in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, among other known voltage-gated sodium channel subtypes (\text{Na}_v 1.1 through 1.9). The inhibitory activity of SUZ for \text{Na}_v1.8 was evaluated in DRG neurons of human, monkey, rat, mouse, rabbit, and dog in vitro. The half-maximal inhibitory concentration (\text{IC}_{50}) values of SUZ against human and monkey \text{Na}_v1.8 were 0.68nM and 0.75nM, respectively. SUZ was at least 80-fold less potent in rodent \text{Na}_v1.8 (\text{IC}_{50}: 56nM) than in human \text{Na}_v1.8. Therefore, in vivo primary pharmacology studies were not conducted in rodents. A major human metabolite, M6-SUZ (also referred to as VRT-1875420, M6), was also demonstrated to be a potent human \text{Na}_v1.8 blocker with an \text{IC}_{50} of 2.5nM in in vitro assays using human DRG neurons.
L105: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L106: SUZ is a new molecular entity with a mechanism of action of blocking the voltage-gated sodium channel Na_v1.8 currents. This represents a new class of drugs, and there is currently no established pharmacologic class (EPC) for this class. The Applicant proposed (b)(4) as the preferred EPC; however, the review team did not consider this acceptable. The review team recommends “sodium channel blocker” as an appropriate EPC for this class. Refer to Section 23 for a discussion on the rationale for selecting “sodium channel blocker” as the appropriate EPC for SUZ.
L107: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L108: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	SUZ is a sodium channel blocker.
Mechanism of action	SUZ is a selective blocker of the Na_v1.8 voltage-gated sodium channel compared to other known voltage-gated sodium channels (i.e., Na_v1.1 through 1.9). Na_v1.8 is predominantly expressed in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, where its role is to transmit pain signals (action potentials). By selectively inhibiting Na_v1.8 channels, SUZ inhibits transmission of pain signals to the spinal cord and brain. Based on an in vitro electrophysiology assay in human DRG neurons, M6-SUZ is a less potent blocker of Na_v1.8 than SUZ by 3.7fold.
Active moieties	SUZ and M6-SUZ are active moieties.
QT prolongation	At 2 times the exposure of the maximum recommended dose, clinically significant QTc prolongation was not observed
General Information
Bioanalysis	A validated LC-MS/MS method was used to measure plasma concentrations SUZ and M6-SUZ. For all studies, the cumulative accuracy (% bias) was < \pm 15\% and precision (%CV) was < 15\% for QC samples and calibration curves used the LC-MS/MS bioanalytical assay. The results of incurred samples reanalysis were within acceptable limits.
Healthy subjects versus patients	Clinically relevant differences in PK are not expected between healthy subjects versus patients.

L111: Characteristic	Drug Information
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	The effective dosing regimen determined based on phase 2 dose ranging studies was 100 mg loading dose/50 mg q12h and was studied in pivotal phase 3 trials
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	The maximum tolerated dose of SUZ has not been determined. In healthy adult subjects, the highest single dose evaluated was 395 mg (Study 005 Part A) and the highest multiple dose and duration was 178 mg qd x 14 days (Study 005 Part B) and 200 mg x 7 days (QT study- Study 009).
Dose proportionality	
The single doses of SUZ were evaluated in the range of 7.5 mg to 395 mg. The AUC0-inf of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased approximately dose proportionally up to and including 171 mg and increased less than dose proportionally at higher doses, while Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased less than dose proportionally across all doses.
The PK after multiple doses of SUZ was evaluated up to 178 mg qd for 14 days. After multiple doses, both AUC0-24h and Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ on Day 14 increased approximately dose proportionally through doses up to 65 mg and slightly less than dose proportionally at higher doses.

Accumulation	The accumulation ratio based on simulations of a dose of 50 mg q12h for SUZ and M6-SUZ were 3.37 and 4.49, respectively.
Time to achieve steady-state	The concentrations near steady state (90% of steady state) are reached by Day 3 for SUZ and Day 5 for M6-SUZ and the steady state is reached by Day 16 for SUZ and Day 21 for M6-SUZ.
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	The 50 mg to-be-marketed tablet formulation was evaluated in the phase 3 studies. The bridge between phase 2 formulation (10-mg tablets) and to-be-marketed formulation (50-mg tablets) was via an in vivo relative BA study (Study 011).
Bioavailability	Absolute bioavailability of SUZ has not been determined.
Tmax	SUZ: 3 hours (range: 1.5 to 5 hours); M6-SUZ: 10 hours (range: 4 to 48 hours)
Food effect (fed/fasted)
Geometric least square mean and 90% CI	
Administration of SUZ 100 mg with a high-fat meal (800 to 1000 calories, 50% derived from fat), a moderate-fat meal (600 calories, 30% derived from fat) and a low-fat meal (up to 500 calories with no more than 25% derived from fat) results in decreased initial concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ in comparison to a fasted state. The median Tmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ in the fasted state is 3 hours and 8 to 10 hours, respectively. When administered in the fed state (a high-fat meal or a moderate-fat meal), the median Tmax of SUZ was delayed to 5 hours and the median Tmax of M6-SUZ was delayed to 24 hours. The Cmax and AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not affected by any of the meal conditions including a high-fat meal consumed 1 hour after the administration of SUZ. Administration of the second dose of 50 mg with or without regard to meals is predicted to not affect the systemic exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Based on the observed decreased initial concentrations and delayed Tmax with both moderate- and high-fat meals in comparison to a fasted condition, the labeling recommendation for food effect is, "Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without food."
L112: ## NDA 219209
## Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
L113: Characteristic	Drug Information
Distribution
Volume of distribution	Apparent volume of distribution based on the terminal phase (Vz/F) of SUZ is 495 L.
Plasma protein binding	Plasma protein binding was 99% for SUZ and 96% for M6-SUZ. Both albumin and alpha-1 acid glycoprotein contribute to SUZ and M6-SUZ binding in human plasma. In vitro blood-to-plasma concentration ratio was approximately 0.6 for both SUZ and M6-SUZ in humans.
Drug as substrate of transporters	SUZ is not a substrate for transporters (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3). M6-SUZ is a substrate for P-gp, but not other transporters.
Elimination
Mass balance results	Following [^{14}\text{C}]SUZ oral administration, ~94% of the administered dose was recovered, out of which ~50% is recovered in feces [primarily as metabolites, unchanged parent (9.1%) and unchanged M6-SUZ (<0.9%)] and ~44% in urine (as metabolites)
Clearance	The mean plasma apparent clearance is 13.9 L/h
Half-life	Effective half-lives of SUZ and M6-SUZ are 24 hours and 33 hours, respectively.
Metabolic pathway(s)	SUZ and M6-SUZ are primarily metabolized by CYP3A, with a minor contribution from CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2C8.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	SUZ was mainly cleared by oxidative metabolism.
After a single oral dose of [^{14}\text{C}]SUZ, 44.0% and 49.9% of the dose is excreted in urine and feces, respectively.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Body weight (44-126kg) did not have a clinically relevant impact on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Age	Age (18-75 years) does not have a clinically relevant impact on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Renal impairment	The PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not altered in patients with renal impairment of eGFR 15 to <60 mL/min (estimated by CKD-EPI equation with adjustment for the body surface area). The effect of renal impairment (eGFR <15 mL/min) and kidney failure not receiving dialysis on SUZ and M6-SUZ pharmacokinetics is unknown.
Hepatic impairment	In patients with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B), the C_{\text{max}} and \text{AUC}_{0-12} SUZ were increased by 31% and 50%, respectively, and hence reduced dosing regimen of SUZ is needed in patients with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B). SUZ was not studied in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C) and should not be used in that population.
L114: Characteristic	Drug Information
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	
SUZ inhibits CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19, but is not expected to result in clinically significant drug interactions. SUZ does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2D6, and CYP3A enzymes, and M6-SUZ does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A enzymes.
SUZ is CYP3A inducer in vitro. SUZ decreased the AUC of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) by 48% and C_{max} by 37% (Study 010). Since SUZ decreases the exposure of CYP3A substrates and may compromise the efficacy, the review team included labeling recommendations for certain CYP3A substrates (refer to section 8.2.2). Compared to CYP3A, SUZ has lesser potential to induce CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. SUZ does not induce CYP1A2.
SUZ has no significant effect on the PK of levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol and therefore, SUZ can be used with oral contraceptives containing levonorgestrel, norethindrone and ethinyl estradiol.

Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	
SUZ is an inhibitor of P-gp in vitro. However, a clinical DDI study (010) showed that SUZ did not affect the PK of digoxin (P-gp substrate). No dosage adjustment of SUZ is needed when administered with P-gp substrates. SUZ and M6-SUZ inhibit OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3 but is not expected to result in clinically significant drug interactions. SUZ and M6-SUZ does not inhibit BCRP OAT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2/K transporters.

Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Substrate)
Inhibitors and inducers of metabolism	
SUZ and M6-SUZ are CYP3A substrates.
Itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor increased the AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 4.79-fold and 4.42-fold, respectively (Study 008). Therefore, concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors is contraindicated. When SUZ is concomitantly used with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, a dosage reduction of SUZ is recommended.
Rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer, decreased AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 93% and 85%, respectively, and decreased the C_{max} of SUZ by 80% and increased that of M6-SUZ by 30% (Study 013). Avoid concomitant use of SUZ with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers.

Inhibitors/inducers of transporter systems	
SUZ and M6-SUZ are not substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3. SUZ is not a P-gp substrate, but M6-SUZ is a P-gp substrate.

L116: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L117: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L118: Dosing regimen selection for the acute pain indication of SUZ is based on two phase 2 randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled dose-ranging studies in bunionectomy (Study 101) and abdominoplasty (Study 102). Study 101 evaluated the efficacy of three dosing regimens: 1) 100-mg loading dose (LD)/50 mg q12h for 36 hours, 2) 60-mg LD/30 mg q12h for 36 hours, and 3) 20-mg LD/10 mg q12h for 36 hours, while Study 102 evaluated two dosing regimens: 1) 100-mg LD/50 mg q12h for 36 hours, and 2) 60-mg LD/30 mg q12h for 36 hours. Regimens were evaluated against an active comparator, hydrocodone/acetaminophen 5/325 mg q6h for 36 hours, and against placebo. In each trial, pain intensity was measured using a patient-reported 11-point NPRS, ranging from 0 to 10, where 0 corresponded to no pain and 10 corresponded to the worst pain imaginable. The efficacy of SUZ was evaluated by SPID48 compared to placebo and HB/APAP. The primary efficacy criteria of SPID48 for SUZ compared to placebo was met at the highest dosing regimen, 100 mg/50 mg dosed at q12h for 36 hours. The magnitude of effect in the SUZ group with the highest dosing regimen was greater than those of the lower-dose groups, and no SUZ dose group other than the highest dose showed a statistically significant difference from placebo. Therefore, the highest dosing regimen of 100-mg LD/50 mg q12h was utilized in the pivotal phase 3 trials in the same pain models of bunionectomy and abdominoplasty. In both phase 3 trials, treatment with SUZ demonstrated statistically significant superior reduction in pain compared to placebo.
L119: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L120: ## 6.2.1. Study VX22-548-104 (Study 104)
L121: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, Study 104
L122: Study 104 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after a bunionectomy. The primary efficacy endpoint was the time-weighted SPID48 compared to placebo. The key secondary efficacy endpoints included SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo. Additional design information is available in Section 15.1.
L123: As described in the draft guidance for industry *Development of Non-Opioid Analgesics for Acute Pain* (February 2022), the randomized, double-blind, repeat-dose design of this superiority study with a treatment duration of 48 hours is acceptable to support a finding of efficacy for a non-opioid analgesic. The primary outcome measure of NPRS is a patient-reported measure of pain
L124: intensity that is well defined and reliable. The primary endpoint of sum of the pain intensity difference (SPID) over the prespecified treatment period is an acceptable analysis for an analgesic intended for acute pain. The time to onset of SUZ as an acute analgesic was evaluated in an acceptable manner using the times to \ge 2-point and \ge 1-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo, as discussed at the July 14, 2022, end-of-phase 2 meeting.
L125: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study 104
L126: Males and females 18 to 80 years of age, inclusive, scheduled to undergo a primary unilateral bunionectomy with distal first metatarsal osteotomy (i.e., Austin procedure) and internal fixation under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block) were eligible for this study. After surgery, subjects must have reported pain that was moderate or severe on the VRS and \ge 4 on the NPRS within 9 hours after removal of the popliteal sciatic block on Day 1.
L127: Subjects who had a prior history of bunionectomy or other foot surgery on the index foot, or bunionectomy on the opposite foot as part of this study, were excluded. Subjects who had chronic use of opioids or NSAIDs with dose escalation within 30 days before admission or were unwilling or unable to stop analgesics at least 5 half-lives or 2 days (whichever was longer) before admission were also excluded.
L128: See Section 15.1 for other inclusion and exclusion criteria.
L129: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 104
L130: Study 104 assumed a standardized effect size of 0.4 for SUZ compared to placebo and 0.25 for SUZ compared to HB/APAP; 338 evaluable subjects per group for SUZ and HB/APAP and 169 evaluable subjects for placebo would provide more than 90% power for the primary endpoint of SUZ versus placebo on SPID48 and 90% power for the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48. These calculations were based on two-sample t-tests at a two-sided significance level of 0.05. To account for a 15% dropout rate, a total of approximately 1,000 subjects were planned to be enrolled in Study 104.
L131: The treatment effects assumed in the sample size planning for Study 104 appeared to be conservative as compared to the treatment effects estimated from the previously completed phase 2 study for bunionectomy surgical model; the estimated standardized effect size was 0.85 for SUZ versus placebo on SPID48 and 0.69 for SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48.
L132: ## Analysis Sets
L133: The all subjects set included all subjects who had been randomized or had received at least one dose of the study drug. This analysis set was used for subject listings and the disposition summary table.
L134: The intent-to-treat (ITT) set included all randomized subjects. Subjects in the ITT set were analyzed according to their randomized treatment group.
L135: The full analysis set (FAS) included all randomized subjects who had received at least one dose of study drug. Subjects in the FAS were analyzed according to their randomized treatment group. The FAS was used for summaries of subject demographics and baseline characteristics, and all efficacy analyses.
L136: The safety set included all subjects who received at least one dose of study drug. Subjects in the safety set were analyzed according to the treatment they received. The safety set was used for all safety analyses.
L137: ## **Multiplicity Adjustment**
L138: A hierarchical testing procedure was used to control the overall type I error rate at a two-sided alpha level of 0.05 accounting for all the tests planned for the primary and key secondary endpoints. The testing order was as follows:
L139: - Primary endpoint: SUZ versus placebo on SPID48
- First key secondary endpoint: SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48
- Second key secondary endpoint: SUZ versus placebo on time to a \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline
L140: ## **Analysis of Primary Endpoint: SUZ Versus Placebo on SPID48**
L141: The Applicant defined pain intensity difference as the difference of NPRS scores between the baseline and a given time point. SPID was calculated as \sum(t_{i-1} - t_i) \times PID_{t_i}, where t_i was the scheduled time since first dose (in hours) for time point i, and PID_{t_i} was the NPRS at time point i minus NPRS at baseline. The primary endpoint was SPID48.
L142: The primary analysis of SPID48 was based on the analysis of covariance model, which included SPID48 as the dependent variable, treatment arm (SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo) as a fixed effect, and site and baseline NPRS as covariates. The treatment effect was estimated by the least-squares (LS) mean difference between SUZ and placebo at a two-sided alpha level of 0.05. The LS mean and its 95% confidence interval (CI) were provided for both the group comparison and each treatment group.
L143: The Applicant used the following imputation methods under four different conditions:
L144: - Rescue medication use: A fixed imputation window of 6 hours was prespecified for ibuprofen. The prerescue NPRS score was collected immediately before the administration of rescue medication. The previous NPRS score was used if the prerescue score was missing, provided that the previous NPRS score did not include any observed score from the previous rescue period. In case of multiple administrations of rescue medication that led to overlapping rescue periods, the prerescue NPRS score (b) (4) replaced all NPRS scores (b) (4) through the end of the last overlapping rescue period.
- Intercurrent event (ICE) of treatment discontinuation: The single imputation method of baseline observation carried forward (BOCF) was used for the treatment discontinuation due to an adverse event (AE). The single imputation method of last observation carried forward (LOCF) was used for treatment discontinuation due to all other reasons than AE.
- Subject who completed the treatment but with missing data from a certain time point to hour 48: A single imputation method of LOCF was used to impute missing data.
- Intermittently missing NPRS scores: Linear interpolation was used to impute intermittent missing data.
L145: ## **Sensitivity Analysis of the Primary Endpoint**
L146: The Applicant planned and conducted two sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint. In the first sensitivity analysis, the last NPRS score prior to discontinuation was used to impute the missing NPRS scores following treatment discontinuation due to an AE. In the second sensitivity analysis, the same primary analysis was performed using the ITT Set. Randomized subjects who did not receive any study drug were assigned a value of 0 for the primary endpoint of SPID48.
L147: ## **Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints**
L148: For the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48, the Applicant used the same imputation methods as described for the primary endpoint.
L149: The second key secondary endpoint, time to \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo, was defined as the time elapsed from the first dose of the study drug until the first time the subject had at least a 2-point reduction in the NPRS from baseline. A Kaplan-Meier method was used to estimate the median time and the survival curve for each treatment group, and a log-rank test was used to compare the survival curves between the treatment groups. The median time with 95% CI from the Kaplan-Meier analysis and the p-value for the group comparison from the log-rank test were reported.
L150: ## **Subgroup Analyses**
L151: Subgroup analyses were performed for sex, age, and race.
L152: ## **6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Study 104**

The three treatment groups were generally balanced on demographics and baseline characteristics. Overall, the study population was predominantly female (85%), White (71%) or Black or African American (24%), and aged 18 to 65 (91%).

L169: ## **Protocol Deviations**
L170: An important protocol deviation (IPD) was defined as any protocol deviation that may have significantly affected the completeness, accuracy, and/or reliability of the study data or one that may have significantly affected a subject's rights, safety, or well-being.
L171: A total of 54 (5.0%) subjects (17 subjects in SUZ, 22 subjects in HB/APAP, and 15 subjects in placebo) had one or more IPDs. Among all the IPDs, the most common protocol deviations were related to the study conduct/procedures (18 subjects, 1.7%). The next most common deviations were related to the randomization criteria (15 subjects, 1.4%), followed by deviations related to the eligibility criteria (14 subjects, 1.3%).
L172: In general, the number of major protocol deviations that could potentially impact the efficacy analysis was small. The frequency of minor protocol deviations was similar across the three treatment groups. The minor protocol deviations that occurred were not anticipated to have a measurable effect on the efficacy analysis.
L173: ## **Rescue Medication Use**
L174: Rescue medication was ibuprofen 400 mg administered orally q6h as needed. Table 9 below summarizes the rescue medication use from 0 to 48 hours by treatment group. The mean total usage was 1,009.3 mg in the placebo group and 862.9 mg in the SUZ group. Table 10 below stratifies the rescue medication use by treatment group over time. The SUZ group had a similar proportion of subjects using rescue medication (69.5%) compared to the placebo group (69.4%) in the first 6 hours. The proportion of subjects using rescue medication in the SUZ and placebo
L175: groups remained similar in each block of time through 48 hours. In contrast, the proportion of subjects using rescue medication in the HB/APAP group in the first 6 hours was considerably less (55.7%) than the SUZ and placebo groups.

L194: ## **Efficacy Results - Secondary Endpoints**
L195: The analysis results of the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48 are shown in Table 12. The Applicant conducted a two-sided test for comparing SUZ to HB/APAP on SPID48, which resulted in a p-value of 0.0016, indicating that there was a statistically significant difference between SUZ and HB/APAP in terms of SPID48. The LS mean difference calculated as SPID48 in SUZ minus SPID48 in HB/APAP, where the HB/APAP group was treated as the reference group, was estimated to be -20.2 (95% CI: -32.7, -7.7), further indicating that subjects in the SUZ group had less pain reduction than did those in the HB/APAP group. Above mentioned results indicate that the Applicant failed to demonstrate superiority of SUZ over HB/APAP on SPID48.

L204: ## 6.2.2. Study VX22-548-105 (Study 105)
L205: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Study 105
L206: Study 105 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after an abdominoplasty. The primary efficacy endpoint was the SPID48 compared to placebo. The key secondary efficacy endpoints included SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo. The overall study design was consistent with that of Study 104. The major differences were study procedures related to abdominoplasty, the anesthetic used for surgery, and procedure-specific subject selection criteria. As with Study 104, the design of this superiority study with a treatment duration of 48 hours was acceptable to support a finding of efficacy for a non-opioid analgesic. Additional design information is available in Section 14.7.
L207: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study 105
L208: Males and females 18 to 80 years of age, inclusive, scheduled to undergo a standard (“full”) abdominoplasty procedure were eligible for this study. Additional inclusion criteria regarding the procedure consisted of the following: the procedure must have included a horizontally oriented incision approximately extending to each anterior superior iliac spine, umbilical dissection and relocation, and plication of the fascia of the rectus muscle above and/or below the umbilical stalk; may have included drain placement and skin closure at the discretion of the surgeon; and must NOT have included a vertically oriented supraumbilical incision or collateral procedures (e.g., liposuction). After surgery, subjects must have reported pain that was moderate or severe on the VRS and \ge 4 on the NPRS within 4 hours after surgery completion on Day 1. In addition,
L209: the abdominoplasty procedure duration must have been \le 3 hours. Lastly, all analgesic guidelines must have been followed during and after abdominoplasty including the use of intravenous general anesthesia along with infiltration of 1% lidocaine without epinephrine at the surgical site after completing the rectus plication.
L210: Subjects who had a prior history of abdominoplasty were excluded. Subjects who had a history of intra-abdominal and/or pelvic surgery that resulted in any complications or, in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude participation in the study were also excluded. The eligibility criteria were otherwise generally consistent with those of Study 104.
L211: See Section 15.2 for other inclusion and exclusion criteria.
L212: ## 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 105
L213: The sample size planning for Study 105 was identical to that for Study 104. The treatment effects assumed in the sample size planning for Study 105 were also conservative as compared to the treatment effects estimated from the previously completed phase 2 study in the abdominoplasty surgical model; the estimated standardized effect size was 0.88 for SUZ versus placebo on SPID48, and 0.78 for SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48.
L214: Since the statistical approaches used in Study 105 were identical to those used in Study 104, refer to Section 6.2.1.3 for details of the statistical analysis plan (SAP).

L223: ## NDA 219209
## Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
L224: In Study 105, a total of 1,118 subjects were randomized. All randomized subjects received at least one dose of study drug, resulting in a total of 1,118 subjects in the FAS. The most common reason for discontinuation of treatment was lack of efficacy (13.3%). A total of 12 (1.1%) subjects discontinued the treatment due to AEs. There was one subject who was randomized to the placebo group yet received one dose of SUZ due to an error in dispensing study drug kits at a single site. This subject was counted in the placebo group for the FAS and in the SUZ group for the safety set. Table 14 shows the subject disposition for Study 105.

L232: ## **Protocol Deviations**
L233: A total of 49 (4.4%) subjects (19 subjects in SUZ, 17 subjects in HB/APAP, and 13 subjects in placebo) had one or more IPDs. Among those IPDs, the most common protocol deviations were related to the eligibility criteria (26 subjects, 2.3%). The next most common deviations were related to the informed consent (10 subjects, 0.9%), followed by deviations related to the randomization criteria (9 subjects, 0.8%).
L234: In general, the number of major protocol deviations that could potentially impact the efficacy analysis was small. The frequency of minor protocol deviations was similar across the three treatment groups. The minor protocol deviations that occurred were not anticipated to have a measurable effect on the efficacy analysis.

The proportion of subjects using rescue medication in the HB/APAP group in the first 6 hours was 72.1% which is more than the SUZ group (64.5%) but less than the placebo group (82.4%).

L267: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L268: ## 6.3.1. Potential Differences in Efficacy Between Pain Models
L269: ## Issue
L270: Is there a major difference in the efficacy of SUZ between the bony and soft tissue pain models evaluated in the phase 3 acute pain studies?
L271: ## Background
L272: The results from two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies were used to demonstrate the efficacy of SUZ in treating moderate to severe acute pain. Two established acute pain models were utilized: bunionectomy for bony tissue pain (Study 104) and abdominoplasty for soft tissue pain (Study 105). Though each study was convincing on its own to establish effectiveness, differences in the time course of pain relief were observed suggesting that the effect of SUZ on acute pain could be pain model dependent. Specifically, in the abdominoplasty study the SUZ group demonstrated a greater mean pain intensity difference (PID) compared to placebo with sustained separation between the groups at all evaluated time points (i.e., 0 to 48 hours). For the bunionectomy study, the SUZ group also demonstrated a greater mean PID compared to placebo; however, the separation between pain curves was only sustained from 8 through 48 hours. To evaluate this difference in the time course of pain relief
L273: and the potential cause(s), perioperative pain management, baseline characteristics, and rescue medication use were assessed between Study 104 and Study 105.
L274: ## Perioperative Pain Management
L275: In Study 104, the bunionectomy was performed under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block) with intravenous sedation. Postoperatively, a continuous popliteal sciatic block infusion (0.2% ropivacaine) was initiated and remained in place until approximately 3 a.m., but no later than 5 a.m., on post-operative Day 1. Subjects were eligible for the study if they met the VRS and NPRS criteria within 9 hours after removal of the popliteal sciatic infusion. Ropivacaine boluses and/or infusion rate changes were permitted to be used as needed prior to removal of the popliteal block for subjects with an NPRS score of \ge 4; however, boluses were not to be used at the time of block removal.
L276: In Study 105, the abdominoplasty was performed under intravenous general anesthesia with fentanyl citrate and propofol, along with infiltration of 1% lidocaine at the surgical site, after completing the rectus plication. The rectus plication is typically performed toward the end of the abdominoplasty and involves suturing separated abdominal muscles together to restore abdominal strength. Subjects were eligible for the study if they met the VRS and NPRS criteria within 4 hours after surgery completion.
L277: ## Baseline Characteristics
L278: Baseline characteristics were compared between Study 104 and 105. Refer to the Tables of Baseline Demographics for Studies 104 and 105 in Section 6.2. While the baseline characteristics were similar between the SUZ and placebo groups within each study, there were differences in baseline characteristics between Study 104 and 105. Overall, the SUZ group in Study 104 (bunionectomy) as compared to Study 105 (abdominoplasty) enrolled a decreased percentage of women (85.9 vs. 97.8), subjects of increased age (median: 49 vs. 41 years), and lower BMI (median 27.77 vs. 29 kg/m2). As noted in the excerpted tables below, though the median NPRS scores were similar between Study 104 and 105, the mean NPRS was lower in Study 104 (6.7 vs. 7.3 in Study 105) and a lower percentage of subjects reported severe pain on the VRS in Study 104 (31.7 vs. 40.5 in Study 105).

L290: ## Rescue Medication Use
L291: Study 104 and 105 used ibuprofen 400 mg orally q6h as needed for rescue medication. Table 22 shows that the percentage of subjects using rescue medication in Study 105 (abdominoplasty) was higher in the placebo group compared to the SUZ group for the 0- to 6-hour time block (82.4% vs. 64.5%) as well as at 48 hours.

L301: ## Assessment
L302: The study populations enrolled in Study 104 and Study 105 had some differences in baseline demographics and clinical characteristics, including a difference in mean NPRS between Study 104 and Study 105 (6.7 vs. 7.3). However, it is unlikely that these accounted for the differences in the time course of pain relief between the two studies (i.e., the lack of separation of the pain curves from 0 to 8 hours in Study 104 as compared to Study 105). Additionally, Studies 104 and
L303: 105 were identical with respect to study drug administration, endpoints assessed, and statistical analysis.
L304: Moreover, the rescue medication use in Study 104 was evaluated to determine if significant differences in usage between the SUZ and placebo groups accounted for the lack of separation of the pain curves in that study. Table 21 showed that the percentage of subjects using rescue medication use was nearly identical between the SUZ and placebo groups from 0 to 6 hours and remained similar for the duration of the treatment period (i.e., through 48 hours). Therefore, rescue medication use does not appear to account for the lack of separation of pain curves in Study 104.
L305: Lastly, there were significant differences in perioperative pain management between Study 104 (bunionectomy) and Study 105 (abdominoplasty). As noted above, Study 104 was performed under regional anesthesia with ropivacaine, both as the nerve block and then as a postoperative nerve block infusion. Notably, ropivacaine is a non-selective sodium channel blocker which targets voltage-gated sodium channels, including Nav1.8. In contrast, Study 105 used intravenous general anesthesia, which does not act on sodium channels, during the procedure. However, lidocaine, a non-selective sodium channel blocker, was administered for localized anesthesia toward the end of the abdominoplasty procedure while performing the rectus plication. Therefore, the duration of action of ropivacaine and lidocaine, and the overlap of the duration of action of each drug with the first administration of SUZ was critical to understanding the differences in pain curves between these two surgical pain models.
L306: A nerve block with ropivacaine, as in Study 104, typically provides analgesia for at least 4-8 hours, and catheter placement extends the duration of analgesia. By contrast, analgesia provided by local infiltration with lidocaine, as performed in Study 105, typically lasts last for 1-2 hours. Given this duration of action and the overlap with the qualifying time period for first administration of SUZ, it is plausible that for Study 104, ropivacaine accounted for lack of separation of the pain curves between SUZ and placebo. Patients became eligible for the first dose of SUZ as soon as the ropivacaine infusion was discontinued and within the subsequent 9 hours. Therefore, there is significant overlap between the potential duration of action of ropivacaine and the first dose of SUZ. In comparison, for Study 105, the local infiltration of lidocaine is administered in advance of closure of the surgical wound, and therefore, despite an approximately 2-hour duration of action, the window of overlap with the qualifying SUZ administration period is much shorter. Therefore, the differences in perioperative pain management between the two studies, including the mechanism of action and the duration of action of those drugs provides a plausible explanation that may account for the difference in time course and degree of pain relief in Study 104.
L307: ## Conclusion
L308: Study 104 and Study 105 are adequate and well-controlled studies, each convincing on its own to establish effectiveness. The difference in time course of pain relief between the two studies may reflect the differences in perioperative pain management between the bunionectomy and abdominoplasty pain models.
L309: ## 6.3.2. Handling of Intercurrent Events
L310: ## Issue
L311: The Agency identified the following potential issues regarding the handling of intercurrent events in both Studies 104 and 105:
L312: Imputation methods for handling intercurrent events of treatment discontinuation:
L313: The single imputation methods (i.e., LOCF and BOCF) were used for imputing data following treatment discontinuation. Since single imputation methods may fail to reflect the uncertainty in data, the Agency recommended adding multiple imputation (MI) to the proposed handling strategies.
L314: Per Agency request as outlined above, the Applicant imputed data following treatment discontinuation using the MI approaches. When imputing data from a normal distribution in the MI process, the Applicant
L315: The Agency recommended using a truncated normal distribution with limits of 0 and 10 instead.
L316: Regarding the Applicant's proposed for treatment discontinuation due to LOE [lack of efficacy], the Agency recommended using the above-mentioned MI of BOCF method because treatment discontinuation due to lack of efficacy is generally viewed as a treatment failure.
L317: Imputation strategy for rescue medication:
L318: In case of multiple administrations of rescue medication leading to overlapping rescue periods,
L319: the Agency recommended carrying forward each of the pre-rescue score until the end of that given rescue period, or the time of the next rescue use, whichever comes first.
L320: ## Past Communications
L321: *In the investigational new drug application (IND) review stage, the first issue regarding the handling strategy for ICE of treatment discontinuation was communicated between the Agency and the Applicant.*
L322: In the end-of-phase 2 (EOP2) meeting package, the Applicant proposed to impute missing NPRS scores following treatment discontinuation using the last observed NPRS score prior to discontinuation.
L323: The Agency advised in the August 12, 2022, EOP2 meeting communication that:
L324: Vertex proposed LOCF for subjects that discontinued the study or completed the treatment but with missing data from certain time point to 48 hours, which is not appropriate. For example, discontinuation due to lack of efficacy may suggest the assessments after the discontinuation will get worse and may be different from the observed assessments. An appropriate imputation scheme would be to assume these subjects do not contribute any treatment benefit in the primary analysis, such as multiple imputations of the subject's baseline score.
L325: The first versions of the SAPs for Studies 104 and 105 were submitted on April 14, 2023. In the SAPs, the Applicant proposed to impute for subjects who discontinued the treatment due to lack of efficacy (LOE) or an AE using the last NPRS score prior to discontinuation.
L326: The Agency provided comments regarding the imputation method to be used for ICEs of treatment discontinuation in an advice letter dated September 5, 2023. The Applicant responded to the comments and provided the following justifications to support their proposal: “Imputation using the last score does not attribute favorable pain scores to subjects who discontinue treatment due to lack of efficacy in acute pain studies.”
L327: The evidence to support this conclusion was their phase 2 study results where the pooled mean baseline NPRS scores was lower than the pooled mean last NPRS scores for Studies 101 and 102, which correspond to the two phase 3 Studies 104 and 105. The Applicant further stated, “However, imputation using the baseline score assumes subjects will have some improvement in pain if they hadn’t discontinued treatment due to lack of efficacy, which is not appropriate.”
L328: The Agency provided the following follow-up comment in response to the Applicant’s justification:
L329: The Division acknowledges your proposal to lock the database for the VX22-548-105 Study. We have provided our comments in September 5, 2023 advice letter in response to your April 14, 2023 submission. We further acknowledge your response submitted on September 29 and October 6, 2023. Acceptability and adequacy of the data analysis will be a review issue when the full NDA is submitted.
L330: In the final versions of the SAPs submitted on January 5, 2024, the Applicant changed the handling strategy for ICEs of treatment discontinuation due to an AE to BOCF as the Agency recommended, while the handling strategy for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE remained as LOCF.
L331: ## Assessment
L332: To address the issues outlined above, results from additional sensitivity analyses performed by the Agency and Applicant are summarized below.
L333: ## Sensitivity Analysis 1
L334: Table 23 below compares the results from the primary analysis, in which a single imputation method of LOCF was used to impute data following treatment discontinuation due to lack of efficacy, and the results from three sensitivity analyses wherein:
L335: - A single imputation method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE instead (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-1)
- An MI method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE, in which the imputed scores outside the [0, 10] range were forced to be 0 and 10, respectively (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-2)
- An MI method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE, in which the imputed scores were generated from a truncated normal distribution with limits of 0 and 10 (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-3).
L336: Sensitivity Analyses 1-2 and 1-3, in which an MI method of BOCF was used to impute data following treatment discontinuation due to LOE, yielded the least treatment differences as compared to the primary analysis in which a single imputation of LOCF was used.

L343: c The following imputation methods were used: (1) NPRS during the rescue period (within 6 hours after rescue medication) were replaced by the prerescue NPRS; (2) Intermittently missing NPRS were imputed using linear interpolation; (3) Missing NPRS following treatment discontinuation due to lack of efficacy or AE were imputed using multiple imputations of the subject's baseline NPRS score while the NPRS following treatment discontinuation due to other reasons were imputed using multiple imputations of the subject's last NPRS score prior to discontinuation; (4) Missing NPRS for subjects who completed the treatment but with missing data from a certain time point to 48 hours were imputed using multiple imputations of the subject's last NPRS score.
L344: d The same imputation methods as for Sensitivity Analysis 1-2 (footnote "c") were used except for (3) and (4), MI was performed with a truncated normal distribution.

L346: ## Sensitivity Analysis 2
L347: A large portion of the population (more than 50%) had two or more doses of rescue medication across all three treatment groups in both studies. However, the percentage of multiple administrations of the rescue medications that led to overlapping imputation periods was small (ranging from 0.6% to 0.8% among treatment groups in the two studies). Table 24 below compares the results from the primary analysis where first pre-rescue scores were carried forward until the end of the last overlapping rescue period and the results from a sensitivity analysis (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 2) where pre-rescue scores were carried forward until the end of the rescue period or the time of the next rescue use, whichever came first. This sensitivity analysis yielded similar results to the primary analysis.

L354: ## Conclusion
L355: Despite the slight treatment differences between the SUZ and placebo groups obtained from the sensitivity analyses as compared to that obtained from the primary analysis, the conclusion that SUZ is superior to placebo in SPID48 remains unchanged.
L356: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L357: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L358: Appropriate nonclinical studies were submitted to support the safety of SUZ (also referred to as VX-548 and VRT-1737548); a major human metabolite, VRT-1875420; and impurities in the drug substance and drug product. The following studies were submitted: primary and secondary pharmacology, safety pharmacology in the monkey in addition to in vitro human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) assays; absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (ADME) and pharmacokinetic (PK) studies; repeat-dose toxicology studies in the rat and monkey for durations of 28 days and 13 weeks; a full battery of reproductive toxicology studies in the rat and rabbit; and a full battery of genetic toxicology studies (Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and in vivo rat micronucleus assays).
L359: All levels of excipients in the 50-mg tablet drug product formulation are within the levels of previously approved FDA drug products by the proposed oral route at the maximum daily dose of 150 mg; therefore, there is no safety concern from the excipients.
L360: General toxicity studies and reproductive toxicity studies performed in rats suggested SUZ may pose risks with respect to adverse effects on female reproductive tissues, fertility, pregnancy, and development. Nonclinical findings that may be relevant to human risks or are causes of safety concerns are highlighted below. However, overall, the nonclinical data support the safety of the drug product for the proposed acute indication; therefore, the new drug application (NDA) may be approved from a nonclinical perspective with the recommended labeling changes, which describe risks for reproductive and developmental effects based on animal studies.
L361: ## 7.1.1. General Toxicology Findings
L362: The systemic safety of SUZ and a major human metabolite, M6-SUZ (also referred to as M6 and VRT-1875420), were evaluated in 28-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys. In addition, the safety of SUZ and impurities/degradants in the drug substance and drug product was evaluated in 91-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys.
L363: In the 28-day rat study, organ weights of the liver and ovaries increased in the high-dose groups. However, there were no associated histopathological changes in the liver or ovaries. In the 28-day monkey study, increased incidences of soft/watery feces were observed in treated groups. The mean organ weight of the thyroid/parathyroid was increased in the females without statistical significance.
L364: In the 91-day rat study, oral administration of SUZ to female rats for 91 days resulted in ovarian cyst formation, decreased number of corpora lutea, pituitary gland hyperplasia, and mammary hyperplasia/dilatation. In the 13-week monkey study, reduced weights of testes (-43 to -46%), epididymis (-14 to -38%), and prostate gland (-47 to -49%) were observed in SUZ treated males without histopathological correlates. While the testis weights appeared to be within the range of organ weights of untreated Cynomolgus monkey of similar ages based on published literature (Haruyama et al. 2012; Amato et al. 2022), it is unclear whether there were testicular functional
L365: effects. Of note, no significant changes in these organ weights in the 28-day monkey studies were noted. Therefore, there is minimal concern for the proposed indication.

L376: ## 7.1.2. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L377: Reproductive and developmental toxicities of SUZ were assessed in male and female fertility and early embryonic development studies in rats, embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rats/rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development (PPND) study in rats.
L378: SUZ doses up to 1000 mg/kg did not affect male reproductive performance when administered at least 28 days before mating and throughout mating. In a female fertility study in rats, increased preimplantation loss was observed at oral SUZ doses of 15 mg/kg when administered prior to mating and through gestation day (GD) 7. These effects on fertility in female rats appear to be reversible as pre-implantation loss was not observed when female rats were mated following discontinuation of SUZ for 4 weeks.
L379: In the rabbit EFD study, higher postimplantation loss and lower fetal body weights were observed at 200 mg/kg/day when administered on GDs 7 to 20. In the rat EFD study, higher
L380: postimplantation loss and lower fetal body weights were observed at 15 mg/kg/day and higher when administered on GDs 6 to 17.
L381: In the rat PPND study, reduced mean gestation duration and increased postnatal pup mortality between birth and postnatal day 4 were observed at \ge 10 mg/kg, and increased incidences of fully resorbed litters, fewer live newborn pups, and reductions in pup body weights were observed at 15 mg/kg when SUZ was orally administered to pregnant rats from GD 6 to lactation day 20. No effects on learning and memory or sexual maturation were observed at doses up to 15 mg/kg.

L395: They noted
L396: referenced published studies that demonstrated Nav1.8 knockout mice are fertile (Akopian et al. 1999). In addition, the Applicant demonstrated SUZ is a potent rat progesterone receptor (PR) antagonist by conducting in vitro PR reporter assays
L397: (b) (4) While it is reasonable to conclude that the reproductive toxicity findings observed in rats may not be due to the on-target effect inhibiting Na_v1.8, the submitted data/information are insufficient to dismiss other potential off-target effects in addition to rat PR antagonism effects (b) (4) of SUZ. Additionally, the Applicant
L398: However, a direct comparison of IC_{50} values from these two independent assays, which used engineered cell systems overexpressing human or rat PR, is very challenging because it is difficult to normalize these two systems and compare their values. In addition, there are some limitations to predicting in vivo inhibitory activity from the submitted in vitro data. Taken together, there are insufficient data to allow us to definitively conclude (b) (4)
L399: ## 7.1.3. Mutagenesis and Carcinogenesis
L400: SUZ was evaluated for genotoxic potential in a full battery of genotoxicity studies, including an in vitro Ames, an in vitro micronucleus assay, and an in vivo micronucleus assay in rats. SUZ was not considered mutagenic nor clastogenic in these assays.
L401: The major human metabolite, M6-SUZ, was also negative in an Ames assay and posed minimal concern for clastogenicity.
L402: ## 7.1.4. Other Toxicity
L403: SUZ drug substance was nonirritant in an in vitro skin irritation test and was not a skin sensitizer in a local lymph node assay in the mouse. SUZ was not considered an eye irritant based on an eye hazard potential evaluation using the reconstructed human cornea-like epithelium model. SUZ was nonphotoreactive based on in vitro and in vivo studies.
L404: ## 7.1.5. Impurities
L405: The proposed specifications for drug substance impurities and drug product degradants are acceptable. Additional impurities of concern were adequately justified for safety.
L406: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L407: SUZ is a selective Na_v1.8 blocker that represents a new pharmacologic class of analgesic medications. Aside from the safety data for SUZ that were reviewed in this NDA, there are limited data available to inform safety concerns based on drug class.
L408: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L409: SUZ has not been approved in any country or region for marketing. Therefore, no postmarketing experience is available at the time of this review.
L410: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L411: The primary sources of safety data are the two controlled phase 3 studies (Studies 104 and 105) and an open-label, uncontrolled phase 3 study (Study 107). Additional data are available from two phase 2 studies (Studies 101 and 102). Studies 101 and 104 were conducted in subjects after bunionectomy, while Studies 102 and 105 were conducted in subjects after abdominoplasty. Study 107 was an open-label evaluation of SUZ in subjects with acute pain resulting from a variety of surgical or nonsurgical conditions. In addition, 13 phase 1 studies evaluated SUZ in healthy subjects and special populations (subjects with hepatic and renal impairment).
L412: The Applicant performed analyses for the individual studies and two pooled populations. These pooled populations consisted of the pooled phase 3 controlled studies (Studies 104 and 105) and the pooled phase 2 and 3 controlled studies (Studies 101, 102, 104, and 105). The Applicant states that the latter pool is a supportive data pool. Data from the phase 1 and open-label phase 3 studies were not pooled given significant differences in their study designs and/or populations.
L413: The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review. No major issues were identified with respect to coding and categorizing adverse events. This review focused primarily on comparisons between the treatment arms in the phase 3 controlled studies. The major differences between these two studies were those relating to the acute pain model studied and procedure-specific subject selection criteria and anesthetic techniques. Pooling of the safety data from these studies is acceptable considering the otherwise similar study designs and selection criteria. The use of descriptive analyses of safety was predefined in the protocol for summarizing the safety outcomes. Separate analyses of the individual phase 3 controlled studies are described in Section 17.2 of this review.
L414: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L415: The safety database provided by the Applicant is acceptable for a comprehensive safety assessment of SUZ for the proposed indication, dosage regimen, and duration. In the 18 clinical studies mentioned in Section 7.4, a total of 1,883 unique subjects received at least one dose of SUZ. This included 1,266 subjects with acute pain who received the to-be-marketed dose regimen in the phase 2 and 3 studies, 169 subjects in the acute pain studies who received a lower dose of SUZ, 393 healthy subjects in the phase 1 studies, and 55 subjects in the phase 1 studies in special populations. In the controlled phase 3 studies, 874 subjects received at least one dose of SUZ at the to-be-marketed dose regimen. In the open-label, uncontrolled phase 3 study, 256 subjects received at least one dose of SUZ at the to-be-marketed dose regimen. A summary of the duration of exposure in the phase 3 studies is shown below in Table 29 and Table 30.

L424: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L425: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Studies VX22-548-104 and VX22-548-105 (Studies 104 and 105)
L426: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L427: The safety evaluation for SUZ was adequate, and the demonstrated safety profile of this medication administered in subjects with moderate or severe acute pain is acceptable for the indicated doses. There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ. The incidence of serious
L428: adverse events (SAEs) and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences between the treatment arms. The incidence of any AE was lower in the SUZ arm than in both the HB/APAP and placebo arms.

L436: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L437: There was one death in the placebo group of Study 105 due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism. There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
L438: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L439: There was no substantial difference in overall incidence of SAEs experienced by subjects in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of SAEs were experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm. There were no substantial differences between the treatment arms for any specific SAE. No SAEs were considered to be related or possibly related to the study drug by the study investigators. Most of these SAEs, such as those related to bleeding, respiratory disorders, and infections, are known risks associated with surgical procedures and would not be unexpected considering the surgical acute pain models used in these studies. Additional details regarding these SAEs can be found in Section 17.3.

L447: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L448: There were no substantial differences in the incidence of AEs leading to discontinuation in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of AEs leading to discontinuation were experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm.
L449: There were no substantial differences between the treatment arms in the incidence of any specific AE leading to discontinuation. Similar to the SAEs described in Section 7.6.1.3, many of the
L450: AEs leading to discontinuation are known to be associated with surgical procedures and would not be unexpected considering the surgical acute pain models used in these studies.
