L456: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L457: **Table 34** presents the common AEs by preferred term (PT) and system organ class (SOC) in Studies 104 and 105 which occurred in at least 0.3% of subjects in any arm. The percentage of subjects with any AEs was lower in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo arms. There were no PTs or SOCs for which the incidence was substantially higher in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP or placebo arms.
L458: No AEs occurred more frequently in the SUZ arm compared to the placebo arm by an incidence of 1.0% or greater. Notable imbalances in AEs between the treatment arms were generally favorable for the safety profile of SUZ. Notable findings included a lower percentage of subjects with the AEs of nausea, dizziness, and hypotension in the SUZ treatment arm than in the placebo arm, as well as a lower percentage of subjects with the AEs of vomiting, nausea, and headache in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP arm.

L466: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L467: Analyses of laboratory data did not raise major safety concerns. Median changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms for the majority of the laboratory assessments conducted in these studies.
L468: There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 35 and Table 36 demonstrate that a higher percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had creatine phosphokinase (CPK) and estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) values exceeding certain threshold levels compared to the other treatment arms. Table 37 demonstrates that the mean changes in creatine phosphokinase and creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from
L469: baseline was lower in the SUZ treatment arm at 48 hours. However, the magnitude of these changes is relatively small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L490: ## 7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L491: Figure 4 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury (DILI). There were no subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy's Law quadrants. There was a lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (1%) in the Temple's Corollary quadrant compared to the HB/APAP (3%) and placebo (2.6%) treatment arms. These findings do not suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced liver injury.
L492: **Figure 4. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Pooled Studies 104 and 105**
L493: Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
L494: Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
L495: A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN criteria within 30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN criteria are circled in red.
L496: The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and Temple's Corollary cases.
L497: All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
L498: In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
L499: For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table "Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot ..." and the listing "Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening..."
L500: Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen 5/325 mg every 6 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal
L501: ## 7.6.1.8. Vital Signs and ECG, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L502: Review of the vital sign data and electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters did not reveal significant clinically meaningful safety concerns. As depicted in Table 38 and Table 39, during the treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and diastolic blood pressures in the SUZ arm compared to the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of this difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L513: ## 7.6.1.9. Subgroups, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
L514: Table 40 depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different treatment arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroups, which limited the conclusions that could be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects \ge65 years of age, and subjects of races other than Black, African American, or White exposed to SUZ.

L519: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, Study VX22-548-107 (Study 107)
L520: ## 7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Study 107
L521: There were no deaths. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low. The majority of AEs were mild in severity. The following analyses of the safety results in Study 107 are limited by the lack of a comparator given the single-arm design of this study.

L530: ## 7.6.2.2. Deaths, Study 107
L531: There were no deaths.
L532: ## 7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 107
L533: There were two SAEs. No SAEs were considered to be related to the study drug by the study investigators. The SAE of cellulitis occurred in a subject who underwent a brachioplasty, and postoperative infection is a known risk associated with surgical procedures. The SAE of suicidal ideation occurred in a subject with situational social stressors that appeared unrelated to the study treatment. Additional details regarding these SAEs can be found in Section 17.3.

L559: ## 7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 107
L560: Analysis of laboratory findings did not raise clinically meaningful safety concerns. The lack of a comparator in this single-arm study limits evaluation of the relationship between SUZ and the observed laboratory findings.
L561: Changes in CPK, creatinine, and eGFR similar to those seen in the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies were observed. These are summarized in Table 45. There was an increase in CPK at discharge compared to baseline, which resolved by end of treatment. There was an increase in creatinine and a decrease in eGFR at discharge compared to baseline. These changes from baseline were larger in magnitude at end of treatment, but resolved by safety follow-up. Overall, the magnitude of these changes was relatively small and unlikely to be clinically significant. Because the end of treatment in this study was Day 14, or earlier for subjects discontinuing study drug before Day 14, it is unknown whether these laboratory changes would have increased in magnitude to become clinically significant with longer duration of treatment.

The magnitude of this difference was generally small. Vital signs could have been affected by subjects' qualifying conditions for this study (i.e., being in the postoperative period), and the lack of a comparator arm limits the interpretability of these results.

L586: ## 7.6.2.9. Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale Analyses, Study 107
L587: One subject (0.4%) experienced the suicidal ideation event of “wish to be dead” during the treatment-emergent period. This was considered an SAE. The subject felt that the suicidal ideation was situational and related to family/relationship issues and limited mobility due to her postsurgical condition. This SAE was considered by the investigator to be not related to the study drug. Additional details regarding this SAE are provided in Section 17.3.
L588: ## 7.6.3. Safety Results, Pooled Phase 2 and 3 Studies
L589: The analyses of safety results from the pooled phase 2 and 3 studies in subjects exposed to a 100-mg loading dose of SUZ followed by 50 mg every 12 hours were generally supportive of the results from the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences between the treatment arms. The incidence of AEs was lower in the SUZ arm than in both the HB/APAP and placebo arms. There were no deaths in the phase 2 studies. Adverse events by PT were generally consistent with those seen in the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies.
L590: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L591: ## 7.7.1. Risk of Negative Effects on Renal Function
L592: ## Issue
L593: Changes in clinical laboratory assessments of renal function, including eGFR and creatinine, appear to be more common in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms.
L594: ## Background
L595: The Applicant is evaluating SUZ for the treatment of pain associated with diabetic peripheral neuropathy (DPN) in addition to the acute pain indication that is the subject of the current NDA review. They have conducted one phase 2 study in subjects with painful DPN (VX21-548-103), which evaluated the dose regimens of SUZ 69, 46, or 23 mg daily compared to pregabalin 100 mg three times daily for a 12-week treatment period.
L596: In Study VX21-548-103, the most common AE in subjects who received SUZ was creatinine clearance (CrCl) decreased. As shown in Table 47 and Table 48, there appears to be a dose-response relationship, with the highest SUZ dose group having the highest incidence of this AE and the largest change from baseline in creatinine. Eleven subjects (8.0%) across the SUZ groups discontinued the study drug due to CrCl &lt;60 mL/min compared to 4 (7.4%) subjects in the pregabalin group. Subjects who met this CrCl threshold were required to discontinue the study drug due to pregabalin labeling recommendations for decreased renal function. The Applicant asserts that the increased incidence of AEs of CrCl decreased and treatment discontinuations due to this AE in the SUZ 69 mg dose group compared to other groups was an artifact of study conduct related to baseline imbalances in CrCl.
L597: Any risk of decreased renal function associated with SUZ should be characterized and described in the labeling to allow healthcare professionals to assess the benefit-risk profile. The review of this risk issue in the acute pain program focused on changes in clinical laboratory assessments of renal function, their clinical significance, and related AEs.

L607: ## Assessment
L608: Clinical laboratory assessments of renal function measured in the phase 3 studies included creatinine and eGFR. Creatinine clearance was not measured in these studies. Longer-term data regarding renal function were generally not available since serum chemistry was not routinely assessed at the safety follow-up visit in the phase 3 studies. In Study 107, this was assessed only if a clinical finding during the treatment period required follow-up.
L609: As demonstrated in Table 37, which depicts pooled data from Studies 104 and 105, the mean change from baseline at 48 hours was negative for creatinine and positive for eGFR in all three treatment arms. Considering that this pattern was observed in the placebo arm, this overall trend may be related to the peri- and intraoperative administration of intravenous fluids and a subsequent dilution effect. The magnitude of the decrease in creatinine and increase in eGFR was similar between the HB/APAP and placebo arms. However, the magnitude of these changes in the SUZ arm was smaller compared to the other arms.
L610: Study 107 was a single-arm study; therefore, a comparison of changes in renal function parameters to another treatment was not possible. However, as demonstrated in Table 45, there was a positive mean change in creatinine from baseline to discharge and end of treatment and a negative mean change in eGFR from baseline to discharge and end of treatment. There was essentially no mean change from baseline in these assessments at the safety follow-up visit.
L611: The magnitude of the changes in the renal function laboratory values described above were overall small and were considered unlikely to be clinically significant based on the data available. In the SUZ arm of the phase 3 studies, there were no SAEs or AEs leading to study drug discontinuation that were directly related to renal function. In the phase 3 controlled studies, the incidence of AEs in the SOC of renal and urinary disorders was lower in the SUZ arm than in the other arms as demonstrated in Table 49. There is no notable imbalance towards the SUZ arm for any of the other AEs listed in this table. In Study 107, the incidence of renal AEs was low.

L618: ## **Conclusion**
L619: There does not appear to be a clinically meaningful impact on renal function associated with SUZ in the acute pain setting. The population for the painful DPN indication may be at higher risk of changes in renal function associated with SUZ because of potential baseline renal impairment due to diabetes. It is also unknown whether the longer duration of treatment for chronic pain compared to acute pain is associated with increased risk of renal impairment in the painful DPN population. These concerns should be further evaluated in the Applicant's painful DPN studies with SUZ.
L620: ## **7.7.2. Duration of Use**
L621: ## **Issue**
L622: (b) (4)
L623: The Applicant is proposing treatment with SUZ
L624: However, there are limited data from the acute pain trials to inform duration of use.
L625: ## **Background**
L626: The proposed indication for SUZ is for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. Acute pain is typically sudden in onset and time limited. The draft guidance for industry *Development of Non-Opioid Analgesics for Acute Pain* (February 2022) defines acute pain as “lasting up to 30 days, typically in response to some form of tissue injury, such as trauma or surgery.” It is well known that a subset of subjects with acute pain will go on to develop chronic pain.
L627: (b) (4)
L628: Studies 104 and 105 were controlled phase 3 acute pain studies that evaluated SUZ for a treatment duration of 48 hours. Study 107 was a single-arm phase 3 acute pain study that evaluated SUZ for a treatment duration of up to 14 days or until pain resolved, whichever occurred first. No other studies have evaluated the acute pain dosage regimen of SUZ (100 mg first dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours) for a longer treatment duration. (b) (4)
L629: ## Assessment
L630: Study 107 is the only source of clinical data to inform the safety of longer-term use of SUZ for acute pain. This was a single-arm study, which has significant limitations for the evaluation of safety or efficacy. Subjects in this study could complete study drug treatment due to reaching pain resolution, in addition to the usual reasons for study discontinuation, such as AEs. The median duration of treatment was 11 days as depicted in Table 50, and 105 (41.0%) subjects completed treatment through Day 14. Only 22 subjects were exposed to SUZ for longer than 14 days. Therefore, there are limited clinical data to inform use of SUZ beyond 14 days.

L636: The safety results from this study are discussed in Section 7.6.2. In summary, there were no deaths, and the incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low. The most common AEs were generally consistent with those seen in the controlled phase 3 studies. An analysis of subjects who had at least one AE that occurred within different time windows after treatment started is depicted in Table 51 and suggests that there was no consistent pattern between duration of treatment and incidence of AEs. There are no reliable efficacy data to inform duration of use given the design of Study 107 and the efficacy data collected (Patient Global Assessment of study drug).

L642: The safety data in Study 107 are generally favorable.
L643: (b) (4)
L644: SUZ
L645: has the potential to be widely used since the proposed indication of moderate-to-severe acute pain is broad and extremely common. SUZ represents a new pharmacologic class, which introduces uncertainty regarding risk and favors a conservative approach to risk management.
L646: (b) (4)
L647: ## Conclusion
L648: The Applicant's proposal (b) (4) for duration of use is not acceptable. In the labeling, (b) (4)
L649: language describing the duration of use studied will be used to inform prescribers about limitations in the available safety data.
L650: ## 7.7.3. Risk of Reproductive and Developmental Effects
L651: ## Issues
L652: Toxicities of SUZ were observed in rat reproductive and developmental studies such as increased pre-implantation loss, increased post-implantation loss, lower fetal body weights, and increased pup mortality.
L653: ## Background
L654: In a female fertility study in rats, increased pre-implantation loss was observed at oral SUZ doses of 15 mg/kg when administered prior to mating and through gestation day (GD) 7. These effects on fertility in female rats appear to be reversible as pre-implantation loss was not observed when female rats were mated following discontinuation of SUZ for 4 weeks.
L655: In the rabbit EFD study, higher post-implantation loss and lower fetal body weights were observed at 200 mg/kg/day when administered on GDs 7 to 20. However, these effects occurred at a dose that also caused maternal toxicity so it is unclear if they are SUZ related. In the rat EFD study, higher post-implantation loss and lower fetal body weights were observed at 15 mg/kg/day
L656: and higher when administered on GDs 6 to 17. These occurred in the absence of maternal toxicity.
L657: In the rat PPND study, reduced mean gestation duration and increased postnatal pup mortality between birth and postnatal day 4 were observed at \ge 10 mg/kg, and increased incidences of fully resorbed litters, fewer live newborn pups, and reductions in pup body weights were observed at 15 mg/kg when SUZ was orally administered to pregnant rats from GD 6 to lactation day 20. No effects on learning and memory or sexual maturation were observed at doses up to 15 mg/kg.
L658: The Applicant provided reproductive toxicity data with other \text{Nav}1.8 blockers, which did not show similar reproductive toxicity findings, to support that those reproductive toxicity findings observed in rats treated with SUZ are not due to \text{Nav}1.8 antagonism. In addition, the Applicant also provided \text{IC}_{50} comparison of SUZ against human versus rat progesterone receptor (PR) by conducting two independent *in vitro* PR reporter assays (one against human PR and the other against rat PR).

L664: ## **Assessment**
L665: While it is reasonable to conclude that the reproductive toxicity findings observed in rats may not be due to the on-target effect inhibiting \text{Nav}1.8 and rat PR antagonism effects of SUZ may play a role in pre-/post-implantation loss observed in rats, the submitted data/information were insufficient to dismiss other potential off-target effects of SUZ in addition to rat PR antagonism effects. Additionally, the Applicant
L666: However, a direct comparison of \text{IC}_{50} values from these two independent assays, which used engineered cell systems overexpressing human or rat PR, is very challenging because it is difficult to normalize these two independent systems and compare their values. Of note, even the positive controls produced \text{IC}_{50} values in these assays were markedly different than indicated in the technical manual of the assay and in vivo values indicated in the public
L667: domain; therefore, a direct comparison of IC50 values from two independent in vitro assays was considered inappropriate. Lastly, there are some limitations to predicting in vivo inhibitory activity from the submitted in vitro data.
L668: ## Conclusion
L669: Taken together, there are insufficient data to allow us to definitively conclude (b) (4) Relevant labeling languages were included in Section 8.3 and 13.1.
L670: ## 7.8. Abuse Potential
L671: The Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) conducted a review of the abuse potential of SUZ. The abuse- and dependence-related TEAE data provided by the Applicant suggest that SUZ did not produce abuse- or dependence-related effects under the tested conditions. CSS does recommend that TEAEs be monitored for dependence if SUZ is investigated for chronic use. In summary, SUZ does not have a potential for abuse and should not be controlled under the Controlled Substances Act. CSS's review dated December 6, 2024, is excerpted below:
L672: ## Conclusions
L673: CSS has reviewed the nonclinical and clinical abuse-related data submitted in NDA 219209 for JOURNAVX (suzetrigine) and concludes that the drug does not have abuse potential and should not be controlled under the CSA [Controlled Substances Act]. This conclusion is based on the following:
L674: - Suzetrigine is a new molecular entity with a novel mechanism of action proposed for the indication of moderate to severe acute pain.
- In vitro binding and activity studies indicate suzetrigine and its active metabolite, M6-SUZ, function as highly selective Nav1.8 channel blockers. Suzetrigine is also an antagonist at the gamma-aminobutyric acid A (GABAA) receptor, dopamine transporter (DAT), and norepinephrine transporter (NET), however, not at clinically relevant doses. Similarly, M6-SUZ is an antagonist at the GABAA and Orexin 1 (OX1) receptors, however, not at clinically relevant doses. As a result, suzetrigine does not appear to bind or have activity at neuronal targets associated with having a potential for abuse.
- Behavioral studies using suzetrigine in rats and nonhuman primates produced no CNS mediated behaviors, and no signs associated with abuse potential.
- An analysis of CNS-mediated AEs that can be indicative of abuse liability or physical dependence was conducted on the clinical studies provided by the Applicant. This analysis indicated that the most prevalent AEs were dizziness and somnolence. There were no concerning reports of AEs that suggest that suzetrigine has a potential for abuse or physical dependence.
- Based on the data provided above, there is no indication that suzetrigine has a potential for abuse. As a result, the Applicant was not asked to conduct animal or clinical studies to directly assess the abuse potential of suzetrigine.
L675: ## Recommendations
L676: Based on the data provided in NDA 219209, CSS recommends that:
L677: - Suzetrigine not be controlled in any schedule under the CSA
- The Applicant should not include Section 9 Drug Abuse and Dependence in their proposed labeling for JOURNAVX
L678: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L679: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L680: ## Hepatic Impairment
L681: Study 007 compared the pharmacokinetics of orally administered SUZ in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh A) and moderate (Child-Pugh B) hepatic impairment to age-, weight-, and sex-matched healthy subjects.
L682: There was no clinically significant effect of mild hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ; hence, no dosage adjustment is needed in these patients.
L683: In subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, SUZ and M6-SUZ exposures (area under the concentration-time curve [AUC]) were 1.50-fold and 1.15-fold higher, respectively, than those in matched healthy subjects. Therefore, a reduced loading dose of 100 mg, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next three doses, followed by 50 mg once daily (qd) for all subsequent doses, not to exceed 14 days is recommended for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment. Modeling and simulation of the regular dosing regimen (100 mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h) and the proposed reduced dosing regimen (100 mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next three doses, followed by 50 mg qd) indicated that exposure during the first 2 to 3 days was similar between the two dosing regimens. At steady state, subjects with hepatic impairment exhibited higher accumulation than did healthy subjects under the regular dosing regimen. In contrast, the reduced dosing regimen resulted in an approximately 20% reduction in PK metrics at steady state, including a 17% reduction in maximum plasma concentration at steady state (C_{max,ss}), a 22% reduction in trough plasma concentration at steady state (C_{trough,ss}), and a 23% reduction in daily AUC_{ss} (refer to pharmacometric analysis in Section 14.5 of this review). Hence, the review team agrees with the proposed reduced dosing in moderate hepatic impairment.
L684: SUZ has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment, and therefore should not be used in these patients.
L685: ## Renal Impairment
L686: The mass balance study showed that renal excretion of SUZ is minimal. In a dedicated renal impairment study, the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not altered in subjects with renal impairment of eGFR 15 to &lt;60 mL/min (estimated by the chronic kidney disease epidemiology collaboration equation with adjustment for body surface area). Therefore, the review team agrees that no dosage adjustment is needed in patients with renal impairment of eGFR &gt;15 mL/min.
L687: The effect of kidney failure not receiving dialysis (eGFR &lt;15 mL/min) on SUZ and M6-SUZ pharmacokinetics is unknown.
L688: ## **Other Intrinsic Factors**
L689: Based on population PK analysis, no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ were observed based on age (18 to 75 years), sex, body weight (44 to 128 kg), or race.
L690: ## **8.2. Extrinsic Factors**
L691: ## **8.2.1. Effect of Food**
L692: The proposed indication for SUZ is acute pain and therefore, the attainment of sufficient initial concentrations after dosing is an expectation for the onset of pain relief. In the phase 3 studies of SUZ (104, 105 and 107), the first dose (100 mg) was administered in fasting condition, and subsequent doses were administered without regard to food. In the phase 3 studies, with the first dose under a fasted state, the median time to onset of perceptible pain relief in abdominoplasty (Study 105) and bunionectomy (Study 104) was 34 minutes and 60 minutes, respectively.(b) (4)
L693: The effect of food on the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ was evaluated in two studies, 011 and 016, using the 50 mg to-be-marketed formulation at a dose of 100 mg. Study 011 evaluated the effect of a high-fat meal (800 to 1000 calories, 50% derived from fat) on the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ. Study 016 evaluated the effects of various meal types (low-fat meal [up to 500 calories with no more than 25% derived from fat], moderate-fat meal [600 calories, 30% derived from fat], and high-fat meal) and the timing of meal administration (60 minutes predose, at 0 hours, and 60 minutes postdose) on SUZ and M6-SUZ pharmacokinetics.
L694: Administration of SUZ after consuming high-fat meals and moderate-fat meals resulted in considerable reduction in SUZ concentrations in initial time points. This decrease in initial concentrations was well represented in individual level PK data where multiple subjects showed very low concentrations for the first 2 to 3 hours postdose in the fed state versus fasted state (Figure 18 and Figure 19). The high-fat meal and moderate-fat meal delayed the median time to maximum concentration (T_{max}) of SUZ from 3 hours in the fasted state to 5 hours in the fed state and that of M6-SUZ from 8 to 10 hours in the fasted state to 24 hours in the fed state. The C_{max} and AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not affected by any of the meal conditions.
L695: A high-fat meal consumed 1 hour after the administration of SUZ did not affect the systemic exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ. Administration of the second dose of 50 mg with or without a meal is also predicted to not affect the systemic exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
L696: Based on the observed decreased initial concentrations and delayed T_{max} with both moderate- and high-fat meals in comparison to a fasted condition,(b) (4)
L697: (b) (4)
L698: (b) (4) Based on overall data, the review team's recommendation for food effect is as follows: "Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without food." Taking the first dose with food may result in a delayed onset of pain relief.
L699: ## **Relative Bioavailability Comparison Between Phase 2 Formulation and To-Be Marketed Formulation**
L700: Study 011 evaluated the relative bioavailability between the to-be-marketed SUZ 50-mg tablet and the phase 2 formulation, SUZ 10-mg tablet, at a dose of 100 mg in the fasted state.
L701: At the 100-mg dose, the SUZ 50-mg tablets (to-be-marketed formulation) showed bioequivalence to the 10-mg tablets (phase 2 formulation) in terms of AUC_{0-\infty} while the C_{max} was approximately 15% lower with the lower 90% CI value marginally outside bioequivalence limits. M6-SUZ showed bioequivalence for both AUC_{0-\infty} and C_{max}. The SUZ T_{max} was unchanged between the two formulations (3 hours) while the 50-mg tablets showed delayed T_{max} (10 hours) versus 10-mg tablets (5 hours) for M6-SUZ. Since the dosing regimen of SUZ is a 100-mg loading dose followed by 50 mg twice daily (b) (4) the minor differences in pharmacokinetics between the to-be-marketed formulation and the phase 2 formulation are considered not clinically relevant. In addition, the phase 3 studies (104 and 105) were conducted with the 50-mg to-be-marketed (TBM) tablet, which achieved statistical significance for efficacy in comparison to placebo for the treatment of acute pain.
L702: ## **8.2.2. Drug Interactions**
L703: ## **Effect of Other Drugs on SUZ**
L704: ## **Strong CYP3A Inhibitors**
L705: SUZ and M6-SUZ are cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A substrates. The effect of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was evaluated in Study 008. The coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole increased the AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 4.79-fold (90% CI: 4.28, 5.35) and 4.42-fold (90% CI: 3.64, 5.37), respectively. The upper bound of the 90% CI was greater than 5; therefore, SUZ and M6-SUZ can be considered sensitive substrates of CYP3A. The coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole resulted in an increase of SUZ C_{max} by 1.47-fold and a 31.7% decrease in the C_{max} of M6-SUZ. These results indicated that CYP3A is the main enzyme involved in metabolism for both SUZ and M6-SUZ.
L706: The Applicant proposed that SUZ (b) (4) Since the increase in exposure of SUZ and M6-SUZ is close to 5-fold, where a 100-mg loading dose of SUZ can behave as a 500-mg single dose for which there is no safety coverage (the maximum single dose of SUZ evaluated was 395 mg), the review team proposed the "contraindication" of SUZ with strong CYP3A inhibitors in the label.
L707: ## **Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors**
L708: For the effects of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ, the Applicant predicted an exposure increase using physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling.
L709: The FDA's PBPK review team conducted its own analysis. Results of the FDA's PBPK model indicated that fluconazole (a moderate CYP3A inhibitor) is predicted to increase the geometric mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 3.0-fold (2.9 to 3.1) and 2.6-fold (2.5 to 2.6), respectively; increase the C_{max} of SUZ by 1.20-fold (1.2 to 1.2); and decrease the C_{max} M6-SUZ by 26% (23 to 28).
L710: Based on the increased exposure with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, the Applicant proposed a reduced SUZ dosing regimen of a 100-mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next three doses, followed by 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses for up to 14 days. The proposed adjusted dose regimen is expected to result in a 25% increase in C_{max,ss}, 27% increases in both C_{trough,ss} and daily AUC_{ss}. These changes in the PK metrics are unlikely to impact efficacy. Based on the population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis, the proposed reduced dosing regimen with moderate CYP3A inhibitors is considered acceptable. Refer to PBPK and popPK reviews for additional details (Section 14.5 of the review).
L711: ## Weak CYP3A Inhibitors
L712: For weak CYP3A inhibitors, PBPK simulation results predicted a SUZ PK exposure ratio of approximately 1.1 after a single SUZ dose in the presence of cimetidine. Therefore, no dosage adjustment is needed with weak CYP3A inhibitors.
L713: ## Strong and Moderate CYP3A Inducers
L714: The effect of a strong CYP3A inducer, rifampin, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was evaluated in Study 013 (Part B), while the effect of a moderate inducer was predicted using PBPK modeling.
L715: Based on the observed results, rifampin decreased the geometric mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 93% (91 to 94%) and 85% (83 to 86%), respectively, indicating that SUZ is a sensitive CYP3A substrate. Rifampin decreased the C_{max} (90% CI) of SUZ by 80% (75 to 83%) and increased the C_{max} of M6-SUZ by 30% (14 to 46%).
L716: Based on PBPK modeling, efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A inducer) was predicted to decrease the geometric mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 63% (61 to 65) and 60% (58 to 62), respectively. Efavirenz was predicted to decrease the C_{max} of SUZ by 29% (27 to 32) and increase the C_{max} of M6-SUZ by 32% (28 to 36). Therefore, the Applicant proposes that SUZ
L717: (b) (4) Since the exposure significantly decreased with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers, the review team's proposal is to "avoid concomitant use of SUZ with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers."
L718: ## Weak CYP3A Inducers
L719: For weak CYP3A inducers, the PBPK simulation results predicted a PK exposure ratio of approximately 0.8 to 1.0 following a single 100-mg dose. No dosage adjustment is needed with weak CYP3A inhibitors.
L720: ## Proton Pump Inhibitors (Omeprazole)
L721: Study 013 (Part A) evaluated the effect of omeprazole on the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ in healthy subjects. Relative to SUZ administered alone, the coadministration of SUZ and omeprazole resulted in a 28% increase in AUC, a 4.5% decrease in C_{max}, and no change in
L722: T_{max} for SUZ. Mean SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations with and without omeprazole were similar over the first 6 hours postdose, during which the maximum effect is expected. M6-SUZ C_{max} (7% increase) and AUC (17% increase) were not affected by omeprazole. Since omeprazole did not affect the oral absorption or decrease the systemic exposure of SUZ, no dosage adjustment is needed.
L723: ## **Effect of SUZ on Other Drugs**
L724: ## **Certain CYP3A Substrates**
L725: SUZ is a CYP3A inducer. Study 010 evaluated the effect of SUZ on the pharmacokinetics of a sensitive CYP3A substrate, midazolam. SUZ decreased the mean (min-max) AUC of midazolam by 48% (43-53) and C_{max} by 37% (27-45). The Applicant has not proposed any labeling recommendations for CYP3A substrates. Since SUZ decreases the exposure and thereby compromises the efficacy of certain CYP3A substrates, the review team included following labeling recommendations for 1) CYP3A substrates for which minimal concentration changes may lead to loss of efficacy, and 2) sensitive CYP3A substrates:
L726: - Avoid concomitant use of SUZ with CYP3A substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to loss of efficacy unless otherwise recommended in the Prescribing Information for CYP3A substrates.
- When using SUZ with sensitive CYP3A substrates, consider increasing the dose of substrate drugs based on their Prescribing Information if needed or use alternative medications.
L727: ## **P-gp Substrates**
L728: SUZ is an inhibitor of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) based on in vitro studies. The effect of repeated doses of SUZ on the pharmacokinetics of digoxin (a P-gp substrate) was evaluated in Study 010. SUZ did not affect the pharmacokinetics of digoxin, indicating that SUZ is not an inducer or inhibitor of P-gp. No dosage adjustment is needed for P-gp substrates when administered with SUZ.
L729: ## **Hormonal Contraceptives**
L730: SUZ induces CYP3A4, which is one of the enzymes involved in the metabolism of oral hormonal contraceptives. Therefore, the Applicant evaluated the effect of repeated doses of SUZ on the pharmacokinetics of oral hormonal contraceptives levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol in Study 010. Levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol are synthetic progestin- and synthetic estrogen-containing contraceptives, respectively. SUZ did not show any significant effect on the systemic exposure of levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol as measured by AUC and C_{max}. Therefore, SUZ can be used with oral contraceptives containing levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol.
L731: In the label, in addition to levonorgestrel, the Applicant included another progestin, norethindrone, based on the sensitivity of progestins to CYP3A induction (Wiesinger et al. 2020). Based on this literature article, norethindrone has comparable or lower sensitivity than levonorgestrel to CYP3A induction caused by a strong CYP3A inducer (rifampin). Since there was no interaction between SUZ and levonorgestrel, no interaction is expected between SUZ and norethindrone. The Applicant's proposal to add norethindrone along with levonorgestrel in the
L732: label is acceptable. The review team included the following labeling recommendations for hormonal contraceptives:
L733: Avoid concomitant use of JOURNAVX with hormonal contraceptives containing progestins other than levonorgestrel and norethindrone. If contraception is desired or needed, alternative contraceptives (such as a combined oral contraceptive containing ethinyl estradiol as the estrogen and levonorgestrel or norethindrone as the progestin, or an intrauterine system) or additional nonhormonal contraceptives (such as condoms) should be used during the use of JOURNAVX and for 28 days after discontinuation of JOURNAVX.
L734: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L735: No studies in the pediatric population have been submitted in this NDA application. The Applicant is requesting a deferral for the initiation of pediatric clinical studies in subjects from birth to &lt;17 years of age until SUZ is determined to have a positive benefit-risk profile in the adult population for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain.
L736: The Applicant plans to fulfill the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement through four randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled clinical studies in pediatric subjects with moderate-to-severe acute pain as proposed in the agreed initial pediatric study plan from May 3, 2024. Each study will include two parts evaluating the safety and pharmacokinetics (parts A and B) and efficacy (part B) of SUZ. Part A will be open-label and used to confirm or adjust the planned doses of SUZ before dosing additional subjects in the same age group in part B.
L737: The studies will be conducted progressively from &lt;17 years of age to birth per the following sequence:
L738: - Study C1: Subjects 12 to &lt;17 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain (b) (4)
- Study C2: Subjects 6 to &lt;12 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain (b) (4)
- Study C3: Subjects 1 to &lt;6 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain.
- Study C4: Subjects from birth to &lt;1 year of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain.
L739: Two pivotal good laboratory practice (GLP) nonclinical juvenile studies are planned to support pediatric clinical studies:
L740: - Study N2: Conduct a juvenile animal study in the rodent model to characterize the impact of SUZ on the developing brain to support clinical studies in pediatric subjects from birth to &lt;3 years of age.
- Study N4: Conduct a juvenile animal study in the rodent model to characterize the impact of SUZ on the developing brain to support clinical studies in pediatric subjects from 3 to &lt;12 years of age.
L741: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L742: Pregnant individuals experience acute pain due to both obstetric and nonobstetric causes. SUZ would be an alternative to opioid treatment of acute pain, and its use to treat moderate-to-severe acute pain in females of reproductive potential is anticipated to be high. The Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health (DPMH) was consulted for this NDA. Key portions of their review are summarized below. DPMH provided labeling recommendations for subsections 8.1, 8.2, 8.3 and section 17 of labeling for compliance with the Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule. Refer to DPMH's full review for additional details.
L743: ## Pregnancy
L744: There are insufficient data on SUZ use in pregnancy to allow an assessment of the drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or other adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. The Applicant reported two subjects with positive pregnancy tests during the clinical development program (Study VX22-548-107) as follows:
L745: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Study Day 1), the 36-year-old subject had joint stabilization surgery. The same day, the subject had a negative urine pregnancy test, was enrolled, and received the first dose of SUZ 100 mg/50 mg q12h. The subject took rescue medications, including ibuprofen and acetaminophen. On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Study Day 14), the subject had a negative urine pregnancy test. The subject received their last dose of the study drug the same day. On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Study Day 28), the subject had two positive urine pregnancy tests at their safety follow-up visit. On [REDACTED](b) (6), a serum human chorionic gonadotropin test was positive. The estimated date of conception was [REDACTED](b) (6) with a last menstrual period of [REDACTED](b) (6). Follow-up information reported that a [REDACTED](b) (6) g male infant was delivered [REDACTED](b) (6) via elective cesarean section for frank breech presentation. The surgery was uncomplicated. Mother and infant were discharged home in good condition.
- Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) One pregnancy occurred in a 30-year-old subject. On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Study Day 1), the subject was enrolled and received the first dose of SUZ 100 mg/50 mg q12h for the treatment of acute pain in the left upper extremity. The subject had a negative urine pregnancy test the same day. The subject took rescue medications, including ibuprofen and acetaminophen. On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Study Day 14), the subject received their last dose of SUZ. The same day, the subject had a positive pregnancy test. The subject was lost to follow-up.
L746: Collecting pregnancy outcome data is needed to evaluate the potential effects in humans based on the findings in animals. Therefore, DPMH recommends issuing a postmarketing requirement (PMR) for a pregnancy exposure registry study and a complementary pregnancy study. Because miscarriages are common in females of reproductive potential, occurring in 15% to 20% of all clinically recognized pregnancies, chart review is necessary as part of the claims database complementary study for outcome validation to minimize misattribution of events as drug-related.
L747: ## Lactation
L748: Lactation data for SUZ are not currently available for humans. Since SUZ use during lactation is anticipated and the physicochemical properties of SUZ indicate it will likely be transferred into breast milk, DPMH recommends issuing a PMR for a clinical milk-only lactation study. The data obtained from a clinical milk-only lactation study would be helpful in determining whether SUZ is present in human breast milk, and if so, in what amounts. If a clinical milk-only lactation study demonstrates a clinically important amount of SUZ in breast milk, further study to examine systemic exposure in the breastfed infant may be needed in the future.
L749: ## Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
L750: There are no clinical data to inform labeling related to human fertility. Nonclinical data revealed increased preimplantation loss at oral SUZ doses \ge 2.2 times the MRHD when administered prior to mating and through Gestation Day 7 in female rats. The relevance to humans is unclear, but based on these findings, SUZ may impair female fertility.
L751: A drug-drug interaction study was performed with an oral combined contraceptive containing ethinyl estradiol and levonorgestrel, and SUZ did not have a clinically relevant effect on the exposures of the tested hormones. Norethindrone was not evaluated in the dedicated drug-drug interaction (DDI) oral contraceptives study but is added in the proposed labeling by the Applicant based on the literature comparison of CYP3A sensitivity to levonorgestrel. Other progestins were not studied and thus, a recommendation either to avoid concomitant use of SUZ with hormonal contraceptives containing other progestins or to use an alternative hormonal or nonhormonal contraceptive during coadministration with SUZ and for 28 days after the final dose is warranted in labeling subsection 8.3.
L752: ## 9. Product Quality
L753: ## Approval
L754: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 219209 with respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. Therefore, OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective. The requested expiry of 24 months is granted with the storage conditions of controlled room temperature: 68°F to 77°F (20°C to 25°C), excursions permitted to 59°F to 86°F (15°C to 30°C).
L755: ## 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations

As noted in the meeting discussion, "although Study 107 is not designed to evaluate a specific safety signal and may not meet the criteria for a covered clinical study (i.e., studies for which financial disclosure is required), Vertex may submit a financial disclosure for Study 107 for completeness of the NDA submission."

L768: ## 11. Advisory Committee Summary

L770: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L771: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L772: On May 30, 2024, Vertex Pharmaceuticals Inc. (Vertex) submitted an original new molecular entity new drug application (NDA 219209) for suzetrigine (SUZ, also referred to as VX-548 and VRT-1737548) tablets, a selective voltage-gated sodium channel (Na_v1.8) inhibitor, for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. NDA 219209 received a priority review determination and was filed under Section 505(b) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act (FDCA) on July 30, 2024. The NDA was reviewed according to the provisions of “the Program,” with a priority designation, under the Prescription Drug User Fee Act VII.
L773: Vertex Pharmaceuticals initiated the SUZ development program under investigational new drug application (IND) 146185, which was submitted on January 8, 2020. The Applicant conducted two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies, *VX22-548-104: A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled Study Evaluating the Efficacy and Safety of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain After a Bunionectomy* (Study 104) and *VX22-548-105: A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled Study Evaluating the Efficacy and Safety of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain After an Abdominoplasty* (Study 105), which were the primary sources of efficacy data for SUZ. Studies 104 and 105 provided controlled safety data over a 48-hour treatment period, while *VX22-548-107: A Phase 3, Single-arm Study Evaluating the Safety and Effectiveness of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain* (Study 107) provided uncontrolled safety data over a treatment period of up to 14 days. Study 107 was a phase 3 open-label study that evaluated subjects with moderate-to-severe acute pain from a variety of surgical and nonsurgical conditions.
L774: Notable regulatory milestones for this application include:
L775: - Fast Track designation for SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain was granted on March 5, 2020.
- Breakthrough Therapy designation for SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain was granted on June 30, 2022.
- Rolling review was granted on March 18, 2024.
L776: Milestone meetings related to the request for marketing approval:
L777: - A type B, end-of-phase 2 (EOP2) meeting was held on July 14, 2022, to obtain the Division's input on the proposed development plan and phase 3 clinical studies to support the registration of SUZ for the indication to treat moderate-to-severe acute pain. Vertex's planned pivotal phase 3 program consisted of two adequate and well-controlled studies, one in bunionectomy and the other in abdominoplasty, and a single-arm safety and effectiveness study in a broad range of surgical and nonsurgical acute pain conditions.
- Type B, EOP2, chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) only, written responses were sent to Vertex on December 1, 2022, with preliminary feedback on Vertex's proposed regulatory starting materials, impurity control strategy, and SUZ drug substance formal stability data proposal.
L778: - Type B, Breakthrough Therapy, written responses were sent to Vertex on December 22, 2022, regarding Vertex's abuse liability assessment plan. The Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) agreed that no further nonclinical studies or human abuse potential studies were required at that time to assess the abuse potential of SUZ. CSS suggested that Vertex conduct an animal physical dependence and withdrawal study for assessment of dependence. The requested physical dependence and withdrawal study was conducted in rats, and the study design was agreed upon with CSS prior the study initiation. The results and report were provided at the time of NDA submission.
- A type D, guidance, CMC-only meeting was held on June 8, 2023, to discuss Vertex's intention to leverage FDA Manual of Policies and Procedures 5015.13 to expedite the time to NDA submission, assessment, and marketing approval for SUZ and to receive Agency feedback regarding the proposed SUZ drug substance and drug product stability data package.
- A type B, guidance meeting was held on December 13, 2023, to obtain alignment with the Division on several procedural topics related to the upcoming NDA submission and adequacy for filing for SUZ.
L779: On May 30, 2024, Vertex submitted a proposed proprietary name request for JOURNAVX, which was found conditionally acceptable on August 15, 2024. The midcycle communication meeting occurred on October 2, 2024, with minutes issued on November 1, 2024. Labeling negotiations for the NDA were opened with Vertex on November 26, 2024. The late-cycle communication meeting occurred on December 5, 2024, with meeting minutes issued on January 2, 2025. Pediatric studies required by section 505B(a) of the FDCA were deferred for birth to less than 17 years, and four clinical postmarketing requirements (PMRs) and two nonclinical PMRs were issued. PMRs were also issued under section 505(o)(3) of the FDCA for a pregnancy exposure registry study, a complementary pregnancy study, and a clinical lactation study.
L780: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L781: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L782: The nonclinical safety profile of SUZ (also referred to as VRT-1737548 and VX-548) supporting this NDA was originally reviewed under IND 146185. All nonclinical safety studies conducted in support of SUZ were also submitted to the present NDA and are reviewed in the following sections.
L783: ## 13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L784: SUZ is a selective human voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (\text{Na}_v1.8) blocker. The selectivity of SUZ relative to other known voltage-gated sodium channels (\text{Na}_v 1.1 through 1.9) was tested in vitro, and the half-maximal inhibitory concentration (\text{IC}_{50}) of SUZ on \text{Na}_v1.8 was 0.7nM, over 30,000\times lower than the \text{IC}_{50} of other \text{Na}_v channels. In addition, SUZ was tested for its inhibitory activity of \text{Na}_v1.8 in dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons of human, monkey, rat,
L785: mouse, rabbit, and dog in vitro for species selectivity. SUZ was at least 70-fold less potent in rodent \text{Na}_v 1.8 than in human or monkey \text{Na}_v 1.8. Of note, a major human metabolite, M6-SUZ (also referred to as VRT-1875420 and M6), is also a potent human \text{Na}_v 1.8 blocker with an \text{IC}_{50} value of 0.5nM. See Table 53 for additional details.
L786: **Table 53. Summary of the In Vitro Pharmacology Studies for SUZ**
L787: Study/Study No.	Findings
Mechanism of action	SUZ selectively inhibits a voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (\text{Na}_v 1.8), which is expressed in DRG neurons, suppressing action potential firing in DRG neurons and reducing pain signaling.
Non-GLP In Vitro Characterization of VRT-1737548 Pharmacology and Selectivity/
Study No. P272	SUZ inhibited \text{Na}_v 1.8 (TTX-resistant) currents in primary DRG neurons in single cell voltage-clamp using a manual patch-clamp electrophysiology (\text{IC}_{50} of 0.68\pm 0.16\text{nM}).
SUZ inhibited action potential firing in primary human DRG in current-clamp manual patch electrophysiology.
Based on \text{IC}_{50} levels, SUZ inhibition of \text{Na}_v 1.8 current in DRG neurons was more potent in human and monkey than in rat, mouse, rabbit, and dog DRG neurons in vitro. See Table 54 and Figure 6 below.

L817: ## 13.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
L818: Secondary pharmacology studies of SUZ and M6-SUZ (a major human metabolite) were conducted to screen activity/binding against other ion channels, receptors, enzyme, and transporters (176 targets). The in vitro secondary pharmacology screens demonstrated SUZ binds to several targets, including the human glucocorticoid receptor, human OX1 receptor, human progesterone receptor (PR)-B (80%), and human norepinephrine transporter, with the highest binding affinity to the human progesterone receptor (IC_{50}: 0.39\mu M) out of the targets evaluated.

VRT-1875420 inhibited glucocorticoid receptor agonist (dexamethasone) binding (IC_{50} 4.9\mu M) and OX1 receptor agonist (orexin-A) binding (IC_{50}: 2\mu M).

Study No. VX-548-TX-022	
The Applicant compared IC_{50} values of SUZ against human versus rat progesterone receptor (PR) by conducting two independent in vitro PR reporter assays (one against human PR and the other against rat PR) and concluded SUZ is 94-fold less potent in inhibiting human PR (IC_{50}: 4240nM) compared to the rat PR (IC_{50}: 45nM), concluding there are approximately 94-fold differences.
A direct comparison of IC_{50} values from these assays is challenging because it is not clear how comparable these two different engineered system overexpressing human or rat PRs are based on differences in EC_{50} used in these assays. See Table 59 below. Of note, it appears that the human PR reporter assays conducted by the Applicant reported an IC_{50} value of 0.422nM for one of the positive controls, mifepristone, a known progesterone receptor inhibitor. This is greater than 10-fold higher than the IC_{50} value of mifepristone to human PR reported in the public domain and in the assay technical manual.

L829: ## 13.1.3. Safety Pharmacology
L830: SUZ and M6-SUZ were evaluated in good laboratory practice (GLP) in vitro human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) assays, and they had no effect on hERG channel activity (IC_{50} greater than 10\muM). SUZ was tested for effects on the central nervous system (CNS), cardiovascular system, and respiratory system in a GLP single-dose safety pharmacology study and a GLP 28-day repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys. SUZ and M6-SUZ had no effects on CNS, cardiovascular, and
L831: respiratory parameters at doses up to 100 mg/kg. Of note, M6-SUZ, which is a major human metabolite, is also a major metabolite in monkeys. Based on toxicokinetic data of this metabolite in the 28-day repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys, there are approximately \ge 2\times safety margin for the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of suzetrigine (i.e., 100 mg loading dose and 50 mg maintenance dose every 12 hours).

L836: ## 13.1.4. ADME/PK
L837: The in vitro and in vivo studies designed to assess absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) and pharmacokinetics are described below. Based on similarities in potency against Nav1.8 and metabolic profile between monkeys and humans, monkeys were considered an appropriate species for use in the pivotal nonclinical safety studies. Additionally, the Applicant selected the rat as the rodent species based on the metabolism profile and achievable free drug maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), which was up to 9-fold higher than the rat IC50 for Nav1.8 inhibition in vitro. While there appeared to be significant potency differences (approximately 80-folds) for SUZ inhibition of rat versus human Nav1.8 in vitro, the Applicant
L838: investigated the potency of SUZ for Na_v1.8 in several species (i.e., mice, rat, dog, monkey, and rabbit) to demonstrate due diligence. Based on the Applicant's justifications noted above, the review team agreed that rat was an acceptable species for toxicity testing.
L839: ## 13.1.4.1. Absorption and PK
L840: In vivo pharmacokinetic (PK) studies were conducted in monkeys, mice, dogs, and rats via oral or intravenous (IV) administration. See Table 61 below.
L841: **Table 61. Summary of Absorption/PK Studies for SUZ**
L842: Study Title/Study No.	Study Design/Findings
Pharmacokinetics, Absorption, Metabolism, and Excretion of [^{14}C]VX-548 Following Oral and Intravenous Administration to Monkeys/ Study No. SDPK37598	Male cynomolgus monkeys were treated with [^{14}C]SUZ up to 15 mg/kg via oral gavage or 2.5 mg/kg via a single IV injection (N=3/group). Half-life was 38.0h in blood, and 30.2h in plasma after a single oral dose of 15 mg/kg and oral bioavailability was 69.5% in blood and 72.2% in plasma.
After 2.5 mg/kg single intravenous dosing in monkeys (N=3), the mean volume of distribution levels in blood and plasma were 2000 and 1350 g/kg, respectively, and 75.2% of the dose was recovered in bile.
Pharmacokinetics of VRT-1737548 in Male Cynomolgus Monkeys following Oral Administration of Single Doses at 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg and at 30 mg/kg/ Study No. S099	In cynomolgus monkeys (N=3/group), a single oral administration of 30 mg/kg SUZ achieved 3.9-fold higher AUC_{0-inf} level compared to SUZ.
Pharmacokinetics of VRT-1737548 Following a Single Intravenous Bolus Administration to Male CD-1 Mice/ Study No. P194	After a single intravenous dose of 1 mg/kg in male CD-1 mice (N=4), the half-life of SUZ was 3.75h.
Pharmacokinetics of VRT-1737548 Following a Single Intravenous or Oral Administration to Male Beagle Dogs/ Study No. P197	Oral bioavailability was >100% with SUZ in dogs.
The effective half-life of SUZ in the male dogs was 22.8 hours after single intravenous bolus injection (1 mg/kg, N=3).
Pharmacokinetics of VRT-1737548 Following a Single Intravenous or Oral Administration to Sprague-Dawley Rats/ Study No. P199	Half-life of SUZ in the male rats (N=2) was 0.861h after single intravenous infusion of 1 mg/kg.
Oral bioavailability was 68.1% in male rats after single oral administration (3 mg/kg, solution formulation, N=3).
Exposure of SUZ (AUC_{0-inf} and C_{max}) was similar at 30 mg/kg and 2.44-fold higher at 300 mg/kg when dosed than when dosed in female rats (N=3/group) via the oral route.

L865: ## 13.1.4.2. Distribution
L866: Plasma protein binding of SUZ, M6-SUZ, and M3-SUZ, which was evaluated in vitro, was high and similar across species (i.e., &gt;98% in rat and monkey and ≥99% in dog and human for SUZ; &gt;91% in rat, monkey, and human and 99% in dog for M6-SUZ; &gt;97% in rat, monkey, dog, and human for M3-SUZ).
L867: Volume of distribution at steady state of SUZ was 1.01, 3.54, and 2.18 L/kg after an intravenous dose of 1 mg/kg in male beagle dogs, Sprague-Dawley rats, and cynomolgus monkeys, respectively.
L868: Distribution to CNS was observed in rats (brain and spinal cord) after oral administration and in monkeys (cerebrospinal fluid, brain, spinal cord, and DRG) after IV or oral administration. See Table 66 through Table 70 below.
L869: Radioactivity concentrations in melanin-containing tissues in rats (i.e., the uveal tract, pigmented skin, and meninges) peaked between 1 and 4 hours postdose then declined to undetectable levels by 72 hours postdose. Therefore, binding to melanin-containing tissues does not appear to be irreversible.

L910: ## 13.1.4.3. Metabolism
L911: Figure 8. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ
L912: Source: Study P158, Page 16, Figure 4
L913: Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine
L914: SUZ was mainly cleared by metabolism in rats, and unchanged SUZ was not detected in bile and was minimal in urine (&lt;0.1%) and feces (0.2%) after IV administration. In rats, M3-SUZ was the major metabolite in plasma (95.9%). M19-SUZ and M20-SUZ were major metabolites in rat bile.
L915: Figure 9. Structural and Description of Moieties of SUZ
L916: Source: Study Q262, Page 9, Figure 1
L917: Abbreviation: SUZ, suzetrigine; THF, tetrahydrofuran
L918: Figure 10. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ in Rats
L919: Chemical structures illustrating the proposed biotransformation pathway of SUZ (VX-548) in rats. The pathway shows the conversion of VX-548 to metabolites M3, M34, M30, M32, M20, and M19.

- VX-548 is converted to M3 (VRT-1875424).
- M3 is converted to M34 and M30.
- M30 is converted to M32.
- M32 is converted to M19.
- M3 is converted to M20.
L920: Note: Only metabolites &gt; 3% of administered dose or &gt;2 % of circulating total radioactivity are shown in the scheme.
L921: Source: Study Q262, Page 40, Figure 2
L922: Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine (VX-548)
L923: In monkeys, M6-SUZ and M12-SUZ were major metabolites in the plasma. M20-SUZ and M38-SUZ were major metabolites in monkey bile. Unchanged SUZ was not detected in bile and was minimal in feces (&lt;2% of the radioactive dose) and urine (1% of the radioactive dose) following both oral and IV dosing in monkeys.
L924: Figure 11. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ in Monkeys
L925: Chemical structures illustrating the proposed biotransformation pathway of SUZ (VX-548) in monkeys, showing metabolites M6, M3, M12, M20, and M38.

The pathway starts with VX-548, which undergoes metabolism to form M6 (VRT-1875420) and M3 (VRT-1875424). M6 is further metabolized to M12 (VRT-1875671). M3 is metabolized to M20. M12 is metabolized to M38. M20 and M38 are shown as major metabolites.
L926: Note: Only metabolites &gt; 5\% of administered dose or circulating total radioactivity are shown in the scheme.
L927: *M12 is formed from M6 only4
L928: Source: Study S001, Page 33, Figure 2
Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine (VX-548)
L929: Cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A4, CYP3A5, and CYP2C19 were major SUZ biotransformation enzymes in vitro. M6-SUZ was metabolized to M12-SUZ by CYP3A4 and CYP2C9 in vitro. Non-CYP contribution was 20 to 30% in vitro.

L972: ## **Figure 12. Biotransformation Pathway of M6-SUZ**
L973: Metabolism of
VRT-1737548
L974: Figure 12 illustrates the biotransformation pathway of M6-SUZ (VRT-1875420). The pathway shows the conversion of VRT-1875420 to VRT-1875671 (All species). VRT-1875671 is then metabolized to two products:

- M1-420 (Rat only, New metabolite): Formed by oxidation (+O) and conjugation (+Glu).
- M2-420 (Rat, Dog, Cyno, Previously observed VRT-1737548 M18): Formed by conjugation (+Glu).

L995: ## **Figure 14. Biotransformation Pathway of M12-SUZ**
L996: Chemical structures illustrating the biotransformation pathway of VRT-1875671 (M12-SUZ) into metabolites M1-671 and M2-671.

VRT-1875671 structure is shown on the left, leading via arrows to the structures of M1-671 and M2-671.

**M1-671:** Structure shown with a hydroxyl group (\text{HO}) on the phenyl ring. Labeled: Rat, cyno, human New Metabolite. Formation involves +\text{O} and +\text{Glu}.

**M2-671:** Structure shown with a hydroxyl group (\text{HO}) on the phenyl ring. Labeled: All species Previously observed as VRT-1737548 M18. Formation involves +\text{Glu}.
L997: Source: Study S164, Page 13, Figure 6-3

Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine
L999: ## 13.1.4.4. Excretion
L1000: ## **Biliary Excretion**
L1001: In rats, 72 to 75% of the administered dose was excreted into bile after oral and IV administration. In monkeys, 75% of the administered dose was excreted into bile after the IV administration.
L1002: ## **Placental Transfer/Lacteal Excretion**
L1003: In GD 13 Sprague-Dawley rat fetuses, the highest radioactivity concentrations were observed in the lungs and spinal cord at 8 hours postdose.
L1004: In GD 18 Sprague-Dawley rat fetuses, the highest radioactivity concentrations were observed in the heart, spinal cord, and adrenal gland.
L1005: In lactating Sprague-Dawley rats (lactation day 10), exposure of total radioactivity in milk was approximately 3-fold higher than in blood and plasma after a single oral administration (10 mg/kg), with peak concentration at 2 hours postdose.

L1078: ## 13.1.5. General Toxicology
L1079: Systemic safety of SUZ and M6-SUZ were evaluated in 28-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys. In addition, the safety of SUZ and impurities/degradants in the drug substance/drug product were evaluated in 91-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys.
L1080: In the 28-day rat study, weights of the liver and ovaries increased in the high-dose groups. However, there were no associated histopathological changes in the liver or ovaries. In the 28-day monkey study, increased incidences of soft/watery feces were observed in treated groups. The mean organ weight of the thyroid/parathyroid was increased in females without statistical significance. The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be 1,000 mg/kg/day (male) and 300 mg/kg/day (female) for rats and 100 mg/kg/day for both sexes in monkeys.
L1081: In a 91-day rat study, males were dosed at 0, 200, 600, and 1,000 mg/kg/day and females at 0, 10, 100, and 1,000 mg/kg/day via oral gavage. Ploughing, salivation, and wet fur at the mouth were observed in test article-treated animals at \ge 100 mg/kg/day. Minimal-to-moderate ovarian follicular cysts were observed in female rats treated with the test article at \ge 100 mg/kg/day. Higher incidences of minimal-to-mild decreased number of corpora lutea, minimal-to-mild distalis hyperplasia in the pituitary gland, moderate-to-marked dilatation in the mammary gland were observed in females treated at 100 mg/kg compared to the concurrent and historical controls. Minimal-to-mild cortical hypertrophy in the adrenal gland was observed in females treated at 1,000 mg/kg/day, which could be a result of stress responses. The NOAELs for males and females were 1,000 mg/kg/day and 10 mg/kg/day, respectively.
L1082: In a 13-week monkey study, animals were treated with SUZ at 0, 20, 50, and 150 mg/kg via oral gavage. Of note, no recovery group was included in this study. Test article-treated animals showed increased soft/liquid feces and decreased thymus weight, which is associated with decreased thymic cellularity, which could be related to stress responses. In addition, reduced weights of testis, epididymis, and prostate gland were observed in SUZ treated males; however, there were no adverse microscopic findings while all animals including the control group showed immaturity in the testis. There were no test article-related changes in body weights, ophthalmoscopy/electrocardiogram (ECG)/ hematology/clinical chemistry/urinalysis parameters, and thyroid biomarkers. The NOAEL for both sexes was determined to be 150 mg/kg.

L1096: Study/Study No.	Study Design/Findings
VX-548: A 13-Week Oral (Gavage) Toxicity and Toxicokinetics Study in Cynomolgus Monkeys/863-866
VX-548-TX-002	
Animals were dose at 0, 20, 50, and 150 mg/kg once daily for 13 weeks (N=4/sex/group) via oral gavage.
Results:
No adverse effects were observed on survival, body weights, ophthalmoscopy, ECG, hematology, clinical chemistry, urinalysis, thyroid biomarker, or gross pathology.
Increased incidences of soft/liquid feces were observed in all SUZ-treated groups.
Decreased thymus weight (up to 37%) and minimal to mild decreased cortical cellularity in the thymus were observed in all SUZ-treated groups, which were considered as stress response.
Dose-dependent decreases were observed in male reproductive tissue organ weights across drug treated groups. Epididymis weights were decreased from -14 to -38% compared to control. Testis weights were decreased from -43 to -46% compared to control. Prostate weights were decreased from -47 to -49% compared to control. However, there were no microscopic correlates. In addition, the mean organ weights observed in the SUZ-treated groups were within the ranges of those in untreated cynomolgus monkeys of similar age. Also of note, changes in these organ weights in the 28-day and 9-month monkey studies were generally of lower magnitude, did not follow a dose-dependent trend for incidence, and the standard deviations were often very large and, in some cases, were larger than the mean values. In addition, the 28-day and 9-month monkey studies did not report histological correlates in these tissues. Therefore, the organ weight changes do not appear to be toxicologically significant and do not appear to have relevance for the proposed acute indication. Of note, Nav1.8 is highly expressed in testes based on information the Applicant provided. Because of the low number of monkeys in the study, the lack of recovery groups, and known variability in male reproductive organ weights in this species, the nonhuman primate data do not provide an ideal model to evaluate the potential risk of SUZ to male reproductive tissues in these longer duration studies. Should further evaluation be deemed necessary to support long-term clinical use, studies in human could be considered.
The NOAEL was 150 mg/kg.
L1097: Source: Study No. VRT-1737548-TX-011, Study No. VRT-1737548-TX-012, Study No. VX-548-TX-001, and Study No. VX-548-TX-002

L1099: ## 13.1.6. Genetic Toxicology
L1100: SUZ was evaluated for genotoxicity potential in a full battery of genotoxicity studies, including an in vitro Ames assay, an in vitro micronucleus assay, and in vivo micronucleus assay in rats. SUZ was not considered mutagenic or clastogenic in these assays.
L1101: M6-SUZ (a major metabolite in human and monkeys) was also evaluated for genotoxicity potential. An Ames study that tested M6-SUZ demonstrated the metabolite was not mutagenic. An in vitro chromosomal aberration test was not conducted with M6-SUZ. However, the chromosomal aberration potential of M6-SUZ was assessed through the in vivo micronucleus assay that tested the parent SUZ, which was negative for genotoxicity. Although M6-SUZ is a
L1102: minor metabolite in rats, the systemic exposure levels of M6-SUZ achieved in rats after SUZ oral administration in this study exceeded the steady-state levels in humans at the maximum recommended daily dose. Together with the negative Ames result of M6-SUZ, negative genetic toxicology battery of SUZ, and in silico quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) data with M6-SUZ, the weight of evidence supported minimal concern for clastogenicity.
