L1110: ## 13.1.7. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L1111: Reproductive and developmental toxicities of SUZ were assessed in fertility (male and female) and early embryonic development studies in rats, embryo-fetal development studies in rats/rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development study in rats. SUZ did not affect male reproductive performance. However, SUZ produced pre- and postimplantation loss, lower fetal body weight, and lower postnatal survival in rats and postimplantation loss and lower fetal body weights in rabbits. Additional investigational studies demonstrated that SUZ affects the estrus cycle in rats and inhibits the rat PR, suggesting that inhibition of the PR may contribute to the SUZ effects on reproduction. Refer to Sections 7.1 and 13.1.8 for a summary of these studies and discussion about the adequacy of those data to support the Applicant's position.
L1112: ## 13.1.7.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L1113: In a male fertility study, male rats were treated with 0, 200, 600, or 1,000 mg/kg of SUZ from at least 28 days before mating and throughout mating with untreated female rats, and there were no adverse effects on male reproductive performance or sperm parameters. Thus, the NOAEL for male reproductive toxicity was 1,000 mg/kg.
L1114: In a female fertility study, female rats were treated with 0, 5, 10, or 15 mg/kg of SUZ from a minimum of 14 days prior to mating, throughout mating with untreated male rats, and continuing until gestational day (GD) 7. There were higher mean preimplantation losses in the 15 mg/kg group. Thus, the NOAEL for female fertility toxicity was 10 mg/kg based on increased preimplantation loss.
L1115: **Table 99. Summary of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Studies**
L1116: Study/Study No.	Findings
An Oral (Gavage) Study of the Effects of VX-548 on Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to Implantation in Crl:CD(SD) Sprague-Dawley Rats/
Study No. VX-548-TX-013	
Male rats (N=22/sex/group) were treated orally with SUZ at 200, 600, and 1,000 mg/kg/day for a minimum of 28 days prior to mating and throughout mating with untreated females.
Female rats (N=22/sex/group) were treated orally with SUZ at 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg/day for a minimum of 14 days prior to mating, throughout mating with untreated males, and through Gestation Day 7.
Results:
No effect on survival, body weights, or food consumption in treated males, untreated females, or treated females
No effect on reproductive performance of treated males or treated females
No effect on sperm motility, sperm concentration, and sperm morphology
No male-mediated effects on implantation sites, or intrauterine survival in untreated females
No effect in estrous cycling in treated females
Mean preimplantation loss (17.8% per litter) in the 15 mg/kg treated females was higher compared to the control (4.8% per litter) and exceeded the (b) (4) historical control range (12.7% per litter)
NOAEL for male reproductive toxicity is 1,000 mg/kg
NOAEL for female reproductive toxicity was 10 mg/kg based on increased preimplantation loss

VX-548: A 4-Week Investigational Oral (Gavage) Study to Evaluate the Reversibility of the Effects of Estrous Cyclicity Following 4-and 6-Week Recovery Periods in Female Sprague-Dawley Rats/
Study No. VX-548-TX-023	
Female rats received 0, 15, or 100 mg/kg SUZ for 4 weeks via oral gavage and then had a no-dosing period of 4 or 6 weeks prior to mating with untreated males (N=12/sex/group). Females were necropsied on GD 13.
Results:
No SUZ related effect on reproductive performance: No effects on female mating index, female fertility index, female pregnancy index, precoital interval, estrous cycle length, or number of cycles
No SUZ related effect on ovarian/uterine examination: No effects on number of corpora lutea, number of implantations, preimplantation loss, number of live embryos, number of dead embryos, postimplantation loss, or number of early resorptions
L1117: Source: Study No. VX-548-TX-013, and Study No. VX-548-TX-023
Abbreviations: GD, gestation day; NOAEL, no-observed-adverse-effect level; SUZ, suzetrigine (VX-548)
L1118: ## 13.1.7.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
L1119: In dose range-finding studies for rat embryo-fetal development (EFD), all pregnant female rats that received \ge 25 mg/kg (GDs 6 through 17) had 100% postimplantation loss, which was a basis for the dose selection for the definitive EFD (dEFD) study in rats. In a rat dEFD study, pregnant rats were dosed at 0, 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg of SUZ during GDs 6 through 17. The high-dose group (15 mg/kg) showed higher postimplantation loss and a lower number of live fetuses. However, there was no SUZ-related effect on malformations or variations in the fetuses. The maternal NOAEL was 15 mg/kg and the developmental NOAEL was 10 mg/kg based on increased postimplantation loss and a lower number of live fetuses.
L1120: In a rabbit dEFD study, pregnant rabbits received 0, 50, 100, and 200 mg/kg of SUZ during GDs 7 through 20. The high-dose group (200 mg/kg) showed body weight loss and lower food consumption during dosing period, lower mean gravid uterine weight, lower adjusted body weight gain, higher postimplantation loss, and lower fetal body weights. There was no SUZ-related effect on malformations or variations in fetuses. The maternal NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/day based on evidence of maternal toxicity, including body weight loss and lower food consumption during treatment period and lower adjusted body weight gain. The developmental NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/day based on postimplantation loss and lower fetal body weights.
L1121: **Table 100. Summary of EFD Studies**
L1122: Study/Study No.	Findings
An Oral (Gavage) Study of the Effects of VX-548 on Embryo/Fetal Development in Rats/
Study No. VX-548-TX-007	
Pregnant rats (N=22/group) received SUZ orally once daily at 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg/day during GDs 6 to 17. Separate TK animals were included in this study.
Results:
The mean gravid uterine weight of the HD was 27.2% lower than that of the control but not statistically significant.
Body weight gain was lower in the HD than in the control during the treatment period of GDs 6 to 18.
The mean postimplantation loss (26.1% per litter) in the HD was higher than that in the control group (5.1% per litter) and exceeded the (b) (4) historical control range (up to 12.6% per litter), although not statistically significant.
Four females in the HD had entirely resorbed litters (100% postimplantation loss).
The mean number of live fetuses (9.7 fetuses/dam) was lower than that in the control group (13.5 fetuses/dam) and below the (b) (4) historical control range (11.4 fetuses/dam).
No SUZ-related effect on malformations or variations.
The maternal NOAEL was 15 mg/kg/day.
The developmental NOAEL was 10 mg/kg/day based on increased postimplantation loss and lower number of live fetuses.
L1123: Study/Study No.	Findings
VX-548: An Oral (Gavage) Embryo/Fetal Development Study in New Zealand White Rabbits/
Study No. VX-548-TX-009	
Pregnant rabbits (N=22/group) received SUZ orally once daily from GDs 7 to GD 20 at 50, 100, and 200 mg/kg/day. Separate TK animals were included in the study.
Results:
The HD group lost body weight and ended the period at 9% below the body weight of the control group during treatment period (GD 7 to GD21).
The HD group showed lower mean gravid uterine weight (~16%) and adjusted body weight gain (~77%) than did the control group.
The HD group showed significantly lower food consumption (57%) during the entire dosing period (GDs 7 to 21) and significantly higher food consumption (26%) following the cessation of treatment (GDs 21 to 29) than did the control group.
The mean postimplantation loss (11.9% per litter) in the HD was significantly higher than that in the control group (4.0% per litter) and exceeded the historical control range (up to 9.8% per litter).(b) (4)
The mean male and female fetal body weights were lower than those of the control group.
No SUZ-related effect on malformations or variations.
The maternal NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/day based on body weight loss during treatment period, lower food consumption during treatment period, and lower adjusted body weight gain.
The developmental NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/day based on postimplantation loss and lower body weights.

L1125: ## 13.1.7.3. Prenatal and Postnatal Development
L1126: In a pre- and postnatal development (PPND) study, pregnant rats received 0, 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg of SUZ once daily via oral gavage from GD 6 to lactation day 20. Two out of 22 F0 females dosed at 10 mg/kg/day (9% dam) and 12 out of 30 F0 females dosed at 15 mg/kg (40% dam) showed fully resorbed litters. F0 females dosed at 15 mg/kg also showed lower body weights (-36%) during the gestation period (GD 6 to GD 20), lower food consumption during gestation/lactation period, fewer live newborn pups, and a shorter mean gestation length. F1 pups from dams dosed at 10 mg/kg and 15 mg/kg showed a lower viability index from birth to postnatal day 4 (84% and 34% per litter, respectively, versus 98% per litter in the control), lower birth weights (13 to 33%), and lower mean body weights (8 to 20%) on postnatal day 21. F1 pups from dams dosed at 15 mg/kg also showed late pinna detachment (4.6 days) compared to the control (3.2 days). There was no effect on eye opening, pupillary reflex, balanopreputial separation, vaginal opening, auditory startle response, motor activity, and Biel Maze swimming trials in F1 pups. There was no effect on reproductive parameters (precoital interval, mating index, fertility index, or pregnancy index) in the F1 generation. The maternal NOAEL was 10 mg/kg based on lower food consumption during the gestation and lactation periods. The developmental NOAEL was 5 mg/kg based on lower body weight gains during preweaning at 15 mg/kg, total resorptions of litters, shorter gestation length, decreased postnatal survival, and lower pup body weight at 10 mg/kg and 15 mg/kg.

L1131: ## 13.1.8. Special Studies
L1132: ## 13.1.8.1. Investigative Studies
L1133: To evaluate the reversibility and effects on estrous cycle in female rats, female rats were dosed at 0, 15, or 100 mg/kg of SUZ for 4 weeks and had a no-dosing period of 4 or 6 weeks prior to mating with untreated males. There was no SUZ-related effect on reproductive performance (female mating index, female fertility index, female pregnancy index, precoital interval, estrous cycle length, or number of cycles), ovarian/uterine examination (number of corpora lutea, number of implantations, preimplantation loss, number of live embryos, number of dead embryos, postimplantation loss, or number of early resorptions), or macroscopic pathology.
L1134: In a 6-week investigational study, female rats received 0, 10, 100, or 300 mg/kg of SUZ for 6 weeks via oral gavage. While there were no test article-related effects on mortality, clinical signs, body weight, or food consumption, animals dosed at mid dose (MD) and high dose (HD) showed a significantly longer estrus state (93 to 120% increase in number of days) with an increase in the number of females in a state of prolonged/persistent estrus (\ge 7 days) and a significantly shorter diestrus state (40 to 54% decrease in number of days). In addition, the mean estradiol concentrations in estrous state in the MD and HD groups were higher than that in the control group (46 to 58% increase). Microscopically, increased incidences of ovarian cysts, increased atretic follicles in the ovary, decreased corpora lutea, persistent estrus in the vagina, and increased secretion in the mammary gland were observed in the MD and HD groups compared to the control group. Minimal severity of hyperplasia in the mammary gland was observed in one MD female and adrenal cortical hypertrophy was observed in two HD females.
L1135: In an 8-week investigational study, caulated female rats received 0 or 100 mg/kg of SUZ via oral gavage. Based on estrous cycle analysis, the SUZ-treated group had started a prolonged estrus state (7 or more consecutive days in estrus) generally within the first 2 to 3 weeks following initiation of dose administration. Hormone analyses showed that the SUZ-treated group showed higher mean estradiol, prolactin, and luteinizing hormone concentrations on both D2 (2nd day of diestrus) and D3 (3rd day of diestrus). Increased incidences of ovarian cysts, decreased number of corpora lutea, increased atretic follicles in the ovary, persistent estrous in the vagina, glandular dilatation, and atypical hyperplasia in the mammary gland were also observed.
L1136: **Table 102. Summary of Investigative Studies Evaluating SUZ Effects on Estrous Cycle in Female Rats**

Results:

Increased secretion in the mammary gland was observed at the MD and HD.

The NOAEL was 10 mg/kg.

Cannulated female rats (N=25/group) received 0 or 100 mg/kg of SUZ for 8 weeks via oral gavage.
Started a state of prolonged estrus generally within the first 2 to 3 weeks following initiation of dose administration.

Higher serum prolactin levels were reported on D2 and D3.

Glandular dilatation and atypical hyperplasia in the mammary gland were observed.

L1164: ## 13.1.8.3. Phototoxicity Assessment
L1165: The Applicant conducted a chemical photoreactivity assay per International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guideline *S10 Photosafety Evaluation of Pharmaceuticals* (November 2013). The molecular extinction coefficient of SUZ was 1339 Lmol-1cm-1 at the maximum absorbance observed of 290 nm. However, SUZ was nonphotoreactive (no generation of singlet oxygen/superoxide anion) with UVA/B and visible light. In addition, radioactivity concentrations in melanin-containing tissues (the uveal tract, pigmented skin, and meninges) declined to undetectable levels by 72 hours after peaking between 1 and 4 hours postdose in rats, suggesting no irreversible binding to melanin-containing tissues. Therefore, SUZ has low potential for phototoxicity and no further assessment is necessary.
L1166: **Table 107. Summary of Phototoxicity Studies**
L1167: Study/Study No.	Findings
In Vitro Chemical Photoreactivity Potential of VRT-1737548 Study No. VX-548-TX-024	Photoreactivity potential of SUZ (Lot #2, 200 µM) was evaluated by measuring superoxide anion formation via reduction of nitroblue tetrazolium and singlet oxygen formation via p-nitrosodimethyl aniline bleaching after exposure to UVA/B and visible light for 40 min.
Photosafety Assessment of VX-548 Study No. VX-548-TX-025	SUZ was nonphotoreactive based on ROS generation potential after exposure to UVA/B and visible light.
The molar extinction coefficient of SUZ (0.01 mg/mL in methanol) was 1339 L mol-1cm-1 at the maximum absorbance observed of 290 nm.

L1169: ## 13.1.8.4. Impurities
L1170: The proposed specifications for drug substance impurities and drug product specifications are acceptable. In addition, additional impurities of concern were adequately justified for safety.
L1171: Safety qualification of five organic impurities (b) (4) in the drug substance, which exceeded the appropriate qualification threshold (0.15%) as recommended in ICH guideline *Q3A(R2) Impurities in New Drug Substances* (October 2006), and two degradation products (b) (4) in drug product, which exceeded the appropriate qualification threshold (0.2% or 3 mg, whichever is lower) as recommended in Q3B, were evaluated in a 13-week toxicity study in monkeys. All the impurities were negative for mutagenic potential based on QSAR assessment, and the levels of these impurities were less than 1 mg (see Table 108). Of note, no additional genotoxicity studies are needed as recommended in ICH harmonised guideline *M7(R2) Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities in Pharmaceuticals To Limit Potential Carcinogenic Risk* (April 2023) when the total daily intake is lower than 1 mg/day of each impurities/degradants in the drug product.

L1178: In addition, two potential impurities (b) (4) were evaluated for mutagenic potential in Ames assays with a total of five *Salmonella* strains (TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537 and the *E. coli* strain WP2 *uvrA*). Both (b) (4) induced point mutations in the genome of *Salmonella typhimurium*. At the proposed specification of byproduct (b) (4) which is then converted to (b) (4) the levels (b) (4) were detectable at (b) (4) ppm, which gives an estimated daily exposure of (b) (4) µg/day at the SUZ maximum daily dose of 150 mg, and it is less than the acceptable daily intake for a chronic use product as recommended in the ICH M7 guidance (April 2023). Therefore, controlling (b) (4) is acceptable and ensures acceptable levels (b) (4) in the drug substance. Refer to the CMC review for additional details on impurities (b) (4) of SUZ.
L1179: An in silico analysis report (Study Report Number: VX-548-TX-031) (b) (4) of SUZ for mutagenic potential using acceptable QSAR models was submitted. The submitted data were confirmed by FDA computational toxicology experts and overall, no additional safety concerns were identified.

L1206: Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; HLM, human liver microsome; fm, fraction metabolized; MATE, multidrug and toxin extrusion; OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptide; OAT, organic anion transporter; OCT, organic cation transporter; SUZ, suzetrigine; UGT, uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase
L1207: ## **Absorption**
L1208: The permeability of SUZ was evaluated using Caco-2-cells, and SUZ showed high permeability and low efflux. The apparent permeability coefficient (P_{app}) values were 12.3\times 10^{-6} cm/sec for P_{app}(a-b) and 11.5\times 10^{-6} cm/sec for P_{app}(b-a), and the efflux (b-a)/(a-b) ratio was 0.94 (Study FDD-36383).
L1209: ## **Distribution**
L1210: The protein binding of SUZ and its major metabolite M6-SUZ in human plasma are approximately 99% and 96%, respectively (Study P171). Both human serum albumin and \alpha-1-acid glycoprotein contribute to SUZ and M6-SUZ binding in human plasma (Study T095). The plasma protein binding of SUZ and M6-SUZ are concentration-independent, and there was no interaction with regards to protein binding between SUZ and M6-SUZ; the presence of M6-SUZ did not impact the protein binding of SUZ, and vice versa (Study T409).
L1211: The blood-to-plasma partitioning ratio was 0.64 for SUZ and 0.62 for M6-SUZ (Study P172), which was consistent with the blood-to-plasma area under the concentration-time curve from time zero to infinity (AUC_{0-\infty}) ratio of 0.562 in the ^{14}C-radiolabeled human ADME study (Study 006), indicating SUZ and M6-SUZ did not preferentially distribute into red blood cells.
L1212: ## **Metabolism**
L1213: Metabolic profiling of SUZ was performed in vitro using hepatocytes from human as well as mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. The metabolites of SUZ identified in human hepatocyte incubation were pyridine N-oxide (M6-SUZ) and O-demethylated (M3-SUZ) derivatives, as well as M3 glucuronidase (M4-SUZ). Trace amounts of an O-demethylated, N-oxide derivative (M12-SUZ) was also detected. All metabolites identified in human hepatocytes were also identified in other species (Study P158). The further metabolic profiling of M6-SUZ and M3-SUZ using human hepatocytes identified that M6-SUZ could be further metabolized to M12-SUZ (Study S164).
L1214: Based on in vitro studies in recombinant CYP enzymes and human liver microsomes (HLMs) with CYP-selective chemical inhibitors (Reports P211, P154, R161, R276, and R184), the N-oxidation of SUZ to M6-SUZ is primarily mediated by CYP3A, with some contribution from CYP2C19. The O-demethylation of SUZ to M3-SUZ is primarily mediated by CYP3A, with minor contributions from CYP2C9 and CYP2C8. The metabolism of M6-SUZ is also mainly mediated by CYP3A4, with some contribution from CYP2C9. Flavin-containing monooxygenases (FMO1 and FMO3) are unlikely to be involved in SUZ oxidation (Report P154).
L1215: ## **Drug-Drug Interactions**
L1216: ## **Inhibition of CYP450**
L1217: The CYP inhibition potential of SUZ and its major metabolite M6-SUZ were evaluated in vitro. The IC_{50} values for reversible inhibition of a panel of CYP enzymes in HLMs, uncorrected or corrected for nonspecific binding of SUZ and M6-SUZ, are shown in Table 113 (Studies P164, U173). SUZ and M6-SUZ are not time-dependent inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4 (Studies P164 and S010). At the efficacious dose, the estimated unbound steady-state plasma C_{max} of 0.0132\mu M for SUZ and 0.124\mu M for M6-SUZ were significantly lower than the IC_{50} values, regardless of correction for nonspecific binding in HLMs, indicating low clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of SUZ and M6-SUZ via inhibition of CYP enzymes.
L1218: As SUZ is an orally administered drug product, with a 100-mg loading dose, the estimated gut concentration of SUZ is 845\mu M (100 mg dosed with 250 mL liquid) and the calculated R_{1, gut} value exceeds the cutoff value of 11, indicating potential inhibition of CYP enzymes by SUZ in the gut. However, further evaluation using a static mechanistic model predicted the AUC ratio of midazolam with and without SUZ was less than 1.25, indicating a low risk of DDI via CYP3A4 inhibition in the gut (Study S309).

L1227: ## Induction of CYP450
L1228: SUZ and M6-SUZ induced CYP3A4 mRNA expression and functional activity in all three hepatocyte lots in a dose-dependent fashion. CYP2B6 mRNA expression and functional activity in all three hepatocyte lots were also induced by SUZ and M6-SUZ, but to a smaller extent than that of CYP3A4. Induction of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19 mRNA expression was observed in at least one lot of hepatocytes. No induction of CYP1A2 by SUZ and M6-SUZ was observed (Studies P169, S006). Based on the in vitro data, SUZ was predicted to have the potential to be a weak to moderate CYP3A4 inducer at the efficacious dose, and the induction potential of M6-SUZ may increase the CYP3A4 induction risk. Therefore, further study of DDI risk caused by CYP3A4 induction was conducted (refer to Study 010). As the CYP2C enzymes are induced via activation of the pregnane X receptor, the same as CYP3A4, but are less sensitive, the CYP2C induction potential of SUZ is expected to be low (Table 115).

L1232: ## Substrate of Transporters
L1233: Based on the in vitro studies, SUZ is not a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, or OATP1B3. M6-SUZ is a P-gp substrate, but not a substrate for BCRP, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3 (Studies R243, U016, and R100)
L1234: ## Inhibition of Transporters
L1235: The inhibition of major transporters involved in drug disposition by SUZ and M6-SUZ was studied in vitro (Studies P214, R072, R081, P220, P221, S011, S013, S014). The IC50 values are shown in **Table 116**. The calculated R values (**Table 117**), using the estimated unbound steady-state plasma Cmax values of 0.0132\muM for SUZ and 0.124\muM for M6-SUZ, were less than the cutoff value of 1.1, except OAT3, which had an R value of 1.1 for M6-SUZ. These results indicated low systemic DDI potential via inhibition of the transporters. However, due to high concentration of SUZ in the gut (estimated gut concentration of 845\muM), the analysis identified a potential for P-gp inhibition by SUZ in the gut (R value of 95.4). A dedicated clinical study to further evaluate the impact of SUZ on P-gp substrate was conducted (refer to Study 010).

L1247: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1248: The clinical development program of SUZ consisted of 13 phase 1 clinical studies, two phase 2 dose-ranging studies, two pivotal phase 3 efficacy studies, and one phase 3 safety study. The list of clinical studies with PK assessments is shown in Table 118 below.

L1255: ## Study 001 and Study 005 Part A (Single Ascending Dose)
L1256: ## Single Ascending Doses
L1257: Single ascending doses of SUZ ranging from 7.5 mg to 395 mg (solution formulation for doses less than 50 mg or suspension formulation for doses 50 mg and greater) under fasted conditions were evaluated in two studies, Study 001 (7.5 to 65 mg) and Study 005 Part A (90 to 395 mg). Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
L1258: After single dose administration:
L1259: - The highest mean SUZ exposures were 31.1 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (\text{AUC}_{0-\infty}) and 1.11 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (C_{max}), after the 395-mg dose (Table 120). The mean terminal half-life of SUZ ranged from 35.8 to 59.8 hours across the single-dose cohorts.
- The highest mean M6-SUZ exposures achieved were 103 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (\text{AUC}_{0-\infty}) and 1.05 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (C_{max}), after the 395-mg dose (Table 120). The mean terminal half-life of M6-SUZ ranged from 44.1 to 76.7 hours across the single-dose cohorts.
- M6-SUZ exposures (\text{AUC}_{0-\infty}) were approximately 3-fold higher than SUZ. This metabolite ratio for \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} (M6-SUZ/SUZ) was consistent across all doses studied.
L1260: Dose proportionality was assessed statistically using a power model across single oral doses. After single doses of SUZ up to 395 mg, \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased approximately dose-proportionally up to and including 171 mg and increased less than dose-proportionally at higher doses. Exposures were therefore approximately dose-proportional through the highest
L1261: single dose (first dose) in the dosing regimen (100 mg). C_{max} of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased less than dose-proportionally across the range of doses tested.

L1273: ## **Study 002 Part A and Study 005 Part B (Multiple Ascending Dose)**
L1274: ## **Multiple Ascending Doses**
L1275: Multiple ascending doses of SUZ (solution/suspension formulation) ranging from 5 mg to 178 mg once daily (qd) for 14 days under fasted conditions were evaluated in two studies, Study 002 part A (5 to 65 mg qd) and Study 005 part B (90 mg and 178 mg qd). A solution formulation was used for doses less than 50 mg, and a suspension formulation was used for doses 50 mg and larger. One cohort in Study 005 evaluated a loading dose of 115 mg followed by 57.5 mg q12h. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected on Day 1 (rich samples, 0 to 24 hours), Day 10 (rich samples, 0 to 24h), Day 14 (rich samples, 0 to 96 hours) and on Days 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 11 (Study 002 only), 12, and 13 (predose, for trough levels).
L1276: The mean SUZ accumulation ratio was consistent through 65 mg qd (mean range: 1.65 to 1.98). The mean accumulation ratio decreased at higher doses (mean range: 1.34 to 1.41).
L1277: After multiple dose administration (Table 121 and Table 122):
L1278: - The highest mean SUZ exposures achieved were 12.6 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (\text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} on Day 14) and 1.11 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (C_{\text{max}} on Day 2), at the 178-mg dose.
- The highest mean M6-SUZ exposures achieved were 39.3 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (\text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} on Day 14) and 2.05 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (C_{\text{max}} on Day 14), at the 178-mg dose.
- Across all dose levels, M6-SUZ exposures were approximately 3-fold higher than SUZ exposures on Day 14, consistent with observations from the single-dose studies. The mean M6-SUZ AUC accumulation ratio was 2.94 to 3.75, with no trends by dose.
L1279: Dose proportionality of SUZ and M6-SUZ C_{\text{max}} and \text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} on Day 14 after multiple doses were assessed using a power model across multiple oral doses from 5 to 178 mg. The C_{\text{max}} for cohort B2 (115 mg/57.5 mg q12h) was excluded as the dosing interval was different for this cohort, making the dose-normalized C_{\text{max}} not comparable. After repeated doses of SUZ up to 178 mg qd, both \text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} and C_{\text{max}} of SUZ and M6-SUZ on Day 14 increased approximately dose-proportionally through doses up to 65 mg, and slightly less than dose-proportionally at higher doses. The less-than-dose-proportional increase in exposure was likely due to autoinduction at higher doses.

L1292: ## Trough Levels
L1293: Figure 15 shows the mean (+standard deviation) plasma trough-concentration-time profiles of SUZ and M6-SUZ across 14 days of SUZ dosing in Studies 002 part A and 005 part B. At doses up to 65 mg qd, SUZ and M6-SUZ trough concentrations increased for approximately 10 days, while at higher doses, the trough concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased for approximately 5 days. The appearance of an early plateau (instead of further increases in SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations), may be due to an autoinduction mechanism on SUZ and M6-SUZ clearance. Trough concentrations were higher for the 115 mg/57.5 mg q12h cohort because this cohort was dosed q12h (57.5 mg q12h) while the other cohorts were dosed once daily (Table 123).

L1301: ## Study 004
L1302: Study 004 was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence, DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeated doses of SUZ on the PK of oral contraceptives, levonorgestrel (LN) and ethinyl estradiol (EE) in healthy female subjects. Each subject received SUZ tablets (70 mg qd) for 16 days (Dosing Period 2, Day 4 through Day 19). Each subject received two single doses of LN (150 \mu\text{g})/EE (30 \mu\text{g}) (Dosing Period 1, Day 1 and Dosing Period 2, Day 17). Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of LN and EE were collected up to 72 hours postdose. The PK parameters of LN and EE with and without SUZ are shown in Table 124. Concomitant administration of SUZ with an oral contraceptive (containing EE and LN) did not affect the systemic exposure of LN and EE as measured by AUC and C_{\text{max}} as the 90% CI of all parameters measured were within 80% to 125% limits. (Table 124).

L1308: ## **Study 006 (Mass Balance Study)**
L1309: Study 006 was a phase 1, open-label, mass balance study to evaluate the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion of [^{14}\text{C}]SUZ in healthy male subjects (N=8). Each subject received a single oral dose of 80 mg/200 \mu\text{Ci} [^{14}\text{C}]SUZ capsules. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ, M6-SUZ, M3-SUZ, and M12-SUZ were collected up to 264 hours postdose.
L1310: The results show that of the administered dose of [^{14}\text{C}]SUZ (80 mg/200 \mu\text{Ci}), 94% of the radioactivity was recovered, out of which 44.0% and 49.9% were recovered in urine and feces, respectively, primarily as metabolites. The details are provided below.
L1311: - Of the 49.9% of the dose excreted in feces, unchanged SUZ was 9.1%, unchanged M6-SUZ was &lt;0.9%, and the remainder was other metabolites.
- Of the 44% of the dose excreted in urine, the majority was metabolites. Unchanged SUZ and unchanged M6-SUZ in urine were below the limit of quantitation.
- In plasma, the median time to maximum concentration (T_{\text{max}}) of unchanged SUZ was 2 hours. The mean half-life of SUZ was 48.1 hours. Total radioactivity (TRA) in plasma had a median T_{\text{max}} of 2.75 hours and mean half-life of 57.8 hours (Table 125).
- The mean plasma apparent clearance and apparent volume of distribution of unchanged SUZ were 9.08 L/h and 584 L, respectively.
- In plasma, unchanged SUZ \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} was 19.9% of TRA \text{AUC}_{0-\infty}, indicating that the majority of circulating exposure was driven by metabolites. M6-SUZ \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} was 63.6% of TRA \text{AUC}_{0-\infty}. There were no other major circulating metabolites of SUZ.
- Mean TRA \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} blood to plasma ratio was 0.562, indicating no preferential distribution of SUZ into blood components.
L1312: Based on this study, it is noted that SUZ was primarily eliminated by oxidative metabolism in humans, as the mean unchanged SUZ recovered in urine and feces accounted for less than 10% of the administered radioactive dose.

L1318: ## **Study 008 (DDI - SUZ With Itraconazole)**
L1319: SUZ and M6-SUZ are CYP3A substrates. Study 008 was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence, crossover, single-cohort, DDI study to evaluate the effect of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ in healthy subjects. The study design of Study 008 is shown in Table 126. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
L1320: **Table 126. Study Design, Study 008**

L1327: Coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole increased the AUC_{0-\infty} of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 4.79-fold (90% CI: 4.28, 5.35) and 4.42-fold (90% CI: 3.64, 5.37), respectively. The upper bound of the 90% CI is greater than 5; therefore, SUZ and M6-SUZ can be considered as sensitive substrates of CYP3A. The coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole resulted in an increase of SUZ C_{max} by 1.47-fold and a 31.7% decrease in the C_{max} of M6-SUZ (Table 127). These results indicate that CYP3A is the main enzyme involved in metabolism for both SUZ and M6-SUZ. Based on the significant increase in SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure, concomitant use of SUZ with strong CYP3A inhibitors is contraindicated.

L1334: ## **Study 009 (QT/QTc)**
L1335: Study 009 was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled, thorough QT/QTc (QT corrected for heart rate) study of SUZ in healthy subjects. The subjects were randomized 2:1:1 to one of three groups (Groups 1, 2A, or 2B) as shown in Figure 16. Subjects received a total of 11 days of double-blinded study drug treatment, starting with a single dose of SUZ-matching placebo on Day 1. Group 1 received supratherapeutic doses of SUZ (200 mg qd for 7 days) on Days 4 through 10. Groups 2A and 2B followed a nested crossover design for moxifloxacin and placebo, receiving a single 400 mg dose of moxifloxacin on either Day 2 (Group 2A) or Day 11 (Group 2B). All groups received SUZ-matching placebo on Day 3 to minimize the effects of moxifloxacin carryover in Group 2A. To maintain the blind, groups that did not receive active SUZ on Days 4 through 10 received SUZ-matching placebo, and groups that did not receive active moxifloxacin on Days 2 or 11 received moxifloxacin-matching placebo.
L1336: **Figure 16. Study Design, Study 009**
Study Design for Treatment Period
L1337: Source: Adapted from Clinical Study Report VX21-548-009, Figure 2-1
Notes: To maintain the blind, matching placebo was administered, as applicable, so that all subjects received the same number of tablets or capsules. On Day 1, continuous ECGs were extracted to establish baseline values for cardiodynamic analyses; PK blood samples were not collected. On other days indicated by brackets, time-matched PK and continuous ECG assessments were both performed.
Abbreviations: ECG, electrocardiogram; Moxi, moxifloxacin; N, total sample size; PBO, placebo; PK, pharmacokinetic; qd, once daily
L1338: The SUZ median T_{max} was 3 to 4 hours, with mean exposures ranging (C_{max} to C_{min} [minimum plasma concentration]) from 1.27 to 0.407 \mug/mL on Day 6, and from 1.19 to 0.439 \mug/mL on Day 10 after 200-mg qd doses of SUZ on Days 4 through 10. The M6-SUZ median T_{max} was 4 hours, with mean exposures ranging (C_{max} to C_{min}) from 1.71 to 1.15 \mug/mL on Day 6, and from 2.19 to 1.58 \mug/mL on Day 10 after 200-mg qd doses of SUZ on Days 4 through 10 (Table 128).
L1339: Based on concentration-QTc analysis, an effect on placebo-corrected \Delta QTcF (corrected QT interval, Fridericia's calculation) exceeding 10 msec can be excluded within the full observed range of SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations up to ~2.16 \mug/mL and 3.0 \mug/mL, respectively. Refer to the QT review for concentration-QTc analysis.

L1344: ## **Study 010 (DDI - SUZ With Midazolam and Digoxin)**
L1345: Study 010 was a phase 1, open-label, 2-period, fixed-sequence, crossover, DDI study to evaluate the effects of SUZ at a dose of 50 mg q12h on the PK of midazolam (CYP3A substrate) and digoxin (P-gp substrate) in healthy subjects. The treatment period (Days 1 to 24) included 2 dosing periods (Table 129). In both dosing periods, doses were administered following a 2-hour fast. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected up to 24 hours postdose for midazolam and 96 hours postdose for digoxin.

L1352: In this study there was a difference in sample number between number of subjects enrolled and subjects excluded. In the response to an information request sent to the Applicant in this regard, the Applicant stated that:
L1353: During study conduct, the number of subjects to be enrolled was increased to approximately 35 subjects due to important protocol deviations relating to PK sampling that led midazolam and/or digoxin PK data from 17 subjects to be unevaluable with PK sampling taken outside of acceptable window. Because midazolam and digoxin were administered 1 hour apart to subjects in the study, most subjects had exclusions for both.
L1354: Since noncompartmental analysis (NCA) uses the actual time points, this reviewer conducted additional analysis including all evaluable subjects and sponsor-excluded subjects. The reviewer's analysis showed no difference in the outcome in the fold changes compared to the Applicant's analysis. Therefore, the Applicant's analysis is acceptable (Table 130).
L1355: Coadministration of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) with SUZ after 12 daily doses of SUZ at 50 mg q12h results in ~48.2% and 36.7% decrease in midazolam AUC_{0-\infty} and C_{max}, respectively, indicating that SUZ is a weak-to-moderate inducer of CYP3A (Table 130). Based on the results of this evaluation, for sensitive CYP3A substrates, the review team included the following language in the label:
L1356: Avoid concomitant use of JOURNAVX with CYP3A substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to loss of efficacy unless otherwise recommended in the Prescribing Information for CYP3A substrates. When using JOURNAVX with sensitive CYP3A substrates, consider increasing the dose of substrate drugs based on their Prescribing Information if needed or use alternative medications.
L1357: Digoxin exposures were comparable when it was administered alone and in the presence of SUZ at 12 daily doses of SUZ at 50 mg q12h, indicating that SUZ is not an inducer or inhibitor of P-gp.

L1363: ## **Study 013 (DDI - SUZ With Omeprazole and Rifampin)**
L1364: Study 013 was a phase 1, two-part, open-label, fixed-sequence DDI study to evaluate the effects of omeprazole (part A) and rifampin (part B) on the PK of SUZ (100 mg) in healthy subjects. All study drugs were administered under fasted conditions. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
L1365: Omeprazole (40 mg qd for 4 days) did not affect the oral absorption or decrease the systemic exposure of SUZ. Relative to SUZ (100 mg) administered alone, coadministration of SUZ and omeprazole resulted in a 28% increase in AUC_{0-\infty}, a 4.5% decrease in C_{max}, and no change in T_{max} for SUZ. Mean SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations with and without omeprazole were similar over the first 6 hours postdose. M6-SUZ PK was not affected by omeprazole and the exposure change was less than 20% (Table 131).

L1374: Relative to SUZ (100 mg) administered alone, when administered with rifampin (CYP3A inducer, 600 mg qd), the SUZ AUC0-\infty was decreased by 92.7% and Cmax by 79.5%. The M6-SUZ AUC0-\infty decreased by 84.7% (Table 132). Rifampin decreased SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure (AUC0-\infty) by more than 80%, indicating that SUZ is a sensitive CYP3A substrate. Based on the observed decrease in exposure of SUZ, avoiding concomitant use of SUZ with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers is recommended. For the impact of moderate and weak CYP3A inducers on SUZ, refer to Section 14.7 Q4.

L1382: ## Study 007 (Hepatic Impairment)
L1383: Study 007 was a phase 1, open-label, parallel-group study evaluating the PK, safety, and tolerability of SUZ in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) or moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) hepatic impairment (HI), compared to healthy subjects matched by sex, age, and weight. The
L1384: PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was not studied in severe hepatic impairment subjects. In all treatments, subjects were administered SUZ q12h in the fasted state on Days 1 through 14. Subjects with mild HI and matched healthy subjects received 50 mg SUZ. Subjects with moderate HI and matched healthy subjects received 20 mg SUZ. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 408 hours postdose.
L1385: Subjects with mild hepatic impairment had decreased SUZ AUC_{0-12h} (8.6%) and C_{max} (10.5%) and decreased M6-SUZ AUC_{0-12h} (17.3%) and C_{max} (17.3%) compared to matched healthy subjects (Table 133).
L1386: Subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had 1.50-fold increased SUZ AUC_{0-12h} and 1.31-fold increased SUZ C_{max}, while M6-SUZ AUC_{0-12h} and C_{max} both increased 1.15-fold compared to matched healthy subjects. SUZ and M6-SUZ had higher accumulation ratios and longer half-lives in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared to matched healthy subjects and subjects with mild hepatic impairment (Table 133).
L1387: The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent across all time points and was similar between subjects with HI and healthy subjects for SUZ (mean range: 0.896% to 1.07%) and M6-SUZ (mean range: 3.64% to 4.16%).

L1395: ## **Study 014 (Renal Impairment)**
L1396: Study 014 was a phase 1, open-label study to evaluate the PK and safety of single doses of SUZ in subjects with severe (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] 15 to 29 mL/min, estimated by the chronic kidney disease epidemiology collaboration equation with adjustment for body surface area) or moderate renal impairment (eGFR 30 to 59 mL/min) and in matched healthy subjects. The severe- and moderate-impairment subjects were matched by sex, age, and body mass index (BMI) to healthy subjects. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 312 hours postdose. Subjects with moderate and severe renal impairment showed clinically insignificant changes in the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ. The details are shown below.
L1397: Moderate renal impairment versus healthy subjects:
L1398: - Subjects with moderate renal impairment had SUZ AUC_{0-\infty} increased by 21% and C_{max} decreased by 19%, and M6-SUZ AUC_{0-\infty} increased by 3% and C_{max} decreased by 24% compared to matched healthy subjects. The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent across all time points and was similar between subjects with moderate renal impairment and matched healthy subjects for SUZ (&lt;1%) and M6-SUZ (3.7%) (Table 134).
L1399: Severe renal impairment versus healthy subjects:
L1400: - Subjects with severe renal impairment had SUZ AUC_{0-\infty} increased by 17% and C_{max} increased by 12%, and M6-SUZ AUC_{0-\infty} increased by 13% and C_{max} increased by 2% compared to matched healthy subjects. The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent across all time points and was similar between subjects with severe renal impairment and matched healthy subjects for SUZ (&lt;1%) and M6-SUZ (~3.4%) (Table 135).

L1415: ## **Food Effect Studies**
L1416: The Applicant conducted two food effect studies, Studies 011 and 016. Study 011 evaluated the effect of a high-fat meal on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ. Study 016 evaluated various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat) and timings of meal administration (60 minutes predose, at 0h, and 60 minutes postdose) on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK.
L1417: ## **Study 011 (Relative Bioavailability and 1st Food Effect Study)**
L1418: Study 011 was a phase 1, open-label, randomized, crossover study to evaluate 1) the relative bioavailability of the to-be-marketed (TBM) SUZ 50-mg tablet compared to phase 2 formulation, SUZ 10-mg tablet, at a dose of 100 mg, in the fasted state; and 2) food effect on SUZ 50-mg tablet at a dose of 100 mg in healthy adult subjects. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
L1419: Relative bioavailability: at the 100-mg dose, the SUZ 50-mg tablets (TBM) showed bioequivalence to 10-mg tablets (phase 2 formulation) in terms of AUC0-∞ for SUZ, while the Cmax was ~15% lower with lower 90% CI value marginally outside bioequivalence limits. M6-SUZ showed bioequivalence to both AUC0-∞ and Cmax. The SUZ Tmax was unchanged between the two formulations (3 hours), while 50-mg tablets showed delayed M6-SUZ Tmax (10 hours) versus 10-mg tablets (5 hours). Since the dosing regimen of JOURNAVX is a 100-mg loading dose followed by a 50 mg twice daily (b) (4) the minor differences in PK between the TBM formulation and phase 2 formulation are not considered clinically relevant (Table 136). In addition, the phase 3 studies (104 and 105) were conducted with the TBM 50-mg tablet, which achieved statistical significance for the primary efficacy endpoint in comparison to placebo for the treatment of acute pain.

L1426: Administration of SUZ with a high-fat meal at the time of dose (30 minutes for meal consumption) did not affect the Cmax and AUC in comparison to fasted conditions for both SUZ and its metabolite; however, it resulted in a longer Tmax (from 3 hours to 5 hours for SUZ and from 10 hours to 24 hours for M6-SUZ) compared to fasted conditions (Table 137). Although Cmax and AUC after a high-fat meal were not affected, in an acute-pain setting, systemic exposures at early time points following the first dose are important to ensure rapid onset of pain relief. Hence the impact of food on the PK of SUZ and its metabolite was further analyzed by the reviewer as a part of this review.

L1433: ## **Study 016 (2nd Food Effect Study)**
L1434: Study 016 was a phase 1, randomized, open-label, crossover study evaluating the effect of food on the PK of SUZ in healthy adults. It evaluated various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and
L1435: high-fat) and timings of meal administration (90 minutes predose, 30 minutes predose, and 60 minutes postdose) on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK.
L1436: The Study was evaluated in the following three parts.
L1437: - Part A: a low-fat and a moderate-fat meal at 0h4 (30 min predose) versus fasted condition
- Part B: a high-fat meal at 60 minutes predose4 (90 min predose, including 30 minutes for meal consumption) and 60 min postdose versus fasted condition
- Part C: a low-fat and moderate-fat meal at 60 minutes predose4 (90 min predose, including 30 minutes for meal consumption) versus fasted condition
L1438: The definitions of low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat breakfast are as follows:
L1439: - Low-fat breakfast: ~11 to 14 g of fat and 400 to 500 total calories with ~25% of calories from fat
- Moderate-fat breakfast: ~20 g of fat and 600 total calories with ~30% of calories from fat
- High-fat breakfast: ~55 to 65 g of fat and 800 to 1000 total calories with ~50% of calories
L1440: Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 336 hours postdose. The various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat) and timings of meal administration (60 minutes predose, at 0h, and 60 minutes postdose) did not affect the Cmax or AUC. However, the median Tmax was delayed, for SUZ or M6-SUZ (Table 138, Table 139, and Table 140). Since SUZ is being developed for an acute pain indication, the attainment of sufficient concentrations in the earlier time points is expected for the onset of pain relief. Refer to the reviewer's analysis of food effect based on Studies 011 and 016 below.

L1469: ## Reviewer Analysis of Food Effect
L1470: SUZ is for an acute pain indication; hence, the attainment of sufficient initial concentrations after dosing is expected for the onset of pain relief. In the phase 3 studies of SUZ (Studies 104, 105 and 107), the first dose (100 mg) was administered under fasting conditions and subsequent doses were administered without regard to food. The median time to onset of perceptible pain relief in abdominoplasty (Study 105) was 34 minutes, while in bunionectomy (Study 104), time to onset was 60 minutes.(b) (4)
L1471: The Applicant conducted two food-effect studies, 011 and 016. Study 011 evaluated the effect of a high-fat meal on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ versus fasted state. Study 016 evaluated various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat) and timings of meal administration (60 minutes predose, at 0h, and 60 minutes postdose4) on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK versus fasted state.
L1472: The effect of food on the first dose of 100 mg and the subsequent doses of 50 mg from 12 hours onwards was evaluated separately.(b) (4)
L1473: ## First Dose With Regard to Food
L1474: To evaluate the effect of administering SUZ after consuming low-, moderate-, and high-fat meals at 0h (allowing 30 minutes for meal consumption) in comparison to the fasted state, the plasma concentration-time profiles of SUZ in the initial hours (0 to 6 hours) were compared [Figure 17 Panel A (Study 011), and Panel B (Study 016, Part A)].
L1475: There was a considerable decrease in SUZ concentrations at the initial time points after dosing with moderate-fat meals and high-fat meals (Figure 17). The individual PK profiles after a high-fat meal versus fasted condition and moderate-fat (standard-fat) meal versus fasted condition are shown in Figure 18 and Figure 19, respectively. This decrease in initial concentrations was well-represented in individual-level PK data, wherein multiple subjects showed very low concentrations for the first 2 to 3 hours postdose in the fed state versus fasted state (Figure 18 and Figure 19). Individual profile data showing low early concentrations in the fed state raised concerns about potential delay in onset of pain relief. The difference in exposure between fed and fasted states for various meals was further evaluated using the partial area under the concentration-time curve (pAUC) ratios of low-, moderate-, and high-fat meals versus fasted conditions. In comparison to the fasted state, taking SUZ with high-fat meals or moderate-fat meals resulted in considerable decreases in SUZ pAUCs for up to 2 to 3 hours postdose, while for the low-fat meals, the exposure was low for up to 1 hour postdose (Figure 20). These results can also be seen when one evaluates differences in T_{max}.
L1476: For SUZ 100 mg, both high-fat and moderate-fat meals resulted in delays in T_{max} of similar magnitude (Table 137 and Table 138); however, the Applicant only suggested to avoid high-fat meals for the first dose. The high-fat and moderate-fat meals delayed the median T_{max} of SUZ from 3 hours in the fasted state to 5 hours in the fed state, and M6-SUZ from 8 to 10 hours in the fasted state to 24 hours in the fed state. The C_{max} and AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not affected by any of the meal conditions (Table 137, Table 138, Table 139, and Table 140).

L1489: ## Administration of Subsequent Doses, i.e., 2nd Dose With Regard to Food
L1490: The Applicant proposed that after the first dose of 100 mg, the subsequent doses of 50 mg (doses at 12h, 24h, 36h...) can be taken without regard to meals (the same conditions employed in the pivotal phase 3 trials that provided evidence of effectiveness). Since the first dose of SUZ has considerable food effect, the food effect on the second dose, 50 mg at 12h, was evaluated under a high-fat meal condition in comparison to a fasted condition. Since there are no direct data for this evaluation, and SUZ follows linear kinetics at a 50-mg dose, nonparametric superposition simulations were conducted using the Study 011 data. For these simulations, the first dose of 100 mg was administered in a fasting state and the second dose of 50 mg at 12h was administered in two scenarios: 1) under fasting conditions (reference) and 2) under fed conditions (test). The SUZ and M6-SUZ PK profiles of these simulations are shown in Figure 21 (reference [red triangles]: 100-mg first dose at 0 hours under fasting plus 50 mg at 12 hours under fasting; test [blue circles]: 100-mg first dose under fasting at 0 hours plus 50 mg at 12 hours under high-fat meals). From the simulated PK profiles, it can be noted that for the second dose (50 mg at 12 hours), the PK profiles (12 to 24 hours) are not different when taken with or without food. In addition, it can be noted that at 12 hours postdose, there are sufficient trough concentrations from the first dose (100 mg) on top of which the second dose of 50 mg at 12 hours is administered.
L1491: Further, the partial areas of the predicted PK profiles were evaluated (Table 141 and Table 142). There were no differences in predicted SUZ pAUCs, AUC_{0-12h}, or AUC_{0-24h} between test and reference arms. The SUZ partial areas of the second dose, pAUC_{12-14h} and pAUC_{12-16h}, were 15% lower under high-fat meals, which is not considered significant. There was no difference in C_{max} values after the second dose between test and reference arms. The median T_{max} after the second dose in the reference arm was 15h, while median T_{max} in the test arm was 17h. Similar to SUZ, there was no difference in partial areas or C_{max} between test and reference arms for M6-SUZ. The median T_{max} of M6-SUZ after the second dose in the reference arm was 22h, while median T_{max} in the test arm was 24h (Table 141 and Table 142).

L1506: ## Recommendation for Food Effect
L1507: Based on these analyses, the review team does not agree with the Applicant's proposal of (b) (4) of SUZ.
L1508: The review team's labeling recommendation for the first dose of JOURNAVX is, "Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without food." Taking the first dose with food may result in delayed onset of pain relief.
L1509: ## Phase 2 Studies With PK
L1510: ## Study 101 (Phase 2 Bunionectionomy)
L1511: Study 101 was a phase 2, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging study evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ for acute pain after a bunionectionomy. Subjects were randomized 2:2:1:2:2 to 5 treatment groups:
L1512: - SUZ 100 mg loading dose (LD)/50 mg q12h for 36h (N= 54)
- SUZ 60 mg LD /30 mg q12h for 36h (N= 54)
- SUZ 20 mg LD /10 mg q12h for 36h (N= 27)
- Hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen (HB/APAP) 5/325 q6h for 36h (N= 54)
- Placebo (N= 54)
L1513: Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 48 hours postdose.

L1535: ## **Comparison of PK Parameters of Suzetrigine and M6-SUZ Between Bunionectomy (Phase 2 Study 101) and Abdominoplasty (Phase 2 Study 102)**
L1536: The PK parameters in abdominoplasty and bunionectomy on Day 1 and Day 2 after administration of SUZ 100-mg loading dose/50 mg q12h for 36h are shown in Table 145.
L1537: On Day 1, exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ were lower and T_{max} was delayed in the abdominoplasty subjects compared to the bunionectomy subjects. On Day 1, the median SUZ T_{max} was approximately 2 hours in bunionectomy, while it was approximately 6 hours in the abdominoplasty. For doses administered after the first dose, exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ
L1538: were similar between abdominoplasty and bunionectomy. On Day 2, median SUZ T_{max} is similar between bunionectomy and abdominoplasty (approximately 2 hours). The slower absorption observed after the first dose in abdominoplasty is likely due to decreased gastrointestinal motility postoperatively due to abdominal surgery. This difference in exposure between the two surgical procedures did not result in a clinically meaningful difference in efficacy, as the primary efficacy endpoint was met for both procedures.

L1549: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1550: For the determination of plasma SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations, the Applicant used a liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometric detection (LC-MS/MS) method. The Applicant submitted bioanalytical method life cycle information to support SUZ and M6-SUZ quantification in human plasma from various clinical studies (validation reports Q136, Q137, Q222, R116, and T411).
L1551: To quantify SUZ and M6-SUZ from plasma samples, a 50-\muL aliquot of plasma sample is fortified with 50-\muL stable labeled internal standard working solution. The analytes are isolated through liquid/liquid extraction. The solvent is evaporated under nitrogen, then reconstituted with acetonitrile:water (50:50, v/v). The final extract is analyzed via high-performance liquid chromatography with MS/MS detection using positive ion electrospray. The concentration of the samples was determined from a linear regression of the standard curve with 1/x^2 weighting. This method was developed
L1552: A summary of the validation parameters is presented in Table 146.

L1560: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1561: This section is a review of Applicant's pharmacometrics analyses including: (1) population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis of SUZ and its major metabolite, M6-SUZ; (2) exposure-response (E-R) analyses; (3) dose adjustment for moderate hepatic impairment; and (4) dose adjustment for coadministration of moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
L1562: ## 14.5.1. Applicant's Analyses
L1563: ## PopPK Analyses
L1564: ## Objectives
L1565: To characterize the PK disposition of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
L1566: ## Data
L1567: The popPK analyses were conducted using plasma concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ from studies 001, 002, 005, 007, 008, 011, 013, 016, 101, 102, 104, and 105. A brief description of these studies is provided in Table 147.
L1568: The PK data consisted of 19,788 evaluable concentrations of SUZ and 19,788 evaluable concentrations of M6-SUZ from 1,435 subjects, including healthy volunteers, subjects with HI, and subjects treated for postsurgical pain.
L1569: The baseline demographics for the continuous and categorical variables for these subjects are provided in Table 148.

L1581: ## Method
L1582: Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was used to characterize the time course of SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations using NONMEM® 7.5. All data manipulations, summarizations, and graphical outputs were performed using R® (version 4.1.1 or higher).
L1583: The base population PK model was developed from prior modeling results using a two-compartment model for SUZ and a three-compartment model for M6-SUZ. The prior model was parameterized for SUZ and M6-SUZ, including clearance (CLP, CLM), volumes of distribution (V2P, V2M, V3P, V3M, V4M), and intercompartmental clearances (Q3P, Q3M, Q4M). Absorption was modeled using both zero- and first-order processes, with a lag time (ALAG) from the depot compartment and relative bioavailability (F1).
L1584: In abdominoplasty subjects, reduced F1 and slower absorption were observed following the first dose; therefore, a separate absorption model was estimated for these subjects. This model included an estimated time (MTIME) at which the reduced absorption parameters—early absorption rate (KAD), early absorption duration (D1D), and reduced bioavailability (F1D)—transitioned to the standard absorption parameters (KA, D1, F1) used for all other subjects. Dose-dependent absorption was modeled by including a dose-level covariate on KA, D1, and F1.
L1585: The base model was chosen after evaluating various compartmental structures with different parameterizations for absorption (including first- and zero-order, dose-dependent absorption, and bioavailability) and autoinduction (using linear, maximum effect [Emax], and sigmoidal Emax models).
L1586: Interindividual variability was modeled as multiplicative exponential random effects, and residual variability was assessed using a combined error model with both additive and proportional error, including a logarithmic transformation of concentrations due to the large range of SUZ/M6-SUZ concentrations.
L1587: Covariates were assessed for inclusion in the final model using stepwise covariate modeling, with multiplicative effects on population parameters. Body weight was used to allometrically scale PK parameters based on graphical assessment of individual PK parameters and body size (weight and BMI) before stepwise covariate modeling analysis. A stepwise covariate selection (forward p-value of 0.05 or \DeltaOFV of 3.84, backward p-value of 0.001 or \DeltaOFV of 10.83) was conducted to assess statistical significance, and covariates were selected based on biological plausibility, diagnostic plots, and statistical tests. The effects of age, baseline BMI, baseline eGFR, sex, and race on CLP, and sex and race on V2P, V2M, V3P, and V3M were tested. After covariate inclusion, model refinement was performed based on goodness-of-fit diagnostics, standard errors of parameter estimates, and clinical relevance of the covariate effects.
L1588: ## Results
L1589: The population PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ were best described in a joint model with SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations modeled simultaneously (Figure 24). SUZ disposition included zero-order depot entry, lag time, first-order absorption, distribution, and nonlinear clearance due to autoinduction. M6-SUZ followed first-order formation from SUZ, distribution, and clearance with autoinduction. A second M6-SUZ peripheral compartment was excluded for simplicity. SUZ metabolism to M6-SUZ was fixed at 80%, based on nonclinical data.
L1590: Covariates in the model included the effects of body weight on the distribution volumes of SUZ and M6-SUZ and effects of BMI and sex on SUZ clearance.
L1591: The population PK parameter estimates from the final PK models are provided in Table 149. The PK models adequately characterize the observed data, as shown in Figure 25, Figure 26, Figure 27, and Figure 28.

L1627: The Applicant conducted simulations using the final popPK model to predict exposures over the first 48 hours and at steady state (Day 30) following administration of SUZ 100 mg/50 mg q12h and to compare to prior clinical experience. Over the first 48 hours, differences in absorption parameters (relative bioavailability) for the first dose following an abdominoplasty result in slightly lower predicted exposures for abdominoplasty than for bunionectomy. At steady state, the predicted exposures for abdominoplasty and bunionectomy are similar (within 2%) and therefore a pooled analysis was conducted. A summary of key PK parameters and properties for the proposed dosing regimen is provided in Table 150.

L1636: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; CV, coefficient of variation; CYP, cytochrome P450, NA, not applicable; q12h, every 12 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine; Tmax, time to maximum plasma concentration
L1637: ## Exposure-Response Analysis
L1638: ## Objectives
L1639: To evaluate the correlation between the PK exposure of SUZ and its active metabolite M6-SUZ with the efficacy endpoint (time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the numerical pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours [SPID48] response) in subjects administered SUZ after abdominoplasty or bunionectomy.
L1640: ## Data
L1641: PK data (individual concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ) and efficacy response (SPID48) from phase 2 (VX21-548-101 and VX21-548-102) and phase 3 (VX22-548-104 and VX22-548-105) studies in subjects who underwent bunionectomy or abdominoplasty were used to develop the E-R models.
L1642: A total of 1,725 subjects who underwent abdominoplasty (N=875) or bunionectomy (N=850) were included in the E-R analysis. Of these 1,725 subjects, 574 received placebo, 33 were administered a loading dose of 20 mg SUZ followed by 10 mg SUZ every 12 hours, 134 received a loading dose of 60 mg SUZ followed by 30 mg SUZ every 12 hours, and 984 were dosed with a loading dose of 100 mg SUZ followed by 50 mg SUZ every 12 hours. The covariate characteristics of the data are provided in Table 151.
L1643: **Table 151. Summary Statistics of Continuous and Categorical Covariates for Subjects Undergoing Abdominoplasty or Bunionectomy**
L1644: Continuous Covariates	Total Number of Patients (N=1725)
Baseline NPRS	
Mean (SD)	7.04 (1.79)
Median [Min, Max]	7.00 [4.00, 10.0]
Baseline BMI (kg/m2)	
Mean (SD)	28.7 (4.49)
Median [Min, Max]	28.6 [18.1, 41.5]

Median [Min, Max]	44.0 [18.0, 75.0]

L1645: Source: The Applicant's popPK report (U058), page 49, Table 26.
L1646: Abbreviations: BMI, body mass index; N, number of subjects; NPRS, numeric pain rating scale score; SD, standard deviation; popPK, population pharmacokinetic
L1647: ## Methods
L1648: The endpoint evaluated in the E-R analysis was SPID48. The E-R dataset consisted of mean SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations, along with SPID48, for 875 subjects who underwent an abdominoplasty and 850 subjects who underwent a bunionectomy.
L1649: Several structural forms of the E-R model were evaluated to characterize the E-R relationship for SUZ. Linear, power, and sigmoidal E-R model structures were investigated to describe the relationship between SPID48 response and SUZ/M6-SUZ mean concentrations over 48 hours.
L1650: Baseline NPRS, baseline BMI, race, age, sex, and type of surgery (abdominoplasty versus bunionectomy) were evaluated as covariates.
L1651: ## Results
L1652: A power model was identified as the most suitable structural model. Models were also developed for the abdominoplasty and bunionectomy datasets individually. The results were similar to those obtained when estimating a single E-R relationship for SUZ using the combined abdominoplasty and bunionectomy dataset.
L1653: Parameter estimates for the final E-R model are presented in Table 152. Baseline NPRS was identified as the only clinically relevant covariate. Age was also found to be a significant covariate; however, its effect on efficacy (SPID48) was small and not clinically meaningful (&lt;20%, Figure 31), with slightly higher SPID48 values in older subjects.
L1654: Mean predictions from the E-R models are overlaid on individual observed SPID48 values from Studies 101, 102, 104, and 105 in Figure 32. The largest SPID48 response was predicted at the
L1655: exposures resulting from the 100 mg/50 mg q12h dose, with a consistent response predicted across the entire exposure range at this dose, supporting its selection as the proposed dosing regimen.
L1656: **Table 152. Parameter Estimates of Final Power Model for Subjects Undergoing an Abdominoplasty or a Bunionectomy**
L1657: Final Power model: SPID48 = E_0 \cdot bnprs^{g1} \cdot age^{g2} + C[g \cdot (bnprs^{g3})]

L1668: ## Dose Adjustment for Special Populations
L1669: ## Objective
L1670: To evaluate the appropriateness of adjusting the SUZ dosage in subjects with HI, coadministered a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, or missing doses.
L1671: ## Method
L1672: The Applicant simulated SUZ and M6-SUZ exposures using the phase 3 popPK model (Report U049) based on data from healthy volunteers and phase 2/3 acute pain studies.
L1673: To predict exposure ranges in acute pain patients receiving the phase 3 SUZ regimen (100-mg loading dose followed by 50 mg q12h), the model simulated a typical subject and 1,000 virtual subjects. Results were compared to simulations in subjects with hepatic impairment and those coadministered SUZ with a CYP3A inhibitor, using both phase 3 and reduced SUZ dosing regimens.
L1674: ## Simulation of SUZ PK in Subjects With HI
L1675: The Applicant modified the phase 3 popPK model to simulate subjects with HI. A separate popPK model (Report U036) estimated the relative bioavailability ratio of SUZ and SUZ/M6-SUZ clearances in subjects with moderate HI compared to matched healthy subjects (Table 153). These parameters were then integrated into the phase 3 model to simulate subjects with moderate HI.
L1676: **Table 153. Scaling Parameters and Demographic Covariates of Different Simulation Populations, Hepatic Impairment**

Acute Pain	1.00	1.00	1.00	47.71	28.02	75.54	14.80
Mild Hepatic Impairment	0.90	1.10	1.01	61.68	26.19	74.90	49.80

L1680: Abbreviations: BMI, body mass index; DDI, drug-drug interaction; HI, hepatic impairment; popPK, population pharmacokinetic; SUZ, suzetrigine
L1681: ## Simulations of SUZ PK in Subjects Coadministered SUZ and a Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor
L1682: The Applicant modified the phase 3 popPK model to simulate subjects coadministered SUZ and a CYP3A inhibitor. As the effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK was not clinically studied, a physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model was developed to predict this effect. The PBPK model predicted the relative bioavailability ratio of SUZ in subjects coadministered SUZ and a CYP3A inhibitor to that of subjects receiving SUZ alone (Report T415), as well as the ratios of SUZ and M6-SUZ clearances in these subjects (Table 154). These parameters were then incorporated into the phase 3 popPK model to simulate coadministration of SUZ and a CYP3A inhibitor in subjects with acute pain.

L1688: ## Simulation of Missed-Doses Scenarios
L1689: The Applicant conducted simulations using the final model to evaluate the impact of missed doses on SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure in subjects administered the standard SUZ regimen and in special-population subjects administered a reduced SUZ dose (100-mg loading dose, 50 mg q12h for the next 3 doses, followed by 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses). Simulations of the standard-SUZ-regimen scenarios were performed where one or two doses were missed, and a make-up dose (50 mg or 100 mg) was taken with the next scheduled dose. A 50-mg dose was given for one missed dose, and a 100-mg dose for more than one missed.
L1690: ## Results
L1691: ## Hepatic Impairment
L1692: The Applicant conducted a dedicated study (Study 007) to evaluate the PK and safety of multiple doses of SUZ in subjects with mild or moderate hepatic impairment, as well as in matched healthy subjects, refer to Section 14.2. Based on the increased SUZ exposure, a reduced dose is recommended in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment: 100 mg for the initial (loading) dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next 3 doses, then 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses. This adjusted regimen will result in SUZ exposure levels similar to those in subjects without hepatic impairment. However, M6-SUZ exposures are lower in subjects with hepatic impairment (Figure 33 and Figure 34).

L1701: ## Coadministered SUZ and Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor
L1702: The effects of moderate CYP3A inhibitors fluconazole, erythromycin, and verapamil on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ were predicted using PBPK modeling (refer to Section 14.7). Based on the predicted increase in SUZ exposure when coadministered with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, a reduced SUZ dosing regimen is recommended: 100 mg for the initial (loading) dose followed by 50 mg q12h for the next 3 doses, and then 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses. In subjects coadministered a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, this adjusted dose will lead to higher SUZ and M6-SUZ exposures than when no DDI is present, but will not exceed the maximum exposure levels at the highest clinical doses (Figure 35 and Figure 36).

L1711: ## Missed Dose Simulations
L1712: The Applicant conducted simulations for a 50-mg make-up dose following a single missed dose or a 100-mg make-up dose following two or more missed doses for subjects receiving the standard regimen. The worst case was simulated, where the make-up dose was administered together with the next scheduled dose.
L1713: As an example of a missed dosing scenario in the subjects on the standard regimen, Figure 37 illustrates the simulated concentration-time profiles where the scheduled dose(s) at 12 hours (top) or 12 and 24 hours (bottom) were missed, and make-up doses (either 50 mg for one missed dose or 100 mg for two missed doses) were taken at the same time as the next regularly scheduled dose.
L1714: If a dose is missed, it is recommended to take the missed dose as soon as possible and resume the original schedule. If two or more doses are missed, it is recommended to take a loading dose of 100 mg and then resume the original schedule. The Applicant's recommendation for missed dose management is appropriate; refer to Section 14.5.2.

L1725: ## Software
L1726: PopPK model fitting was performed in NONMEM 7.5 and Pirana 2.9.9. Exploratory E-R analysis and plotting were performed in R 4.0.0.
L1727: ## Models
L1728: The reviewer utilized the Applicant's final popPK model for SUZ and M6-SUZ. SUZ disposition was described by a two-compartment model, with zero-order entry into the depot compartment, followed by first-order absorption from the depot compartment into the central compartment, first-order distribution to and from a peripheral compartment, and parallel linear clearances (metabolism and elimination) from the central compartment. The disposition of the major metabolite (M6-SUZ) was described by a two-compartment model, with formation by nonreversible, first-order creation from SUZ, first-order distribution to and from the peripheral compartment, and clearance from its central compartment with nonlinearity due to autoinduction.
L1729: Additionally, the reviewer evaluated the E-R relationship and evaluated the appropriateness of the proposed dose regimen for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, coadministered with a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, or missing doses. Typical-subject simulations were performed to evaluate the impact of those intrinsic or extrinsic factors.
L1730: ## Results
L1731: ## PopPK and E-R Analysis
L1732: The reviewer was able to reproduce the Applicant's popPK model and obtain the E-R analysis results.
L1733: The relationship between SPID48 and average combined SUZ and M6-SUZ concentration is shown in Figure 38. Overall, maximum SPID48 response was observed at the exposures
L1734: associated with the 100 mg/50 mg q12h dosing regimen. This supported the efficacy of SUZ at a dose of 100 mg/50 mg q12h.

L1764: **Table 156 and Table 157** show the comparison of PK exposure metrics across different dose regimens in subjects who underwent bunionectomy or abdominoplasty. The Applicant's proposed adjusted dosing regimen for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment would result in
L1765: approximately a 20% reduction in PK metrics, including a 17% reduction in maximum plasma concentration at steady state (C_{max,ss}), a 22% reduction in trough plasma concentration at steady state (C_{trough,ss}), and a 23% reduction in daily AUC_{ss}. For subjects coadministered a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, the proposed adjusted dose regimen would lead to a 25% increase in C_{max,ss} and 27% increases in both C_{trough,ss} and daily AUC_{ss}. These changes in the PK metrics are unlikely to impact efficacy (Figure 43).
