L1794: ## 14.7. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling
L1795: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's PBPK analyses to predict impact of weak or moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers on the PK of SUZ and its primary metabolite, M6-SUZ.
L1796: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (Reports S313 and T415), modeling files, and modeling files and Response to FDA's Information Request (IR) received on June 28, July 7, July 24, September 10, and October 1, 2024. The reviewers concluded that the final PBPK model can be used to simulate the DDI effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers on the PK of suzetrigine and M6-SUZ.
L1797: The Applicant has developed PBPK models (Reports S313 and T415) for SUZ using Simcyp version 22 software. The PBPK modeling objectives were to 1) predict the impact of hepatic impairment on the steady-state exposure parameters (e.g., AUC0-24 and Cmax) of SUZ and M6-SUZ; 2) predict the impact of weak or moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers on the PK of suzetrigine and its primary metabolite, M6-SUZ.
L1798: The DDI simulation results indicated that:
L1799: - The AUC and Cmax for SUZ increase approximately 3.0- and 1.2-fold, respectively, when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor (such as fluconazole).
- SUZ AUC and Cmax increased approximately 1.5-fold and 1.4-fold, respectively, when an alternative SUZ dosing regimen was implemented to manage the DDI with fluconazole, compared to the default SUZ dosing.
- The AUC and Cmax for M6-SUZ increase approximately 2.6-fold and 0.7-fold, respectively, when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor (such as fluconazole).
- M6-SUZ AUC and Cmax increased approximately 1.2-fold and 1.1-fold, respectively, when an alternative SUZ dosing regimen was implemented to manage the DDI with fluconazole, compared to the default SUZ dosing.
- The AUC and Cmax for SUZ would decrease approximately by 60% and 30%, respectively, when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inducer (such as efavirenz).
L1800: - The AUC and C_{max} for M6-SUZ would decrease approximately 60% and increase approximately 1.3-fold, respectively, when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inducer (such as efavirenz).
- Weak CYP3A inhibitors and weak CYP3A inducers would have minor effects on SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure.
L1801: ## **Background**
L1802: The suzetrigine (JOURNAVX) dosing regimen is a loading dose of 100 mg in a fasted state (b) (4) followed by a maintenance dosing regimen of 50 mg twice daily (BID) (b) (4). Food restriction is not required during the maintenance dosing time frame.
L1803: Apparent volume of distribution based on the terminal phase (V_z/F) of SUZ is 495 L. Plasma protein binding was 99% for SUZ and 96% for M6-SUZ. Both albumin and alpha-1 acid glycoprotein contribute to SUZ and M6-SUZ binding in human plasma. The in vitro blood-to-plasma concentration ratio was approximately 0.6 for both SUZ and M6-SUZ in humans. SUZ is not a substrate for transporters (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3). M6-SUZ is a substrate for P-gp, but not other transporters.
L1804: Following [^{14}C]SUZ oral administration, ~94% of the administered dose was recovered, out of which ~50% was recovered in feces (primarily as metabolites, with 9.1% unchanged parent and &lt;0.9% unchanged M6-SUZ) and ~44% in urine (as metabolites). The mean plasma apparent clearance is 13.9 L/h. Effective half-lives of SUZ and M6-SUZ are 24 hours and 33 hours, respectively. SUZ and M6-SUZ are primarily metabolized by CYP3A, with minor contributions from CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2C8. SUZ was mainly cleared by oxidative metabolism. After a single oral dose of [^{14}C]SUZ, 44.0% and 49.9% of the dose was excreted in urine and feces, respectively.
L1805: Based on in vitro data, SUZ and M6-SUZ are not expected to result in clinically significant inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A enzymes. SUZ decreased the AUC of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) by 48% and C_{max} by 37% (Study 010). Compared to CYP3A, SUZ has lesser potential to induce CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19 based on the in vitro data. SUZ does not induce CYP1A2 according to the in vitro data. SUZ has no significant effect on the PK of levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol.
L1806: A clinical DDI study (Study 010) showed that SUZ did not affect the PK of digoxin (P-gp substrate). Based on in vitro data, SUZ and M6-SUZ do not inhibit transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3 BCRP OAT1, organic cation transporter (OCT) 2, multidrug and toxin extrusion protein [MATE] 1, and MATE2/K).
L1807: The Applicant also used PBPK analysis to assess the effects of hepatic impairment. At the current stage, the application of PBPK analysis to assess the effects of hepatic impairment without clinical data is considered exploratory. The Applicant's PBPK analysis for severe hepatic impairment was not reviewed.
L1808: ## Methods
L1809: ## Model Development
L1810: The PBPK analyses were performed using the PBPK software Simcyp® V22 (Simcyp Ltd., Sheffield, UK).
L1811: The suzetrigine PBPK model was developed based on physicochemical properties and preclinical and clinical PK data. The SUZ 50-mg(b) (4) tablet is an immediate-release tablet with a(b) (4) film coating(b) (4). However, absorption for the SUZ(b) (4) tablet was described using(b) (4) dissolution, absorption, and metabolism model under the fasted state. It allows simulating an increased solubilization of SUZ in small intestine, where the solubility(b) (4) is higher than in the stomach. Human effective permeability (P_{\text{eff,man}}) (12\times 10^{-4} cm/s) was estimated from Caco-2 permeability by using the SIMCYP built-in calculator and the in vitro permeability data in Madin-Darby canine kidney – knockout (MDCK-KO) cells (P_{\text{app}} = 12.25\times 10^{-6} cm/s) calibrated with propranolol's P_{\text{app}} value of 12.43\times 10^{-6} cm/s (Study FDD-36383). The values of Q_{\text{gut}} (nominal flow through gut model) and unbound fraction in enterocytes (f_{\text{u,gut}}) were optimized based on using PK data from VX19-548-001 (single ascending dose study), VX20-548-002 (multiple ascending dose study), and VX21-548-008 (DDI study with itraconazole).
L1812: The full PBPK distribution model was utilized, and steady-state volume of distribution (V_{\text{ss}}) was predicted via Method 2 in Simcyp software. Furthermore, a tissue-to-plasma partitioning coefficient scalar (K_p scalar) of 0.55 was applied to fit the observed SUZ PK data from the following clinical studies: VX21-548-008 (itraconazole DDI study), VX22-548-013 (rifampin DDI study), VX21-548-011 (relative bioavailability study).
L1813: SUZ intravenous clearance (CL_{\text{iv}}) was set as 5.8 L/h based on the PBPK model-predicted bioavailability value (F) and the observed apparent oral CL values in Study VX21-548-011 and VX22-548-013, assuming minimal first-pass elimination in the gut (Response to the IR received on July 24, 2024). SUZ's f_{\text{m}} (fraction metabolized) values for CYP3A4, CYP2C19, and CYP2C9 were set to 0.85, 0.10, and 0.05, respectively, based on CYP reaction phenotyping studies in recombinant CYP enzymes in human liver microsomes (Report R161). M6-SUZ's f_{\text{m}} values for CYP3A4 and CYP2C9 were set to 0.85 and 0.15, respectively, according to Report R184. These f_{\text{mCYP}} (fraction of hepatic clearance of the substrate mediated by an individual CYP enzyme) values were confirmed by two DDI study results with rifampin (VX22-548-013) or itraconazole (VX21-548-008).
L1814: Additionally, the CYP3A4 induction E_{\text{max}} value of 12.92, half-maximal effective concentration (EC_{50}) value of 1.097 and fraction of unbound drug (f_{\text{u,inc}}) value of 0.63\mu\text{M} were incorporated into the PBPK model for SUZ. These values are the average values of the calibrated fold mRNA E_{\text{max}} and EC_{50} from data of three hepatocyte lots in Report P169.
L1815: An absorption process using the first-order absorption model for the active primary metabolite, M6-SUZ, is included in the PBPK model, although the gastrointestinal absorption process does not occur for a metabolite. A full PBPK distribution model predicted the V_{\text{ss}} via Method 2 in Simcyp software. A K_p scalar of 0.2 was applied to fit the observed M6-SUZ PK data. The CL_{\text{iv}} value of M6-SUZ was set to 2.23 L/h. M6-SUZ's f_{\text{mCYP3A4}} and f_{\text{mCYP2C9}} values were set to be 0.85 and 0.15, respectively, based on results in human liver microsomes (Report R184). These
L1816: fmCYP values were confirmed by two DDI study results with rifampin (VX22-548-013) or itraconazole (VX21-548-008).
L1817: Additionally, the CYP3A4 induction E_{max} value of 9.63, EC_{50} value of 4.115 and f_{u,inc} value of 0.73\mu M were incorporated into the PBPK model for M6-SUZ. These values are the average values of the calibrated fold mRNA E_{max} and EC_{50} from data of three hepatocyte lots in Report P169. The CYP3A induction parameters for SUZ and M6-SUZ were confirmed by matching the observed midazolam PK data in Study VX21-548-010, where 2 mg midazolam was dosed after SUZ PK reached steady state.
L1818: In response to the FDA's IR, the Applicant stated that the fasted-state simulated intestinal fluid (FaSSIF) was used to represent the intrinsic solubility of the compound in the PBPK model.
L1819: A solubility value of 0.036 mg/mL FaSSIF was used in the model (Report T415); for comparison, the solubility of FaSSIF is &lt;0.002 mg/mL. Reviewers noted that the intrinsic solubility refers to the solubility of the compound itself. The bile salt, therefore, should not be integrated. The Applicant stated that the human ADME study (VX21-548-008) results indicated a fraction absorbed more than 0.9 following an 80-mg single dose in capsules. Furthermore, the Applicant estimated the dose-normalized AUC across clinical study cohorts following a single dose of 100 mg and compared them to the dose-normalized AUC value after an 80-mg dose in the human absorption, metabolism, and excretion study (VX21-548-006). The resultant ratio was in the range of 0.97 to 1.2. These results implicitly demonstrated a high fraction absorbed (Fa) following a 100-mg single dose in the formulation.

L1821: ## PK Performance

- Multiple dosing (50 mg q12h) in healthy subjects (VX21-548-007)

- Verification of the contribution of CYP-mediated metabolic pathways using the clinical DDI data with the strong CYP3A inhibitor rifampin (Study VX21-548-013) in healthy subjects
L1825: ## DDI Potential of SUZ as a CYP3A Inducer

L1827: ## Model Application
L1828: - Prediction of the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on SUZ and M6-SUZ's PK: Simulation: SUZ 100-mg single dose (on Day 8), administered with fluconazole (200 mg BID for 28 days) in healthy volunteers, fasted state
- Prediction of the effect of a weak CYP3A inhibitor on SUZ and M6-SUZ's PK: SUZ 100-mg single dose (on Day 4), administered with cimetidine (400 mg BID Days 1 through 24) in healthy volunteers, fasted state
- Prediction of the effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer on SUZ and M6-SUZ's PK: SUZ 100-mg single dose (on Day 28), administered with phenobarbital (100 mg qd Days 1 through 37) in healthy volunteers, fasted state
- Prediction of the CYP3A induction effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK: SUZ 100-mg single dose (on Day 12), administered with efavirenz (600 mg qd Days 1 through 21) in healthy volunteers, fasted state
- Prediction of the CYP3A induction effect of a weak CYP3A inducer on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK: SUZ 100-mg single dose (on Day 9), administered with modafinil (200 mg qd Days 1 through 18) in healthy volunteers, fasted state
- Prediction of the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following the modified dosing regimen in healthy volunteers, fasted state

L1874: ## **Q4. Can PBPK analyses predict the effect of a weak or moderate CYP3A modulator on the PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following a single SUZ dose?**
L1875: Yes, the PBPK model adequately predicted the effect of a weak or moderate CYP3A modulator on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ after a single SUZ dose. The Applicant conducted the DDI simulation for a single 20-mg SUZ dose (not the proposed therapeutic dose) as a CYP3A substrate administered 2 hours after CYP3A inhibitor dosing in a 23- to 52-year-old population (not the intended patient population age range). The Applicant responded to the agency that the SUZ dose, dosing time, and the participants' age range in the itraconazole DDI study (VX21-548-008) were applied to the DDI simulations for SUZ as a CYP3A substrate with weak to moderate CYP3A inhibitors. The Applicant conducted the DDI simulation for a single 100-mg SUZ dose as a CYP3A substrate after CYP3A inducer dosing in 23- to 52-year-old population. Hence, the reviewer performed a DDI simulation for a single 100-mg dose in an 18- to 65-year-old population. The simulation results are summarized in Table 162.
L1876: - After a single 100-mg SUZ dose coadministered with the moderate CYP3A inhibitor fluconazole (200 mg qd Days 1 through 28), SUZ's geometric mean AUC and Cmax ratio was predicted to be 3.0 (90% CI: 2.9, 3.1) and 1.2 (90% CI: 1.2, 1.2), whereas for M6-SUZ, the predicted AUC and Cmax ratio was 2.6 (90% CI: 2.5, 2.6) and 0.74 (90% CI: 0.72, 0.77).
- The geometric mean PK exposure ratio was predicted to be ~1.1 after a single 100-mg SUZ dose in the presence of a weak CYP3A inhibitor, such as cimetidine (400 mg BID).
- SUZ's and M6-SUZ's geometric mean AUC ratios (90% CI) were 0.37 (0.35, 0.39) and 0.40 (0.38, 0.42), and geometric mean Cmax ratio (90% CI) were 0.71 (0.68, 0.73) and 1.32 (1.28, 1.36) following a single 100-mg SUZ dose in the presence of a moderate CYP3A inducer (e.g., efavirenz 600 mg qd). The Applicant recommend avoiding concomitant use of SUZ with moderate CYP3A inducers, as coadministration with a moderate CYP3A inducer (e.g.,
L1877: efavirenz) results in an approximate 40% decrease in SUZ exposure. The reviewer did not assess the effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer following multidosing SUZ.
L1878: 4. SUZ's and M6-SUZ's exposure ratio for AUC and C_{max} were ~0.8-1.0 after a single 100-mg SUZ dose in the presence of a weak CYP3A inducer (e.g., modafinil).

L1883: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; C_{max}, maximum plasma concentration; CYP, cytochrome p450; GM, geometric mean; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetics; PK, pharmacokinetics; SUZ, suzetrigine; qd, once daily
L1884: ## **Q4. Can PBPK analyses predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following the modified dosing regimen?**
L1885: The reviewer conducted DDI simulations to assess the effect of fluconazole on SUZ and M6-SUZ's PK following the modified dosing regimen (i.e., 100 mg on Day 1 + 50 mg every 12 hours for three doses, followed by 50 mg qd on Days 4 to 14, in the presence of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor), and compared the predicted exposure PK parameters with the baseline dosing regimen (i.e., 100 mg on Day 1 + 50 mg BID on Days 2 to 14). The simulation using the modified dosing regimen is needed for the following reasons: 1) the appropriate dose metrics at PK steady state are more relevant than those estimated after a single dose of SUZ in the presence of a moderate CYP modulator, 2) SUZ is a weak-to-moderate CYP3A inducer and would have decreased PK exposure after multiple doses. Therefore, a straightforward dose adjustment using the AUC ratio derived from the single-dose DDI simulation is not suitable. In the virtual DDI trial setting, 200 mg fluconazole qd was set from Day 1 to Day 28, and the modified SUZ dosing regimen was set to start on Day 8. The PBPK-predicted effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following the modified dosing regimen was that the AUC and C_{max} ratio for SUZ or M6-SUZ was \le 1.5 on Day 14 when compared to the baseline dosing regimen (Figure 50, Table 163).

L1896: ## 15. Study/Trial Design
L1897: This section describes in detail the designs of the two phase 3 double-blind, placebo-controlled studies (Studies 104 and 105) that were submitted as evidence of safety and efficacy. In addition, this section briefly describes Study 107, a phase 3, open-label, longer term safety and efficacy study.
L1898: ## 15.1. Study of Bunionectomy Pain, VX22-548-104 (Study 104)
L1899: ## 15.1.1. Study Design, Study 104
L1900: ## Overview and Objective
L1901: The primary objective of Study 104 was to evaluate the efficacy of SUZ in treating acute pain after a bunionectomy.
L1902: ## Study Design
L1903: Study 104 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after a bunionectomy. The study took place at 21 clinical sites in the United States.
L1904: Subjects were randomized 2:2:1 to the SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo treatment groups, respectively. The sample size was 1,073 subjects with 216 subjects in the placebo arm, 431 subjects in the HB/APAP arm, and 426 subjects in the SUZ arm (full analysis set).
L1905: The treatment arms consisted of placebo, SUZ 100 mg for the first dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours, and HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. All subjects received the same number of tablets and capsules every 6 hours in a double-dummy design.
L1906: The surgery was performed under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block) with intravenous sedation. A continuous popliteal sciatic block infusion was started after surgery and remained in place until approximately 3 a.m., but no later than 5 a.m., on post-operative Day 1. Subjects were on a clear liquid diet (\le 8 fluid ounces per hour) starting 2 hours before block removal through 2 hours after the first dose of study drug. After this period, study drug could be taken with or without food.
L1907: The duration of treatment was 48 hours. The safety follow-up visit took place 14\pm 2 days after the last dose of study drug.
L1908: Ibuprofen (400 mg orally every 6 hours as needed) was permitted as a rescue medication starting any time after the first dose of study drug through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. All other medications with analgesic properties, except for those permitted for perioperative pain management, were prohibited for five half-lives or 2 days (whichever was longer) before admission through completion of 48-hour assessments (or after early discontinuation, if applicable).
L1909: In the postoperative period, supplemental analgesic medications were permitted before the time of popliteal block removal. This included acetaminophen (975 to 1,000 mg every 6 hours as needed) until 6 hours before removal of the popliteal block for subjects with NPRS score of 4 through 6, intravenous fentanyl citrate (12.5 to 25 µg every 2 hours as needed) until 1.5 hours before removal of the popliteal block for subjects with NPRS score of 7 or above, and ropivacaine boluses and/or infusion rate changes as needed prior to removal of the popliteal block for subjects with NPRS score of \ge 4. Boluses were not to be used at the time of block removal.
L1910: Administration of study drug began once the subject's eligibility had been confirmed by their request for the first dose of study drug for pain relief and postoperative pain that was reported as moderate or severe on the verbal rating scale (VRS) and \ge 4 on the NPRS within 9 hours after removal of the popliteal sciatic block. The first dose of study drug was required to be administered within 25 minutes after randomization.
L1911: Other key inclusion criteria included the following: 1) male or female between the ages of 18 and 80 years, inclusive; 2) BMI of 18 to 40 kg/m^2, inclusive; 3) had undergone a primary unilateral bunionectomy with distal first metatarsal osteotomy (i.e., Austin procedure) and internal fixation under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block); 4) lucid and able to follow commands; 5) all analgesic guidelines were followed during and after the bunionectomy.
L1912: The key exclusion criteria included the following:
L1913: - Before surgery:Prior history of bunionectomy or other foot surgery on the index foot or bunionectomy on the opposite foot as part of this study.
- History of any illness or any clinical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, might confound the results of the study or pose an additional risk in administering study drug to the subject.
- Cardiac dysrhythmias requiring antiarrhythmic treatment(s) within the last 2 years; history or evidence of abnormal study ECGs that, in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude the subject's participation in the study; or history of QT prolongation or standard 12-lead ECG (performed in triplicate) demonstrating median corrected QT interval, Fridericia's calculation (QTcF) &gt;450 msec at the screening visit or on Day -1 (preprocedure).
- Presence of an automated implantable cardioverter defibrillator, cardiac resynchronization therapy device, or pacemaker.
- History of significant hepatic disease.
- Alanine transaminase or aspartate transaminase values &gt;2.5\times upper limit of normal.
- History of severe renal impairment defined as \text{aneGFR} of &lt;30 \text{ mL/min}/1.73\text{m}^2 calculated using the subject's measured serum creatinine.
- History of any sensory abnormality that, in the opinion of the investigator, may confound the ability of the subject to assess postoperative pain.
- Subjects who have a painful physical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, may confound the assessments of postoperative pain.
L1914: - Any prior surgery within 1 month before the first study drug dose, unless approved by the medical monitor.
- American Society of Anesthesiologists physical status classification of \ge 3.
- Chronic use of opioids (pure agonists, agonist/antagonists, partial agonists, antagonists) or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) with dose escalation within 30 days before admission or unwillingness or inability to stop analgesics at least five half-lives or 2 days (whichever is longer) before admission.
- Subjects who have started new medications that have not been at a stable dose for at least 14 days prior to the scheduled bunionectomy procedure and before dosing with investigational product.
- Subjects with sleep apnea and/or on a home positive airway pressure device.
- History of peptic ulcer disease or gastrointestinal bleeding that, in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude the subject's participation in the study.
- Evidence of misuse, aberrant use, or addiction to alcohol or an illicitly used drug of abuse in the past 3 years, or a positive test for drugs of abuse.A positive drug screen for a known prescribed concomitant medication that is not otherwise exclusionary (e.g., benzodiazepines) will not disqualify subjects.
- After surgery:Subject had a type 3 deformity requiring a base wedge osteotomy, concomitant surgery such as hammertoe repair, or any surgical complications during the bunionectomy.
- Subject had a medical complication during the bunionectomy that, in the opinion of the investigator, should preclude randomization.
- Standard 12-lead ECG (performed in triplicate) demonstrating median QTcF &gt;450 msec at baseline (Day 1 predose).

L1930: ## **Study Endpoints**
L1931: The primary efficacy endpoint was SPID48 compared to placebo.
L1932: The key secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
L1933: - SPID48 compared to HB/APAP
- Time to \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo
L1934: Other secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
L1935: - Time to \ge 1-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects reporting good or excellent on the Patient Global Assessment (PGA) at 48 hours compared to placebo
- Incidence of vomiting or nausea compared to HB/APAP
- SPID24 compared to placebo
- Rescue medication use from 0 to 48 hours compared to placebo:Time to first use
- Proportion of subjects with use
- Total usage
L1936: Other endpoints included the following:
L1937: - SPID36 and SPID12 compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 30\%, \ge 50\%, and \ge 70\% reduction in NPRS at 48 hours compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 24 hours compared to placebo
- Time to first use of rescue medication in the first 12 hours compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 12 hours and 24 to 48 hours compared to placebo
- PK parameter estimates of SUZ and its metabolite, M6-SUZ
L1938: The safety endpoints were the incidence and type of adverse events (AEs), changes from baseline in clinically significant laboratory test results, vital signs, and ECGs.
L1939: ## **Pain Intensity Assessment**
L1940: Pain intensity assessment was obtained by a subject's report on the NPRS. Before removal of the popliteal sciatic block, an unscheduled NPRS was completed before administration of supplemental analgesic medication. After removal of the popliteal sciatic block, NPRS was completed after VRS only if the subject's pain was rated moderate or severe on the VRS. During the treatment period, subjects reported their pain intensity on the NPRS at each scheduled time point through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. In addition, pain intensity was recorded on an unscheduled NPRS immediately before each administration of rescue medication.

L1947: ## 15.2. Study of Abdominoplasty Pain, VX22-548-105 (Study 105)
L1948: ## 15.2.1. Study Design, Study 105
L1949: ## Overview and Objective
L1950: The primary objective of Study 105 was to evaluate the efficacy of SUZ in treating acute pain after an abdominoplasty.
L1951: ## Study Design
L1952: Study 105 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after an abdominoplasty. The study took place at 12 clinical sites in the United States.
L1953: Subjects were randomized 2:2:1 to the SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo treatment groups, respectively. The sample size was 1,118 subjects with 223 subjects in the placebo arm, 448 subjects in the HB/APAP arm, and 447 subjects in the SUZ arm (full analysis set).
L1954: The treatment arms consisted of placebo, SUZ 100 mg for the first dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours, and HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. All subjects received the same number of tablets and capsules every 6 hours in a double-dummy design.
L1955: The surgery was performed under intravenous general anesthesia. Subjects abstained from all food and drink (except \le 8 fluid ounces per hour of water) from the time of surgery completion through 4 hours after the first dose of study drug. After this period, study drug could be taken with or without food.
L1956: The duration of treatment was 48 hours. The safety follow-up visit took place 14\pm 2 days after the last dose of study drug.
L1957: Ibuprofen (400 mg orally every 6 hours as needed) was permitted as a rescue medication starting any time after the first dose of study drug through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. All other medications with analgesic properties, except for those permitted for perioperative pain management, were prohibited for five half-lives or 2 days (whichever was longer) before admission through completion of 48-hour assessments (or after early discontinuation, if applicable).
L1958: Intravenous fentanyl citrate (12.5 to 25 \mu\text{g} as needed) was permitted as postoperative supplemental analgesic medication in the postanesthesia care unit if a subject was not lucid enough for randomization but deemed to be in severe pain per clinical judgement and/or was unable to swallow oral medications. Randomization was not permitted until at least 15 minutes after the last administration of supplemental analgesic medication.
L1959: Administration of study drug began once a subject's eligibility had been confirmed by their request for the first dose of study drug for pain relief and postoperative pain that was reported as moderate or severe on the VRS and \ge 4 on the NPRS within 4 hours after surgery completion. The first dose of study drug was required to be administered within 25 minutes after randomization.
L1960: Other key inclusion criteria included the following: 1) male or female between the ages of 18 and 80 years, inclusive; 2) BMI of 18 to 40 \text{kg}/\text{m}^2, inclusive; 3) had undergone a standard ("full") abdominoplasty procedure; 4) lucid, able to follow commands, and able to swallow oral medications; 5) all analgesic guidelines were followed during and after the abdominoplasty; 6) abdominoplasty procedure duration \le 3 hours.
L1961: The key exclusion criteria included the following:
L1962: - Before surgery:Prior history of abdominoplasty.
- History of intra-abdominal and/or pelvic surgery that resulted in any complications or, in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude participation in the study.
- History of any illness or any clinical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, might confound the results of the study or pose an additional risk in administering study drug to the subject.
L1963: - Cardiac dysrhythmias requiring antiarrhythmic treatment(s) within the last 2 years; history or evidence of abnormal study ECGs that in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor would preclude the subject's participation in the study; or history of QT prolongation or standard 12-lead ECG (performed in triplicate) demonstrating median QTcF &gt;450 msec at the screening visit or on Day -1 (preprocedure).
- Presence of an automated implantable cardioverter defibrillator, cardiac resynchronization therapy device, or pacemaker.
- History of significant hepatic disease.
- Alanine transaminase or aspartate transaminase values &gt;2.5× upper limit of normal.
- History of severe renal impairment defined as an eGFR of &lt;30 mL/min/1.73m2 calculated using the subject's measured serum creatinine.
- History of any sensory abnormality that, in the opinion of the investigator, may confound the ability of the subject to assess postoperative pain.
- Subjects who have a painful physical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, may confound the assessments of postoperative pain.
- Any prior surgery within 1 month before the first study drug dose unless approved by the medical monitor.
- American Society of Anesthesiologists physical status classification of ≥3.
- Chronic use of opioids (pure agonists, agonist/antagonists, partial agonists, antagonists) or NSAIDs with dose escalation within 30 days before admission or unwillingness or inability to stop analgesics at least five half-lives or 2 days (whichever is longer) before admission.
- Subjects who have started new medications that have not been at a stable dose for at least 14 days prior to the scheduled abdominoplasty procedure and before dosing with investigational product.
- Subjects with sleep apnea and/or on a home positive airway pressure device.
- History of peptic ulcer disease or gastrointestinal bleeding that, in the opinion of the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude the subject's participation in the study.
- Evidence of misuse, aberrant use, or addiction to alcohol or an illicitly used drug of abuse in the past 3 years, or a positive test for drugs of abuse.A positive drug screen for a known prescribed concomitant medication that is not otherwise exclusionary (e.g., benzodiazepines) will not disqualify subjects.
- After surgery:Subject had a nonstandard abdominoplasty, collateral procedures during the abdominoplasty, or any surgical complications during the abdominoplasty.
- Subject had a medical complication during the abdominoplasty that, in the opinion of the investigator, should preclude randomization.

L1983: ## **Study Endpoints**
L1984: The primary efficacy endpoint was the SPID48 compared to placebo. The key secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
L1985: - SPID48 compared to HB/APAP
- Time to \ge 2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo
L1986: Other secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
L1987: - Time to \ge 1-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects reporting good or excellent on the PGA at 48 hours compared to placebo
- Incidence of vomiting or nausea compared to HB/APAP
- SPID24 compared to placebo
- Rescue medication use from 0 to 48 hours compared to placebo:Time to first use
- Proportion of subjects with use
- Total usage
L1988: Other endpoints included the following:
L1989: - SPID36 and SPID12 compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 30\%, \ge 50\%, and \ge 70\% reduction in NPRS at 48 hours compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 24 hours compared to placebo
- Time to first use of rescue medication in the first 12 hours compared to placebo
- Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 12 hours and 24 to 48 hours compared to placebo
- PK parameter estimates of SUZ and its metabolite, M6-SUZ
L1990: The safety endpoints were the incidence and type of AEs, changes from baseline in clinically significant laboratory test results, vital signs, and ECGs.
L1991: ## **Pain Intensity Assessment**
L1992: Pain intensity assessment was obtained by a subject's report on the NPRS. In the postoperative period before randomization, NPRS was completed after VRS only if the subject's pain was rated moderate or severe on the VRS. During the treatment period, subjects reported their pain intensity on the NPRS at each scheduled time point through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. In addition, pain intensity was recorded on an unscheduled NPRS immediately before each administration of rescue medication.

L1996: ## 15.3. Longer-Term Phase 3 Study, VX22-548-107
## (Study 107)
L1997: Study 107 was a single-arm study evaluating the safety and effectiveness of SUZ in treating acute pain. The study took place at 13 clinical sites in the United States. The study population consisted of subjects with a variety of surgical and nonsurgical conditions resulting in pain that was moderate or severe on a VRS and \ge 4 on an NPRS. Surgical subjects had to have had pain that was expected to last for at least 72 hours and was expected to require no more than a short-term (i.e., &lt;24 hours) admission, if any. Nonsurgical subjects had to have had pain for a duration of \le 48 hours. The sample size was 256 subjects (full analysis set).
L1998: The treatment consisted of SUZ 100 mg for the first dose then 50 mg every 12 hours. Subjects continued to receive SUZ for up to 14 days or until their pain resolved, whichever occurred first. Acetaminophen 650 mg/ibuprofen 400 mg every 6 hours as needed, up to a maximum of 2,600 mg/1,600 mg in any 24-hour period, was permitted as a rescue medication.
L1999: Pain was considered to be resolved if a subject took no study drug during a 48-hour period and took no more than one dose of acetaminophen/ibuprofen during each 24-hour period within the 48-hour period.
L2000: The safety assessments included adverse event monitoring, clinical laboratory assessments, vital sign measurement, standard 12-lead ECGs, physical examinations, and the Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale. The efficacy assessment was PGA of the study drug.
L2001: ## 15.4. Overall Assessment of the Phase 3 Study
## Designs
L2002: The phase 3 controlled studies (Study 104 and Study 105) were appropriately designed to meet their stated objectives. As described in the draft guidance for industry *Development of Non-Opioid Analgesics for Acute Pain* (February 2022), the randomized, double-blind, repeat-dose design of these superiority studies was acceptable for evaluating the safety and efficacy of a nonopioid analgesic intended for the treatment of acute pain. The primary efficacy endpoint of sum of the pain intensity difference (SPID) as recorded on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours compared to placebo is acceptable. Bunionectomy and abdominoplasty are established models for bony and soft tissue pain, respectively. The inclusion and exclusion criteria were appropriate to select a representative patient population with moderate-to-severe acute pain. Appropriate monitoring for potential safety signals of interest was included in these protocols.
L2003: The design of the phase 3 single-arm study (Study 107) was acceptable for providing supportive data regarding the safety profile of SUZ, especially for longer term use given the treatment period of up to 14 days. However, the lack of a comparator in this study limits the conclusions that can be drawn from these data. The usefulness of the efficacy data generated in this study is limited from a regulatory decision-making standpoint given its single-arm design and the use of PGA, rather than a measure of pain intensity, as the efficacy assessment.
L2004: ## 16. Efficacy
L2005: Prespecified clinical efficacy results from Studies 104 and 105 were presented in Section 6.2.2.4. In the current section, the presented exploratory analyses were not planned to formally test the specified hypotheses given their exploratory aims.
L2006: ## 16.1. Subgroup Analyses for the Primary Endpoint

Additionally, in Study 105, the Applicant did not include results from subgroup analysis by sex in the original submission.

L2028: ## 16.2. Exploratory Result for the Second Key Secondary Endpoint
L2029: Because both studies failed on the first key secondary endpoint, any analysis of the second key secondary endpoint (i.e., SUZ versus placebo on time to a ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline) was considered exploratory.
L2030: In Study 104, the median time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline was 240 minutes (95% CI: 117, 477) for SUZ, 90 minutes (95% CI: 85, 110) for HB/APAP, and 480 minutes (95% CI: 476, 716) for placebo. In Study 105, the median time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline was 119 minutes (95% CI: 90, 180) for SUZ, 475 minutes (95% CI: 236, 480) for HB/APAP, and 480 minutes (95% CI: 477, 705) for placebo.
L2031: ## 17. Clinical Safety
L2032: ## 17.1. Consultative Review, Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies
L2033: The Applicant conducted a thorough QT study (Study VX21-548-009). Based on this study, SUZ is not associated with significant QTc prolongation up to two times exposure of the maximum recommended dose. The interdisciplinary review team for Cardiac Safety Studies Consultative Review dated July 31, 2024, including recommended revisions to labeling, is excerpted below:
L2034: ## 1 SUMMARY
L2035: VX-548 did not prolong the QTcF interval in this thorough QT (TQT) assessment study as described in the ICH E14 guidance – see Table 1 for overall results.
L2036: The clinical study VX21-548-009 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled parallel with nested crossover, multiple-dose ECG study evaluating the effect of VX-548 on the QT/QTc interval in healthy subjects. The highest dose studied was 200 mg QD for 7 days (from Day 4 to Day 10), which covers the maximum anticipated high exposure (C_{max}) scenario when VX-548 is administered to subjects with severe hepatic impairment by 1.2-fold for VX-548 and 0.9-fold for M6-548.
L2037: Data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as primary analysis, which do not suggest that VX-548 is associated with significant QTc prolongation (refer to section 4.5). The findings of the primary analysis are further supported by the lack of QTc prolongation in by-time analysis (refer to section 4.3) and categorical analysis (section 4.4). Assay sensitivity was established based on the concentration-QTc analysis of 400 mg oral moxifloxacin.
L2038: **Table 1: Summary of findings**
L2039: QT assessment pathway	 Thorough QT study
 Substitute for thorough QT study (5.1)
 Alternative QT study when a thorough QT study is not feasible (6.1)
Clinical QT study findings	The high clinical exposure scenario for VX-548 and M6-548 is expected in subjects with severe hepatic impairment. The predicted mean ratios for Cmax for subjects with severe hepatic impairment relative to healthy volunteers based on a PBPK model (Report S313) are 1.7 and 1.6, respectively (section 3.1).
The highest dose of 200 mg QD on Day 6 and Day 10 of oral tablets covers the high clinical exposure scenario by 1.2 for VX-548 and 0.9 for M6-548, respectively.

L2050: ## 12.2 Pharmacodynamics
L2051: ## Cardiac Electrophysiology
L2052: At 2 times exposure of the maximum recommended dose, clinically significant QTc prolongation was not observed.
L2053: (b) (4)
L2054: Reviewer's comment: We propose to use labeling language for this product consistent with the "QTc Information in Human Prescription Drug and Biological Product Labeling Guidance for Industry" draft guidance (link).
L2055: In the aforementioned review, the interdisciplinary review team for Cardiac Safety Studies also commented on cardiac safety events (per ICH harmonized tripartite guideline E14 *The Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and Proarrhythmic Potential for Non-Antiarrhythmic Drugs* (May 2005)) in the controlled phase 3 trials. They found that the incidence of cardiac safety events in the SUZ group was similar to that in the active control or placebo groups. The relevant section from their review is excerpted below:
L2056: ## 3.2.4 Safety Analysis
L2057: In the TQT study 009, there was no death, SAEs, severe AEs, or AE leading to treatment discontinuation. There was no cardiac disorder AEs. The most common AEs (\ge 2 subjects in any treatment group) were nausea, dizziness, headache, dermatitis contact, and diarrhea. The incidence of AEs that were considered related or possibly related to study drug was similar between the VX-548 and placebo/moxifloxacin treatment groups. There were no clinically meaningful findings in laboratory results, ECG parameters, or vital signs assessments. One subject in the 200 mg dose group (1/36, 3%) had a syncope event (Grade 2).
L2058: A total of 874 subjects with pain after bunionectomy or abdominoplasty have received at least 1 dose of VX-548 in a total of 2 completed placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies. In study 104, subjects with pain after bunionectomy were randomized 2:2:1 and received VX-548 100 mg (loading dose)/50 mg q12h (3 maintenance doses) (n = 426), HB/APAP q6h (n = 431), and placebo (n = 216). In study 105, subjects with pain after bunionectomy were randomized 2:2:1 and received VX-548 100 mg/50 mg q12h (3 doses) (n = 448), HB/APAP q6h (n = 448), and placebo (n = 251).
L2059: Out of the cardiac safety events per ICH E14 guidance (e.g., QT prolongation, syncope, seizures, ventricular arrhythmias, ventricular tachycardia, ventricular fibrillation, flutter, torsade de pointes, or sudden deaths), 7 events of syncope and 1 event of QT prolongation were reported in study 104 and 105, in the VX-548 group. The incidence of syncope events in the VX-548 100 mg/50 mg q12h was similar to that in HB/APAP and placebo groups (0.8%, 1.0%, and 1.1% respectively). Most syncope events were likely related to surgery and perioperative volume shifts in the pooled Phase 3 studies. Only 1 subject (0.1%) in the pooled phase 3 studies had a mild AE of ECG QT prolonged that was considered possibly related to VX-548. This subject had a single, isolated QTcF on 1 ECG, which resolved without intervention. Comparatively, 2 subjects (0.2%) in the HB/APAP group had a QTcF interval increase &gt;60 msec.
L2060: In the completed phase 3 trials, the incidence of cardiac safety events (per ICH E14 (May 2005)) in the SUZ group was similar to that of the active control or placebo.

L2089: ## 17.2.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 104
L2090: Analyses of laboratory data did not raise significant clinically meaningful safety concerns. Median changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms for the majority of the laboratory assessments conducted.
L2091: There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 176 and Table 177 demonstrate that a higher percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had creatine phosphokinase (CPK) and eGFR values exceeding certain threshold levels than did subjects in the other treatment arms. Table 178 demonstrates that the mean changes in CPK and creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from baseline was lower in the SUZ treatment arm at 48 hours. However, the magnitude of these changes was relatively small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L2112: ## 17.2.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study 104
L2113: Figure 54 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury (DILI). There were no subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy's Law quadrants. There was a lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (0.2%) in the Temple's Corollary quadrant than in the HB/APAP (1.9%) and placebo (1.9%) treatment arms. These findings do not suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced liver injury.
L2114: Figure 54. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study 104
L2115: Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN criteria within 30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple's corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table "Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot ..." and the listing "Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening..."
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen 5/325 mg every 6 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal
L2116: ## 17.2.1.8. Vital-Sign and ECG Analyses, Study 104
L2117: Review of the vital-sign data and ECG parameters did not reveal significant clinically meaningful safety concerns associated with SUZ. As depicted in Table 179 and Table 180, during the treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and diastolic blood pressures in the SUZ arm than in the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of this difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L2128: ## 17.2.1.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 104
L2129: Table 181 depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different treatment arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroup categories, which limited the
L2130: conclusions that can be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects ≥65 years of age, and subjects of races other than Black, African American, or White exposed to SUZ.

L2135: ## 17.2.2. Safety Results, Study VX22-548-105 (Study 105)
L2136: ## 17.2.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Study 105
L2137: There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences between the treatment arms. The incidence of any AE was lower in the SUZ arm than in both the HB/APAP and placebo arms.

L2146: ## 17.2.2.2. Deaths, Study 105
L2147: There was one death in the placebo group due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism. There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
L2148: ## 17.2.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 105
L2149: There was no substantial difference in the overall incidence of SAEs experienced by subjects in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of SAEs were experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm. There were no substantial differences between the treatment arms for any specific SAE. No SAEs were considered related or possibly related to the study drug by the study investigators. Most of these SAEs were known risks associated with surgical procedures and were not unexpected considering the surgical acute pain model used.

L2158: ## 17.2.2.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Study 105
L2159: There was no substantial difference in the incidence of any AE leading to discontinuation in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of AEs leading to discontinuation were experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm.
L2160: There were no substantial differences between the treatment arms for any specific AE leading to discontinuation. Similar to the SAEs described in Section 17.2.2.3, many of the AEs leading to discontinuation were known to be associated with surgical procedures and were not unexpected considering the surgical acute pain model used.

L2179: ## 17.2.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 105
L2180: Analyses of laboratory data did not raise significant clinically meaningful safety concerns. Median changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms for the majority of the laboratory assessments conducted.
L2181: There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 186 demonstrates that a higher percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had CPK values exceeding certain threshold levels than did subjects in the other treatment arms. Table 187 demonstrates that the mean changes in CPK and creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from baseline was lower in the SUZ treatment arm at 48 hours. However, the magnitude of these changes was relatively small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L2194: ## 17.2.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study 105
L2195: Figure 55 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI. There were no subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy's Law quadrants. There was a lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (1.8%) in the Temple's Corollary quadrant than in the HB/APAP (4.1%) and placebo (3.2%) treatment arms. These findings do not suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced liver injury.
L2196: Figure 55. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study 105
L2197: Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
L2198: Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
L2199: A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN criteria within 30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN criteria are circled in red.
L2200: The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple's corollary cases.
L2201: All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
L2202: In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
L2203: For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table "Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot ..." and the listing "Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening..."
L2204: Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen 5/325 mg every 6 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal
L2205: ## 17.2.2.8. Vital-Sign and ECG Analyses, Study 105
L2206: Review of the vital sign data and ECG parameters did not reveal significant clinically meaningful safety concerns associated with SUZ. As depicted in Table 188 and Table 189, during the treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and diastolic blood pressures in the SUZ arm than in the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of this difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

L2214: ## 17.2.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 105
L2215: **Table 190** depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different treatment arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroup categories, which limited the conclusions that could be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects ≥65 years of age, and subjects of races other than Black, African American, or White exposed to SUZ.

L2220: ## 17.3. Major Safety Findings, Phase 2 and 3 Studies
L2221: Summaries of narratives for the major safety findings in subjects treated with SUZ in the phase 2 and 3 clinical studies are provided below.
L2222: ## Phase 2 Studies
L2223: ## **Study VX21-548-101**
L2224: There were no deaths, no SAEs, and no AEs leading to study drug interruption or discontinuation.
L2225: ## **Study VX21-548-102**
L2226: There were no deaths. One subject in the SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h group experienced an SAE. Two subjects in the SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h group experienced an AE leading to treatment discontinuation. There were no AEs leading to treatment interruption.
L2227: - Subject (b) (6) (SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 53-year-old female status post (s/p) abdominoplasty surgery. The subject was randomized to SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h. On Study Day 1, before the first dose of study drug, the subject had an AE of mild hypoxia. Treatment included oxygen and this AE was considered resolved the next day. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 10, the subject had worsening tachycardia, hypertension, and hypoxia. The subject was subsequently hospitalized for an
L2228: SAE of pulmonary embolism. Treatment included apixaban and oxygen therapy. The subject was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 14. The SAE of pulmonary embolism was considered resolved approximately 10 weeks after the start of this SAE.
L2229: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - chest discomfort): This was a 43-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject was randomized to SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h. On Study Day 2, the subject had an AE of moderate chest discomfort (pressure in esophageal area of upper chest). The subject did not receive treatment for this. There were no ECG or oxygen saturation abnormalities at scheduled time points before and after the start of this AE. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this event. The subject was lost to follow-up and was considered to have discontinued the study on Study Day 3.
- Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation – anemia): This was a 42-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, before the first dose of study drug, the subject had AEs of moderate hypoxia and mild hypothermia, which were considered resolved that same day. The subject was randomized to SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h. Later on Study Day 1, the subject had AEs of moderate anemia and moderate hypotension. The subject also had high output from their surgical drains. The subject was treated with IV fluid. Relevant concomitant medication before or at onset of the AE of anemia included heparin. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this AE. On Study Day 2, the subject experienced decreasing hemoglobin/hematocrit laboratory values and hypotension. The subject returned to the operating room for wound exploration, and a venous bleed was identified and treated. The subject had an AE of moderate hypoxia, which was treated with oxygen and considered resolved the next day. The AE of anemia was considered resolved approximately 2.5 weeks after the start of this AE.
L2230: ## Phase 3 Studies
L2231: ## **Study VX22-548-104**
L2232: There were no AEs leading to death, no SAEs, and no AEs that led to treatment discontinuation or interruption in subjects treated with SUZ.
L2233: ## **Study VX22-548-105**
L2234: There were no AEs leading to death in the SUZ group. Eleven (2.5%) subjects in the SUZ group experienced an SAE. Five (1.1%) subjects in the SUZ group experienced an AE leading to treatment discontinuation. There were no subjects in the SUZ group who experienced an AE leading to treatment interruption.
L2235: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (SAE - hypoxia): This was a 61-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery who was found to have an oxygen saturation (SpO2) of 89% approximately 2 hours after the first dose of study drug. The subject's predose SpO2 was 97%. No symptoms, such as dyspnea and shortness of breath, were reported but their room air SpO2 remained low despite regular incentive spirometry, ambulation, deep breaths, and cough. Treatment included supplemental oxygen. The subject also had moderate AEs of anemia postoperative and coagulopathy and a mild AE of dizziness on Study Day 3. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 5, the SAE of hypoxia was considered resolved with a SpO2 of 95%.
L2236: 2. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE - anemia): This was a 31-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery who was found to have anemia with hemoglobin of 6.4 g/dL on Study Day 3. Screening laboratory results showed a hemoglobin of 12.2 g/dL. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. Approximately 2.5 weeks after the last dose of study drug, the subject was hospitalized due to the SAE of anemia. Laboratory test results included hemoglobin of 6.3 g/dL. The subject was transfused with a total of two units of packed red blood cells and underwent debridement, which showed infection and a hematoma. Five days after this surgery, the subject's hemoglobin improved to 8.9 g/dL and they were discharged from the hospital.

3. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE - impaired healing): This was a 35-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 15, the subject had an SAE of impaired healing. They reported drainage and described superficial necrosis of the skin at the incision edge, which was confirmed as a wound infection. The subject subsequently underwent debridement, wound packing, and wound care. The SAE of impaired healing was considered resolved approximately 20 weeks after the start of this SAE.

4. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 36-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the subject had a moderate AE of syncope, which resolved after treatment with IV fluids. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 9, the subject had an SAE of pulmonary embolism and was discharged home with apixaban for treatment. On Study Day 10, the subject was hospitalized due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism and presented with right leg pain. Additional treatment included IV heparin and normal saline. The subject's breathing improved, the right lower chest pain was controlled, and the subject was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 12.

5. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 36-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject received her last dose of study drug on Study Day 2 (discontinued due to lack of efficacy). On Study Day 4, the subject was hospitalized due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism after a syncopal episode. The subject also had an AE of moderate ECG ST-T segment abnormal. Computed tomography (CT) angiography showed an acute submassive pulmonary embolism. The subject was treated with alteplase. They started bleeding from lower abdominal drains and went into respiratory failure and shock. The subject had six vessels embolized by interventional radiology to control bleeding. The subject was asymptomatic and was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 13. The SAE of pulmonary embolism was considered resolved approximately 23 weeks after the start of this SAE.

6. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE, AE that led to study drug discontinuation - post procedural hematoma): This was a 29-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the subject had AEs of moderate nausea and mild dizziness and was hospitalized for an SAE of postprocedural hematoma. The subject underwent incision and drainage of hematoma. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this SAE. On the same day, the SAE of postprocedural hematoma and AEs of nausea and dizziness were considered resolved.

7. Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (SAE, SAE that led to study drug discontinuation - postprocedural hematoma): This was a 34-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject had increased drainage and slightly increased swelling at surgical site along with tachycardia and
L2237: hemoglobin of 9.4 g/dL. The subject was given a red blood cell transfusion and taken back to the operating room for an abdominal flap exploration, hemostasis, washout, and removal of 700 mL of blood clot. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to the SAE of postprocedural hematoma. On the same day, the SAE of postprocedural hematoma was considered resolved.
L2238: 8. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (SAE, AE that led to study drug discontinuation - syncope): This was a 27-year-old male s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject received only one dose of study drug. On Study Day 1, the subject was found to be hypotensive and sent to the emergency department for evaluation. During evaluation, they had an episode of weakness, lightheadedness, pallor, tachycardia, and diaphoresis with a depressed mental state but without syncope while attempting to stand up. The subject was subsequently admitted for the SAE of syncope and was treated with IV fluids, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen for pain, and ondansetron for nausea. On the same day, the SAE of syncope was considered resolved. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this SAE. The subject was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 2.

9. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (SAE - small intestinal obstruction): This was a 37-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 3, the subject was taken by ambulance to a medical center due to complaints of shortness of breath, nausea, and intractable vomiting. A CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis revealed a small bowel obstruction. A nasogastric tube was then placed for bowel decompression. On Study Day 4, the subject was hospitalized for an SAE of small intestinal obstruction. On Study Day 7, this SAE was considered resolved and the subject was discharged from the hospital.

10. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (SAE - infected seroma): This was a 34-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 15, the subject presented to the emergency department complaining of pain and fever and was subsequently hospitalized for an SAE of infected seroma. On the same day, the subject had an AE of moderate wound dehiscence. Treatment included incision and drainage with wound packing. On Study Day 17, the subject was discharged from the hospital. At the time of reporting, the SAE of infected seroma and the AE of wound dehiscence were considered not resolved.

11. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (SAE - medical device site infection): This was a 20-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 10, the subject presented to the emergency department and was hospitalized for an SAE of medical device site infection (infection of abdominoplasty drainage tubes). On Study Day 12, the subject was discharged from the hospital and their drains were removed two days later. At the time of reporting, the SAE of medical device site infection was considered not resolved.

12. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation – hypotension): This was a 42-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject's relevant medical history included low baseline blood pressure. The subject received only one dose of study drug. On the same day (Study Day 1), they had AEs of severe hypotension and severe bradycardia. Treatment included IV fluids for hypotension and bradycardia. Additional treatment for the event of bradycardia included placement in the Trendelenburg position, oxygen, and IV
L2239: glycopyrronium bromide. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to the event of hypotension. On Study Day 2, the AEs of nausea, hypotension, and bradycardia were considered resolved.
L2240: 13. Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - hypotension): This was a 42-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the subject had AEs of moderate presyncope and moderate hypotension. They also had an AE of mild oxygen saturation decreased. Treatment included IV fluids and oxygen. Study drug dosing was discontinued due to the event of hypotension. On the same day, the AEs of presyncope and oxygen saturation decreased were considered resolved. On Study Day 2, the AE of hypotension was considered resolved.
L2241: ## Study VX22-548-107
L2242: There were no AEs leading to death. Two (0.8%) subjects had SAEs. Five (2.0%) subjects had AEs that led to treatment discontinuation. There were no AEs leading to treatment interruption.
L2243: - Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) (SAE - suicidal ideation): This was a 42-year-old female s/p arthroscopic surgery (left ankle) and ligament operation (left ankle ligament repair and removal of Bassett lesion). On Study Day 8, the subject completed study drug treatment due to pain resolution. On Study Day 12, they had an SAE of suicidal ideation. The subject felt that the suicidal ideation was situational, as they were a single parent of multiple children and had recently ended a relationship. An additional struggle was being nonweight-bearing after surgery. The subject had already discussed their feelings with their primary care physician and scheduled therapy. The subject improved over the subsequent weeks and the SAE of suicidal ideation was considered resolved as of approximately 1 month after the start of this SAE.
- Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) (SAE - cellulitis): This was a 42-year-old female s/p a skin cosmetic procedure (brachioplasty). On Study Day 8, the subject completed study drug treatment due to pain resolution. On Study Day 13, the subject had an AE of moderate cellulitis (right arm). They were treated with antibiotics in an emergency room and discharged home. On Study Day 23, the subject was hospitalized for an SAE of cellulitis (worsening right arm cellulitis). A computerized tomography scan was suggestive of a small abscess within the subcutaneous tissues of the right upper arm. They were treated with antibiotics and discharged from the hospital on Study Day 27. Follow-up information received after database lock indicated that the SAE of cellulitis was considered resolved.
- Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - somnolence): This was a 32-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty and mammoplasty. On Study Day 2, the subject had an AE of mild somnolence and did not receive treatment for this event. Relevant concomitant medication before onset of this AE included fluoxetine hydrochloride. On Study Day 4, the AE of somnolence was considered resolved. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this AE.
L2244: 4. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - accidental overdose): This was a 50-year-old female who experienced neck pain (acute cervical pain due to fall). On Study Day 1, the subject had an AE of mild accidental overdose. They took two tablets of SUZ for 11 succeeding doses from Study Day 1 through Study Day 7, when the overdose was discovered at their clinic visit and study drug dosing was withdrawn. On Study Day 5, the subject had an AE of mild dry mouth and did not receive treatment for this event, which was considered resolved on Study Day 9.

5. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - rash): This was a 56-year-old female who experienced arthralgia (acute left wrist pain). On Study Day 3, the subject had an AE of mild rash (chest) and, on Study Day 4, they had an AE of mild rash (both wrists). Treatment included diphenhydramine hydrochloride for both events. Study drug dosing was withdrawn on Study Day 4 due to these AEs. On Study Day 5, the subject had an AE of mild pruritus and they continued treatment with diphenhydramine hydrochloride. The chest rash, pruritus, and wrist rash were considered resolved on Study Day 7, 8, and 9, respectively.

6. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - arrhythmia): This was a 38-year-old female s/p a ligament operation (left anterior cruciate ligament repair). The subject's relevant medical history included sinus arrhythmia and cardiac murmur. On Study Day 5, they experienced exacerbation of preexisting arrhythmia. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this AE. On Study Day 6, ECG results (performed twice) were normal, with the exception of premature atrial complexes in both readings. At the time of reporting, the adverse event of arrhythmia was considered not resolved.

7. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (AE that led to study drug discontinuation - nausea): This was a 65-year-old female who experienced post-traumatic pain (right hip pain from fall). The subject's relevant medical history included Meniere's disease, Crohn's disease, and nausea/vomiting. On Study Day 2, they had an AE of nausea. The subject stated that they took omeprazole to help with nausea/vomiting due to Meniere's disease. However, this medication was prohibited during the study. The subject was not able to tolerate oral intake without omeprazole and decided to stop taking study drug. On Study Day 3, the AE of nausea was considered resolved.

A summary of the significant changes made to the full prescribing information from the Applicant's proposed label (submitted on January 29, 2024) is shown below in Table 191. Highlights and table of contents were revised for consistency with the rest of the prescribing information (PI). The PI was reviewed to ensure that the PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.

However, the Applicant disagreed with this proposal, citing the favorable safety profile of JOURNAVX, safety data from their chronic pain program, and regulatory precedent. There were also concerns about limiting the use of JOURNAVX, considering the possibility of acute pain lasting more than 14 days and the risks associated with needing to switch to an alternative analgesic, such as opioids. L2260: Full PI Sections1	Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to Applicant's Draft PI
Based on overall data, the review team's recommendation for food effect is, "Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without food." Upon further discussion with the Applicant, the Applicant wanted to add consumption of clear liquids with the first dose because this was how the efficacy was conducted. Thus, the following language was agreed upon: Take the starting dose on an empty stomach at least 1 hour before or 2 hours after food to avoid delay in onset of action. Clear liquids may be consumed during this time (e.g., water, apple juice, vegetable broth, tea, black coffee). Avoid food or drink containing grapefruit during treatment with JOURNAVX.

4 CONTRAINDICATIONS	
There were originally no contraindications listed. The review team added: "Concomitant use of JOURNAVX with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors is contraindicated" due to the \ge 5-fold exposure that occurs with SUZ in this setting. See the Drug Interactions section below for further information.

5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS	
There were originally no warnings and precautions. Four subsections were added which included: 1) Increased Risk of Adverse Reactions with Concomitant Use with Strong or Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors, 2) Risk of Drug Interactions with Certain CYP3A Substrates, 3) Risk of Drug Interactions with Certain Hormonal Contraceptives, and 4) Risk of Adverse Reactions in Patients with Moderate and Severe Hepatic Impairment.
The rationale for adding these subsections is related to predicted drug interactions through which serious or otherwise clinically significant outcomes may occur, and in the case of hepatic impairment, anticipated adverse reactions. These decisions were made in accordance with guidance for industry Warnings and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed Warning Sections of Labeling for Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products – Content and Format (October 2011).
A warning regarding potential renal impairment was considered but not included. Although mean changes in CPK and creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from baseline was lower in the SUZ group compared to placebo, the magnitude of these changes were small and unlikely to be clinically significant. Notably, the changes from baseline in these parameters for the SUZ groups were minimal, while the changes from baseline for the placebo groups indicated an overall improvement, possibly attributable to perioperative hydration. Thus, while there is a potential signal that SUZ could counteract the positive impact of perioperative hydration, it cannot be confirmed by these perioperative studies, and ultimately, the observed changes were unlikely to be clinically significant. No warning regarding renal impairment was added.
L2261: Full PI Sections1	Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to Applicant's Draft PI
6 ADVERSE REACTIONS	
In Section 6.1, the Applicant stated [REDACTED] (b) (4)
The Applicant agreed to describe the imbalances and state that there are insufficient data to detect differences in safety signals between subgroups.
The review team initially recommended expanding the common adverse events table to include those occurring in \ge 1\% (from \ge 2\%) of JOURNAVX treated patients to provide a more robust representation of the safety profile of this first-in-class drug. 7 DRUG INTERACTIONS	
CYP3A Substrates
The Applicant did not have any proposed language for CYP3A substrates. Since SUZ decreases the exposure, it can thereby compromise the efficacy of certain CYP3A substrates. The review team included following labeling recommendations:
Avoid concomitant use of JOURNAVX with CYP3A substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to loss of efficacy unless otherwise recommended in the Prescribing Information for CYP3A substrates.
When using JOURNAVX with sensitive CYP3A substrates, consider increasing the dose of substrate drugs based on their Prescribing Information if needed or use alternative medications.
During labeling negotiations, the Applicant proposed the following language, which was acceptable:
Refer to the prescribing information (b) (4)
instructions (b) (4)
for dosing (b) (4)
Suzetrigine is an inducer of CYP3A. Concomitant use with JOURNAVX may reduce the exposure of sensitive CYP3A substrates which may decrease the efficacy of these substrates. Discontinuation of JOURNAVX may increase the exposure of sensitive CYP3A substrates.

L2273: ## 24. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L2274: The following Pediatric Research Equity Act PMRs will be issued at the time of approval. Moderate to severe acute pain affects pediatric patients and, similar to adults, there is a need for non-opioid analgesics in this population. However, given the risks inherent to a new pharmacologic class of medications, the review team favors a conservative approach to the pediatric studies. The study in the oldest age group (12 to &lt;17 years) will be completed before proceeding with the subsequent age group (6 to &lt;12 years) to confirm that the safety data is favorable before exposing younger cohorts. Some overlap, as specified in the Agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP), will be allowed between the studies in younger cohorts. Careful consideration was given to balancing the safety of the study subjects with the need for expeditious development of SUZ for use in pediatric patients. Further, the review team thoroughly discussed the dates below with the DAAP Safety team, acknowledging that the overlap of dates is intentional. These are effectively interdependent PMRs which will increase the likelihood that milestone dates may be missed and will need to be re-negotiated.
L2275: - Conduct a randomized, controlled study evaluating the pharmacokinetic, safety, and efficacy of JOURNAVX in pediatric patients aged 12 to less than 17 years with moderate to severe acute pain.

L2277: Final protocol submission: 09/2025

L2279: Final report submission: 12/2027
